Triangulation and Persistence: Algebra 101 Thanks: The second author was supported by an individual NSERC Discovery grant.
Paul Biran, Octav Cornea and Jun Zhang

Abstract
. This paper lays the foundations of triangulated persistence categories (TPC), which brings together persistence modules with the theory of triangulated categories. As a result we introduce several measurements and metrics on the set of objects of some triangulated categories. We also provide examples of TPC’s coming from algebra, algebraic topology, microlocal sheaf theory and symplectic topology.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
55N31 53D12 (Primary); 35A27 (Secondary)

1.
Introduction

Persistence theory, invented for the first time in
Zomorodian-Carlsson’s pioneering work
[
48
]
, is an abstract
framework that emerged from investigations in parts of machine
learning as well as algebraic and differential topology formalizing
the structure and properties of a class of phenomena that are most
easily seen in the homology of a filtered chain complex over a field

\mathbf{k}
,

\ldots\subset(C^{\leq\alpha},d)\subset(C^{\leq\beta},d)\subset\ldots\subset(C,d)
(for

\alpha<\beta,\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
). The homology of such a complex
forms a family
\{H(C^{\leq\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}
related by maps

i_{\alpha,\beta}:H(C^{\leq\alpha})\to H(C^{\leq\beta})
,

\alpha\leq\beta
subject to obvious compatibilities and called a
persistence module. Given two filtered complexes
(C,d)
and
(D,d)

that are quasi-isomorphic, it is possible to compare them by the
so-called bottleneck distance. Its definition is based on the fact
that the linear maps
v:C\to D
are themselves filtered by their
“shift”:
v
is of shift
\leq r
if

v(C^{\leq\alpha})\subset D^{\leq\alpha+r}
, for all

\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. Using this, given two chain maps
\phi:C\to D
,

\psi:D\to C
such that
\psi\circ\phi\simeq id_{C}
, there is a
natural measurement for how far the composition
\psi\circ\phi
is
from the identity, namely the infimum of the “shifts” of chain
homotopies
h:C\to C
such that
dh+hd=\psi\circ\phi-id
. The
machinery of persistence modules is much more developed than the few
elements mentioned here. For a survey on this topic and its
applications in various mathematical branches, see research monographs
from Edelsbrunner
[
21
]
, Chazal-de Silva-Glisse-Oudot

[
12
]
, and Polterovich-Rosen-Samvelyan-Zhang

[
35
]
. In particular, there is a beautiful interpretation of
the bottleneck distance in terms of so-called barcodes (
[
3
]
,

[
44
]
), but we can already formulate the question that we
address in this paper.

How can one use a persistence type structure on the morphisms of
some (small) category to compare not only (quasi)-isomorphic objects
but rather define a pseudo-metric on the set of all objects ?

We provide here a solution to this question based on mixing
persistence with triangulation. Triangulated categories are basic
algebraic structures with applications in algebraic geometry,
algebraic and symplectic topology, mathematical physics and other
fields. They were introduced independently by Puppe
[
36
]
and
Verdier
[
45
]
in the early ’60’s. A triangulated category

\mathcal{D}
is a category endowed with a translation
endomorphism
T
and a class of so called “distinguished (or exact)
triangles” - meaning a triple of objects and morphisms

A\to B\to C\to TA
- subject to four axioms. These axioms mimic
abstractly the properties of cone attachments in topology, where the
object
C
is obtained by attaching a cone over a continuous map

A\to B
. For instance, one axiom claims that any morphism can be
included in a distinguished triangle (corresponding to the fact that
one can attach a cone over any continuous map) and another, the
octahedral axiom, can be viewed as a form of associativity for cone
attachments.

Given a triangulated category
\mathcal{D}
there is a simple notion
of
triangular weight

w
on
\mathcal{D}
that we introduce in
§
2
. This associates to each distinguished
triangle
\Delta
a non-negative number
w(\Delta)
satisfying a
couple of properties. The most relevant of them is a weighted variant
of the octahedral axiom (we will give a more precise definition
later). A basic example of a triangular weight is the flat one: it
associates to each distinguished triangle the value
1
. The interest
of triangular weights is that they naturally lead to
fragmentation pseudo-metrics
on
\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{D})
(we
assume here that
\mathcal{D}
is small) defined roughly as follows
(see §
2
for details). Such a pseudo-metric
depends on a family of objects
\mathcal{F}
of
\mathcal{D}
. With

\mathcal{F}
fixed, and up to a certain normalization, the
pseudo-distance
d^{\mathcal{F}}(X,Y)
between
X
,

Y\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{D}))
is (the symmetrization of) the
infimum of the total weight of distinguished triangles needed to
construct iteratively
X
out of
Y
by only attaching cones over
morphisms with domain in
\mathcal{F}
. The weighted octahedral axiom
implies that this
d^{\mathcal{F}}
satisfies the triangle
inequality. Using such pseudo-metrics one can analyze rigidity properties of
various categories by exploring the induced topology on

\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{D})
, in particular, by identifying cases when
relevant fragmentation pseudo-metrics are non-degenerate. One can
also study the dynamical properties of endofunctors
\phi
of
\mathcal{D}
by estimating

d^{\mathcal{F}}(X,\phi^{k}(X))
for
X\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{D})

as well as various associated asymptotic invariants.

Even the fragmentation pseudo-metrics associated to the flat weight are of interest. Many qualitative questions concerned with numerical lower bounds for the complexity of certain
geometric objects - classical examples are the Morse inequalities, the Lusternik-Schnirelmann inequality as well as, in symplectic topology, the inequalities predicted by the Arnold conjectures - can be understood by means of inequalities involving such fragmentation pseudo-metrics. Remarkable results based on
measurements using this flat weight and applied to the study of endofunctors have appeared recently in work of Orlov
[
34
]
as well as Dimitrov-Haiden-Katzarkov-Kontsevich
[
19
]
and Fan-Filip
[
22
]
. On the other hand, a discrete weight, such as the flat one, is not ideal in a variety of geometric contexts because the associated fragmentation pseudo-metrics vanish on too many pairs of objects.

The main aim of the paper is to show how to use persistence machinery to produce “exotic” (non-flat) triangular weights and associated fragmentation pseudo-metrics. The main tool is a type of refinement of triangulated categories, called
Triangulated Persistence Categories
(TPC). A triangulated persistence category,
\mathcal{C}
, has two main properties. First, it is a persistence category, a natural notion we introduce in §
3
. This is a category
\mathcal{C}
whose morphisms
{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)
are persistence modules,
\{\{{\rm Mor}^{r}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)\}_{r\in\mathbb{R}}\},\{i_{r,s}\}_{r\leq s}\}
(see
[
35
]
for a general introduction of the persistence module theory), and that satisfies obvious relations relative to composition. The second main structural property of TPC’s
is that the objects of
\mathcal{C}
together with the “shift”
0
morphisms
{\rm Mor}^{0}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)
have the structure of a triangulated category.
The formal definition of TPC’s is given in §
4
. The mixture of the persistence and triangulation structures gives rise to a rich algebraic framework. In particular, we will show how to endow such a

\mathcal{C}
with a class of weighted triangles.
The main result of the paper, in Theorem
5.1.2
, shows that the properties of these weighted triangles lead to a triangulated structure on the limit category
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
- that has the same objects as
\mathcal{C}

and has as morphisms the
\infty
-limits of the morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
- as well as to a triangular weight
on
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
.

Therefore, if a triangulated category
\mathcal{D}
admits
a TPC refinement - that is a TPC,
\mathcal{C}
, such that
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}=\mathcal{D}
(as triangulated
categories), then
\mathcal{D}
carries an exotic triangular weight induced from the persistence structure of

\mathcal{C}
. As a result, this construction provides a technique to build non-discrete fragmentation pseudo-metrics
on the objects of
\mathcal{D}
. The construction of TPC’s is inspired
by recent constructions in symplectic topology and, in particular, by the shadow pseudo-metrics introduced in

[
8
]
and
[
5
]
in the study of Lagrangian cobordism. Remarkably, in that case, rigidity in the sense of
the non-degeneracy of the fragmentation metrics is implied by symplectic rigidity in the sense of unobstructedness and, conversely, flexibility in the sense of Gromov’s h-principle, implies that the relevant fragmentation pseudo-metrics vanish. However, the general set-up of TPC’s is independent of symplectic
topology considerations and is potentially of use in a variety of other contexts where both persistence and triangulation are available.

This paper only contains the basic algebraic machinery that emerges from
the mixture of triangulation and persistence. Triangulated persistence categories are natural algebraic structures that appear in a wide variety of mathematical fields. In §
6
we discuss four classes of examples: algebraic, topological, sheaf-theoretic and symplectic.

Acknowledgement
. The third author is grateful to Mike Usher for useful discussions. We thank Leonid Polterovich for mentioning to us the work of Fan-Filip
[
22
]
. This work was completed while the third author held a CRM-ISM Postdoctoral Research Fellowship at the
Centre de recherches mathématiques
in Montréal. He thanks this Institute for its warm hospitality.

2.
Triangular weights

In this subsection we introduce triangular weights associated to a
triangulated category
\mathcal{D}
. Using such a triangular weight

w
on
\mathcal{D}
we define a class of, so called fragmentation,
pseudo-metrics
d^{\mathcal{F}}_{w}
on

\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{D})
. All categories used in this paper
(
\mathcal{D}
in particular) are assumed to be small unless otherwise
indicated.

Definition 2.0.1
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a (small)
triangulated category and denote by
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{D}}
its
class of exact triangles. A
triangular weight

w
on

\mathcal{D}
is a function

w:\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{D}}\to[0,\infty)

that satisfies properties (i) and (ii) below:

(i) [Weighted octahedral axiom] Assume that the triangles

\Delta_{1}:A\to B\to C\to TA
and
\Delta_{2}:C\to D\to E\to TC

are both exact. There are exact triangles:

\Delta_{3}:B\to D\to F\to TB
and

\Delta_{4}:TA\to F\to E\to T^{2}A
making the diagram below
commute, except for the right-most bottom square that anti-commutes,

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TB\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TC\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T^{2}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T^{2}A}

and such that

w(\Delta_{3})+w(\Delta_{4})\leq w(\Delta_{1})+w(\Delta_{2})~.~

(1)

(ii) [Normalization] There is some
w_{0}\in[0,\infty)
such that

w(\Delta)\geq w_{0}
for all

\Delta\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{D}}
and

w(\Delta^{\prime})=w_{0}
for all triangles
\Delta^{\prime}
of
the form
0\to X\xrightarrow{\mathds{1}_{X}}X\to 0
,

X\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{D})
, and their rotations. Moreover, in
the diagram at (i) if
B=0
, we may take
\Delta_{3}
to be

\Delta_{3}:0\to D\to D\to 0~.~

(2)

Remark 2.0.2
.

(a) Neglecting the weights
constraints, given the triangles
\Delta_{1}
,
\Delta_{2}
,

\Delta_{3}
as at point (i), the octahedral axiom is easily seen to
imply the existence of
\Delta_{4}
making the diagram commutative,
as in the definition.

(b) The condition at point (ii), above equation (
2
), can be reformulated as a replacement property for exact triangles
in the following sense: if
\Delta_{2}:C\to D\to E\to TC
is exact and
C
is isomorphic to

A^{\prime}\ (=TA)
, then there is an exact triangle
A^{\prime}\to D\to E\to TA^{\prime}
of weight at most
w(\Delta_{2})+w(\Delta_{1})-w_{0}

where
\Delta_{1}
is the exact triangle
T^{-1}A^{\prime}\to 0\to C\to A^{\prime}
.

Given an exact triangle
\Delta:A\to B\xrightarrow{f}C\to TA
in
\mathcal{D}
and any
X\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{D})

there is an associated exact triangle
X\oplus\Delta:A\to X\oplus B\xrightarrow{\mathds{1}_{X}\oplus f}X\oplus C\to TA
and a similar one,
\Delta\oplus X
.
We say that a triangular weight
w
on
\mathcal{D}

is
subadditive
if for any exact triangle
\Delta\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{D}}
and any object
X
of
\mathcal{D}
we have

w(X\oplus\Delta)\leq w(\Delta)

and similarly for
\Delta\oplus X
.

The simplest example of a triangular weight on a triangulated category

\mathcal{D}
is the flat one,
w_{fl}(\Delta)=1
, for all
triangles
\Delta\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{D}}
. This weight
is obviously sub-additive. A weight that is not proportional to the
flat one is called
exotic
.

The interest of triangular weight comes from the next definition that provides a measure for the complexity
of cone-decompositions in
\mathcal{D}
and this leads in turn to the definition of corresponding pseudo-metrics on the set
\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{D})
.

Definition 2.0.3
.

Fix a triangulated category

\mathcal{D}
together with a triangular weight
w
on

\mathcal{D}
. Let
X
be an object of
\mathcal{D}
. An
iterated cone decomposition

D
of
X
with
linearization

\ell(D)=(X_{1},X_{2},...,X_{n})
consists of a family of exact
triangles in
\mathcal{D}
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Delta_{1}:&X_{1}\to 0\to Y_{1}\to TX_{1}\\
\Delta_{2}:&X_{2}\to Y_{1}\to Y_{2}\to TX_{2}\\
\Delta_{3}:&X_{3}\to Y_{2}\to Y_{3}\to TX_{3}\\
&\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\vdots\\
\Delta_{n}:&X_{n}\to Y_{n-1}\to X\to TX_{n}\end{array}\right.

To accommodate the case
n=1
we set
Y_{0}=0
.
The weight of such a cone decomposition is defined by:

w(D)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w(\Delta_{i})-w_{0}~.~

(3)

This weight of cone-decompositions easily leads to a class of
pseudo-metrics on the objects of
\mathcal{D}
, as follows.

Let
\mathcal{F}\subset{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{D})
.
For two objects
X,X^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{D}
, define

\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(X,X^{\prime})=\inf\left\{w(D)\,\Bigg|\,\begin{array}[]{ll}\mbox{$D$ is an
iterated cone decomposition}\\
\mbox{of $X$ with linearization}\ \mbox{$(F_{1},...,T^{-1}X^{\prime},...,F_{k})$}\\
\mbox{ where $F_{i}\in\mathcal{F}$, $k\geq 0$}\end{array}\right\}.

(4)

Note that we allow here
k=0
, i.e. the linearization of
D

is allowed to consist of only one element,
T^{-1}X^{\prime}
, without using
any elements
F_{i}
from the family
\mathcal{F}
. Fragmentation pseudo-metrics are obtained by symmetrizing

\delta^{\mathcal{F}}
, as below. Recall that a topological space is
called an
H-space
if there exists a continuous map

\mu:X\times X\to X
with an identity element
e
such that

\mu(e,x)=\mu(x,e)=x
for any
x\in X
.

Proposition 2.0.4
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a triangulated category and let
w
be a
triangular weight on
\mathcal{D}
. Fix
\mathcal{F}\subset{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})

and define

d^{\mathcal{F}}:{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})\times{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})\to[0,\infty)\cup\{+\infty\}

by:

d^{\mathcal{F}}(X,X^{\prime})=\max\{\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(X,X^{\prime}),\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(X^{\prime},X)\}.

(i)

The map
d^{\mathcal{F}}
is a pseudo-metric
called
the fragmentation pseudo-metric
associated to
w

and
\mathcal{F}
.

(ii)

If
w
is subadditive, then

d^{\mathcal{F}}(A\oplus B,A^{\prime}\oplus B^{\prime})\leq d^{\mathcal{F}}(A,A^{\prime})+d^{\mathcal{F}}(B,B^{\prime})+w_{0}.

(5)

In particular, if
w_{0}=0
, then
\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{D})

with the operation given by
\oplus
and the topology induced by

d^{\mathcal{F}}
is an H-space.

The proof of Proposition
2.0.4
is based on simple
manipulations with exact triangles. We will prove a similar
statement in §
4.3
in a more complicated setting
and we will then briefly discuss in §
4.3.1
how
the arguments given in that case also imply

2.0.4
.

Remark 2.0.5
.

(a) In case
\mathcal{F}
is invariant by translation in the sense that
T\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{F}
and, moreover,
\mathcal{F}
is a family of triangular generators for
\mathcal{C}
, then
the metrics
d^{\mathcal{F}}
are finite. This is not difficult to show by first proving
that
\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(0,X)
is finite for all
X\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
.

(b) It is sometimes useful to view an iterated cone-decomposition as in Definition
2.0.3

as a sequence
\eta
of spaces and maps forming the successive triangles
\Delta_{i}
below

\textstyle{Y_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ldots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y_{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ldots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y_{n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Delta_{1}}
\textstyle{\Delta_{i+1}}
\textstyle{\Delta_{n}}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

(6)

where the dotted arrows represent maps
Y_{i}\to TX_{i}
and, in
2.0.3
, we have
Y_{0}=0
,
Y_{n}=X
.

(c) The definition of fragmentation pseudo-metrics is quite flexible and there are a number of possible variants.
One of them will be useful later. Instead of
\delta^{\mathcal{F}}
as given in (
4
)
we may use:

\underline{\delta}^{\mathcal{F}}(X,X^{\prime})=\inf\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}w(\Delta_{i})\,\Bigg|\,\begin{array}[]{ll}\mbox{$\Delta_{i}$ are successive exact triangles as in (\ref{eq:iterated-tr})}\\
\mbox{with $Y_{0}=X^{\prime}$,
$X=Y_{n}$ and $X_{i}\in\mathcal{F}$, $n\in\mathbb{N}$}\end{array}\right\}~.~

(7)

For this to be coherent we need to assume here
0\in\mathcal{F}
.
Comparing with the definition of
\delta^{\mathcal{F}}
in (
4
),
\underline{\delta}^{\mathcal{F}}
corresponds to only taking into account cone decompositions with linearization
(T^{-1}X^{\prime},F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})

and with the first triangle
\Delta_{1}:T^{-1}X_{1}\to 0\to X_{1}
. There are two advantages of this
expression: the first is that it is trivial to see in this case that
\underline{\delta}^{\mathcal{F}}
satisfies the
triangle inequality, this does not even require the weighted octahedral axiom. The other advantage is that one starts the sequence of triangles from
X^{\prime}
and thus
the negative translate
T^{-1}X^{\prime}
is not needed to define
\underline{\delta}^{\mathcal{F}}
.
There is an associated fragmentation pseudo-metric
\underline{d}^{\mathcal{F}}
obtained by symmetrizing
\underline{\delta}^{\mathcal{F}}
and this satisfies a formula similar to (
5
). Of course, the disadvantage of this fragmentation pseudo-metric is that it is larger than
d^{\mathcal{F}}
and thus more often infinite.

3.
Persistence categories

We introduce in this section the notion of persistence category - a
category whose morphisms are persistence modules and such that
composition respects the persistence structure - and then pursue with
a number of related structures and immediate properties.

3.1.
Basic definitions

View the real axis
\mathbb{R}
as a category with

{\rm Obj}(\mathbb{R})=\{x\,|\,x\in\mathbb{R}\}
and for any

x,y\in{\rm Obj}(\mathbb{R})
, the hom-set

{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}(x,y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}i_{x,y}&\mbox{if}&x\leq y\\
\emptyset&\mbox{if}&x>y\end{array}\right..

By definition, for any
x\leq y\leq z
in
\mathbb{R}
,

i_{y,z}\circ i_{x,y}=i_{x,z}
. We denote this category by

(\mathbb{R},\leq)
. It admits an additive structure. Explicitly, consider
the bifunctor
\oplus:(\mathbb{R},\leq)\times(\mathbb{R},\leq)\to(\mathbb{R},\leq)

defined by
\oplus(r,s):=r+s
, where
0\in\mathbb{R}
is the zero
object and for any two pairs

(r,s),(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})\in{\rm Obj}((\mathbb{R},\leq)\times(\mathbb{R},\leq))
,

{\rm Hom}_{(\mathbb{R},\leq)\times(\mathbb{R},\leq)}((r,s),(r^{\prime},s^{\prime}))=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}(i_{r,r^{\prime}},i_{s,s^{\prime}})&\mbox{if}&r\leq r^{\prime}\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,s\leq s^{\prime}\\
\emptyset&\mbox{if}&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right..

and further

\oplus(i_{r,r^{\prime}},i_{s,s^{\prime}}):=i_{r+s,r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}}\in{\rm Hom}_{(\mathbb{R},\leq)}(r+s,r^{\prime}+s^{\prime})
. Fix a ground field
\mathbf{k}
and denote by

{\rm Vect}_{\mathbf{k}}
the category of
\mathbf{k}
-vector spaces.

Definition 3.1.1
.

A category
\mathcal{C}
is called a
persistence category
if for any
A,B\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
, there
exists a functor
E_{A,B}:(\mathbb{R},\leq)\to{\rm Vect}_{\mathbf{k}}
such that
the following two conditions are satisfied:

(i)

The hom-set in
\mathcal{C}
is

{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)=\{(f,r)\,|\,f\in E_{A,B}(r)\}
. We denote

{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{r}(A,B):=E_{A,B}(r)
, or simply
{\rm Mor}^{r}(A,B)
when the ambient category
\mathcal{C}
is not emphasized.

(ii)

The composition

\circ:{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{r}(A,B)\times{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(B,C)\to{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{r+s}(A,C)
in
\mathcal{C}
is a natural transformation from

E_{A,B}\times E_{B,C}
to
E_{A,C}\circ\oplus
(with
\oplus

the product
(\mathbb{R},\leq)\times(\mathbb{R},\leq)\to(\mathbb{R},\leq)
).

Remark 3.1.2
.

Item (i) means that each hom-set
{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)

is a persistence
\mathbf{k}
-module with persistence structure morphisms

E_{A,B}(i_{r,s})
for any
r\leq s
in
\mathbb{R}
. Here, we use the weakest possible definition of a persistence
\mathbf{k}
-module in the sense that no regularities, such as the finiteness of the dimension of
{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{r}(A,B)
or the semi-continuity when changing the parameter
r
, are required (see subsection 1.1 in
[
35
]
). Item (ii) implies
that the following diagram

\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{r}(A,B)\times{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(B,C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ_{(r,s)}}
\scriptstyle{E_{A,B}(i_{r,r^{\prime}})\times E_{B,C}(i_{s,s^{\prime}})}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{r+s}(A,C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E_{A,C}(i_{r+s,r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}})}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{r^{\prime}}(A,B)\times{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{s^{\prime}}(B,C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ_{(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})}}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}}(A,C)}

(8)

commutes.

We will often denote an element in
{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)
, by a single
symbol
\bar{f}
instead of a pair
(f,r)
. We will use the notation

\left\lceil\bar{f}~\right\rceil=r
to denote the real number
r
and
refer to this number as the
shift
of
\bar{f}
. For each

A\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
the identity

\bar{\mathds{1}}_{A}:=(\mathds{1}_{A},0)\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(A,A)
is of shift

0
. For brevity, we will denote from now on the structural morphisms

E_{A,B}(i_{r,s})
by
i_{r,s}
.

A persistence structure allows us to consider morphism that are identified up to
r
-shift and, similarly, objects that are negligible up to a shift by
r
.

Definition 3.1.3
.

Fix a persistence category
\mathcal{C}
.

(i)

For
f,g\in{\rm Mor}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)
, we say that
f
and
g
are
r
-equivalent
for some
r\geq 0
if

i_{\alpha,\alpha+r}(f-g)=0~.~

We write
f\simeq_{r}g
if
f
and
g
are
r
-equivalent.

(ii)

Two morphisms,
f\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}(A,B)
and
g\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\beta}(A,B)
, are
\infty
-equivalent
, written
f\simeq_{\infty}g
, if there exist
r,r^{\prime}\geq 0
with
\alpha+r=\beta+r^{\prime}
such that

i_{\alpha,\alpha+r}(f)=i_{\beta,\beta+r^{\prime}}(g)
.

(iii)

An object
K\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
is called
r
-acyclic
for some
r\geq 0

if
\mathds{1}_{K}\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(K,K)
has the property that
\mathds{1}_{K}\simeq_{r}0
.

Obviously, if
f\simeq_{r}g
then
f\simeq_{s}g
for all
s\geq r
. Notice also that
\simeq_{r}

is indeed an equivalence relation. Indeed, for
r\not=\infty
this follows immediately from the fact that
i_{\alpha,\beta}:{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}(A,B)\to{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\beta}(A,B)
is a linear map and it is an easy exercise for
r=\infty
.

Definition 3.1.4
.

Given a persistence category
\mathcal{C}
,
there are two categories naturally associated to it as follows:

(i)

the
0
-level of
\mathcal{C}
, denoted
\mathcal{C}_{0}
, which is the category with the same objects as
\mathcal{C}
and, for any
A,B\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
, with
{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{0}}(A,B):={\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(A,B)
.

(ii)

the limit category (or
\infty
-level) of
\mathcal{C}
, denoted

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
, that again has the same objects as
\mathcal{C}
but for any

A,B\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
,

{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}(A,B):=\varinjlim_{\alpha\to\infty}{\rm Mor}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)
, where the direct limit is taken with respect
to the morphisms

i_{\alpha,\beta}:{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}(A,B)\to{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\beta}(A,B)
for any

\alpha\leq\beta
.

Remark 3.1.5
.

(a) In general, a persistence category is not pre-additive as the hom-sets
{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)
are not always abelian groups. However, it is easy to see that both
\mathcal{C}_{0}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
are pre-additive
(the proof is immediate in the first case and a simple exercise in the second).

(b) The limit category
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
can be equivalently defined as a quotient category
\mathcal{C}/\simeq_{\infty}
which is defined by
{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C}/\simeq_{\infty})={\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
and
{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{C}/\simeq_{\infty}}(A,B)={\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)/\simeq_{\infty}
.

Two objects
A,B\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
are said
0
-isomorphic
, we write
A\equiv B
, if they
are isomorphic in the category
\mathcal{C}_{0}
. This is obviously an equivalence relation and it preserves

r
-acyclics in the sense that if
K\simeq_{r}0
and
K\equiv K^{\prime}
, then
K^{\prime}\simeq_{r}0
.

3.2.
Persistence functors

Persistence categories come associated notions of persistence functors
and natural transformations relating them, as described below.

Definition 3.2.1
.

Given two persistence categories

\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, a
persistence functor
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is a
functor which is compatible with the persistence structures.
More explicitly, the action of
\mathcal{F}
on morphisms
restricts to maps

(\mathcal{F}_{A,B})_{r}:{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{r}(A,B)\to{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}^{r}(\mathcal{F}(A),\mathcal{F}(B))
defined for any

A,B\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
and
r\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, for
every
r\leq s
we have the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{r}(A,B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathcal{F}_{A,B})_{r}}
\scriptstyle{i^{\mathcal{C}}_{r,s}}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}^{r}(\mathcal{F}(A),\mathcal{F}(B))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}_{r,s}}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(A,B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathcal{F}_{A,B})_{s}}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}^{s}(\mathcal{F}(A),\mathcal{F}(B))}

(9)

where
i_{r,s}^{\mathcal{C}}
and
i_{r,s}^{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
are
persistence structure maps in
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
,
respectively. In particular, for each

\bar{f}\in{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)
with
\left\lceil\bar{f}~\right\rceil=r
, we
have
\left\lceil\mathcal{F}_{A,B}(\bar{f}~)\right\rceil=r
.

For any functor
E:(\mathbb{R},\leq)\to{\rm Vect}_{\mathbf{k}}
and

\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote by

\Sigma^{\alpha}E:(\mathbb{R},\leq)\to{\rm Vect}_{\mathbf{k}}
the
\alpha
-shift
of
E
defined by
\Sigma^{\alpha}E(r)=E(r+\alpha)
and

\Sigma^{\alpha}E(i_{r,s})=E(i_{r+\alpha,s+\alpha})
for any

i_{r,s}:r\to s
,
r\leq s
.

Definition 3.2.2
.

Given two persistence functors between two
persistence categories
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, a
persistence
natural transformation

\eta:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
is a natural
transformation for which there exists
r\in\mathbb{R}
such that
for any
A\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
, the morphism

\eta_{A}:\mathcal{F}(A)\to\mathcal{G}(A)
belongs to
{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}^{r}(\mathcal{F}(A),\mathcal{G}(A))
. We say
that
\eta
is a natural transformation of shift
r
.

Remark 3.2.3
.

(a) The morphisms

\eta_{A}:\mathcal{F}(A)\to\mathcal{G}(A)
,
A\in\text{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
, give rise to
the following commutative diagrams for all

X\in\text{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
:

\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}^{\alpha}(X,\mathcal{F}(A))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{A}\circ}
\scriptstyle{i_{\alpha,\beta}}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}^{\alpha+r}(X,\mathcal{G}(A))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{\alpha+r,\beta+r}}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}^{\beta}(X,\mathcal{F}(A))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{A}\circ}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}^{\beta+r}(X,\mathcal{G}(A))}

(10)

and

\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{G}(A),X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ\eta_{A}}
\scriptstyle{i_{\alpha,\beta}}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}^{\alpha+r}(\mathcal{F}(A),X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{\alpha+r,\beta+r}}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}^{\beta}(\mathcal{G}(A),X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ\eta_{A}}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}^{\beta+r}(\mathcal{F}(A),X)}

(11)

commute for any
\alpha\leq\beta\in\mathbb{R}
.

(b) Given two persistence categories
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, the persistence
functors themselves form a persistence category denoted by

\mathcal{P}{\rm Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
, where

{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{P}{\rm Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})=\left\{\left(\eta,\left\lceil\eta\right\rceil\right)\,\bigg|\,\begin{array}[]{l}\mbox{$\eta$ is a natural transformation}\\
\mbox{from $\mathcal{F}$ to $\mathcal{G}$ of shift $\left\lceil\eta\right\rceil$}\end{array}\right\}.

When
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, simply denote

\mathcal{P}{\rm Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
by
\mathcal{P}{\rm End}(\mathcal{C})
. It is
easy to verify that
\mathcal{P}{\rm End}(\mathcal{C})
admits a strict
monoidal structure.

3.3.
Shift functors

The role of shift functors, to be introduced below, is to allow
morphisms of arbitrary shift (as well as
r
-equivalences) to be
represented as morphisms of shift
0
with the price of “shifting”
the domain (or the target) - see Remark
3.3.5
. This turns
out to be very helpful in the study of triangulation for persistence
categories.

View the real axis
\mathbb{R}
as a strict monoidal category
(\mathbb{R},+)

induced by the additive group structure of
\mathbb{R}
. In other words,

{\rm Obj}(\mathbb{R})=\{x\,|\,x\in\mathbb{R}\}
and for any

x,y\in{\rm Obj}(\mathbb{R})
,
{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}(x,y)=\{\eta_{x,y}\}
such
that
\eta_{x,x}=\mathds{1}_{x}
and, for any
x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}
,

\eta_{y,z}\circ\eta_{x,y}=\eta_{x,z}
. In particular,

\eta_{x,y}\circ\eta_{y,x}=\eta_{y,y}
and

\eta_{y,x}\circ\eta_{x,y}=\eta_{x,x}
. Both
\eta_{x,x}
and

\eta_{y,y}
are the respective identities and therefore each morphism

\eta_{x,y}
is an isomorphism. The monoidal structure is defined by

\oplus(x,y):=x+y
on objects and for any two morphisms

(\eta_{r,r^{\prime}},\eta_{s,s^{\prime}})
we have

\oplus(\eta_{r,r^{\prime}},\eta_{s,s^{\prime}}):=\eta_{r+s,r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}}
.

Definition 3.3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a persistence
category. A
shift functor
on
\mathcal{C}
is a strict monoidal functor

\Sigma:(\mathbb{R},+)\to\mathcal{P}{\rm End}(\mathcal{C})
such that

\Sigma(\eta_{x,y}):\Sigma(x)\to\Sigma(y)
is a natural
transformation of shift
\left\lceil\Sigma(\eta_{x,y})\right\rceil=y-x
for any

x,y\in\mathbb{R}
and
\eta_{x,y}\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathbb{R}}(x,y)
.

For later use, denote

\Sigma^{r}:=\Sigma(r)\in\mathcal{P}{\rm End}(\mathcal{C})
and, for brevity,
we denote
\Sigma(\eta_{r,s})
by
\eta_{r,s}
for
r,s\in\mathbb{R}
and we
let
(\eta_{r,s})_{A}
be the respective morphism

\Sigma^{r}A\to\Sigma^{s}A
.

Remark 3.3.2
.

Since
\Sigma
is a strict monoidal
functor, it preserves the product structure. Therefore,

\Sigma^{s}\circ\Sigma^{r}=\Sigma^{r+s}
and

\Sigma^{0}=\mathds{1}
. Moreover, since each
\eta_{r,s}

is an isomorphism in
(\mathbb{R},+)
, the corresponding
natural transformation
\eta_{r,s}
is a natural isomorphism. We
also have
\Sigma^{r}(\eta_{s,s^{\prime}})_{A}=(\eta_{s+r,s^{\prime}+r})_{A}
for
each object
A
in
\mathcal{C}
and all
r,s,s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
.

In particular, this implies that for any
Y,A\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})

and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, we have an isomorphism,

{\rm Mor}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{C}}(Y,A)\xrightarrow{(\eta_{0,r})_{A}\circ}{\rm Mor}^{\alpha+r}_{\mathcal{C}}(Y,\Sigma^{r}A).

(12)

Similarly, for any
A,X\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, we
have an isomorphism

{\rm Mor}^{\alpha-r}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Sigma^{r}A,X)\xrightarrow{\circ(\eta_{0,r})_{A}}{\rm Mor}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,X).

(13)

Further, for any
A,B\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
, the isomorphisms
(
12
) and (
13
) imply the existence of an
isomorphism

{\rm Mor}^{\alpha+s-r}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Sigma^{r}A,\Sigma^{s}B)\simeq{\rm Mor}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B).

(14)

In particular, when
r=s
, we get a canonical isomorphism:

\Sigma^{r}:{\rm Mor}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)\to{\rm Mor}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Sigma^{r}A,\Sigma^{r}B).

(15)

Finally, diagrams (
10
) and (
11
) imply that the
following diagrams obtained by setting
\mathcal{F}=\Sigma^{0}
and

\mathcal{G}=\Sigma^{r}

\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}(X,A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\eta_{0,r})_{A}\circ}
\scriptstyle{i_{\alpha,\beta}}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha+r}(X,\Sigma^{r}A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{\alpha+r,\beta+r}}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\beta}(X,A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\eta_{0,r})_{A}\circ}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\beta+r}(X,\Sigma^{r}A)}

(16)

and

\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}(\Sigma^{r}A,X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ(\eta_{0,r})_{A}}
\scriptstyle{i_{\alpha,\beta}}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha+r}(A,X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{\alpha+r,\beta+r}}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\beta}(\Sigma^{r}A,X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ(\eta_{0,r})_{A}}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\beta+r}(A,X)}

(17)

are commutative for any
\alpha\leq\beta
. All the horizontal
morphisms in (
16
) and (
17
) are isomorphisms
but the vertical morphisms (which are the persistence structure
morphisms) are not necessarily so.

Assume that
\mathcal{C}
is a persistence category (with persistence structure
morphisms denoted by
i_{r,s}
) endowed with a shift functor
\Sigma
.
To simplify notation, for
A\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
,
r\geq 0
,
we consider
(\eta_{r,0})_{A}\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{-r}(\Sigma^{r}A,A)
and

(\eta_{0,-r})_{A}\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{-r}(A,\Sigma^{-r}A)
and we will denote
below by
\eta^{A}_{r}
the following maps

\eta^{A}_{r}=i_{-r,0}((\eta_{r,0})_{A})\,\,\,\mbox{or}\,\,\,\eta^{A}_{r}=i_{-r,0}((\eta_{0,-r})_{A}).

(18)

Thus
\eta^{A}_{r}\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(\Sigma^{r}A,A)
or

\eta^{A}_{r}\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(A,\Sigma^{-r}A)
, depending on the context. Note that there is no ambiguity of the notation
\eta_{r}^{A}
due to the canonical identification via
\Sigma^{r}
in (
15
).
The notions discussed before,
r
-acyclicity,
r
-equivalence and so
forth, can be reformulated in terms of compositions with appropriate
shift morphisms
\eta^{A}_{r}
.

The next lemma is a characterization of
r
-equivalence that follows
easily from the diagrams (
16
) and (
17
).

Lemma 3.3.3
.

Suppose that

f\in{\rm Mor}^{\alpha}(A,B)
. Then
i_{\alpha,\alpha+r}(f)=0
for
some
r\geq 0
if and only if
f\circ\eta^{A}_{r}=0
in

{\rm Mor}^{\alpha}(\Sigma^{r}A,B)
and (equivalently) if and only if

\eta^{B}_{r}\circ f=0
in
{\rm Mor}^{\alpha}(A,\Sigma^{-r}B)
.

In particular, we easily see that for two morphisms

f,g\in{\rm Mor}^{\alpha}(A,B)
,
f\simeq_{r}g
if and only if

f\circ\eta^{A}_{r}=g\circ\eta^{A}_{r}
. Moreover,
r
-equivalence
is preserved under shifts. Further, it is immediate to check that

f\in{\rm Mor}^{\alpha}(A,B)
and
g\in{\rm Mor}^{\beta}(A,B)
are

\infty
-equivalent if and only if there exist
r,r^{\prime}\geq 0
with

\alpha+r=\beta+r^{\prime}
such that

f\circ\eta_{r}^{A}=g\circ\eta_{r^{\prime}}^{A}\,\,\,\,\mbox{in}\,\,{\rm Mor}^{\alpha+r}(A,B)

where we identify both

{\rm Mor}^{\alpha}(\Sigma^{r}A,B)
and
{\rm Mor}^{\beta}(\Sigma^{r^{\prime}}A,B)

with
{\rm Mor}^{\alpha+r}(A,B)
through the canonical isomorphisms in
Remark
3.3.2
.

Here is a similar characterization of
r
-acyclicity.

Lemma 3.3.4
.

K\simeq_{r}0
is equivalent to
each of the following:

(i)

\eta_{r}^{K}=0
.

(ii)

i_{\alpha,\alpha+r}:{\rm Mor}^{\alpha}(A,K)\to{\rm Mor}^{\alpha+r}(A,K)
vanishes for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
A
.

(iii)

i_{\alpha,\alpha+r}:{\rm Mor}^{\alpha}(K,A)\to{\rm Mor}^{\alpha+r}(K,A)
vanishes for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
A
.

Proof.

Point (i) is an immediate consequence of the definition of
r
-acylics in Definition
3.1.3
and of Lemma
3.3.3
applied for
A,B=K
,

f=\mathds{1}_{K}
.
We now prove (ii). The proof of (iii) is similar and will be omitted.
It is obvious that (ii) implies
K\simeq_{r}0
by specializing to
A=K
,
\alpha=0

and applying
i_{0,r}
to
\mathds{1}_{K}
.
To prove the converse, we first use diagram (
16
) to deduce that
the map
i_{\alpha,\alpha+r}
factors as below:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 29.35634pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-29.35634pt\raise 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 222.41396pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern (19)

Therefore, since
(\eta_{-r,0})_{K}\circ
is an isomorphism, for any
f\in{\rm Mor}^{\alpha}(A,K)

we have
i_{\alpha,\alpha+r}(f)=0
if and only if
i_{-r,0}(\eta_{0,-r})_{K}\circ f=0
. From point
(i) we know that this relation is true for
f=\mathds{1}_{K}
.
Now, for any
f\in{\rm Mor}^{\alpha}(A,K)
, we write
f=\mathds{1}_{K}\circ f
and conclude

i_{-r,0}(\eta_{0,-r})_{K}\circ f=i_{-r,0}(\eta_{0,-r}\circ\mathds{1}_{K})\circ f=0
. ∎

In particular, we see that
K
is
r
-acyclic if and only if any of
its shifts
\Sigma^{\alpha}K
is so.

Remark 3.3.5
.

(a) Assume that
\mathcal{C}
is a persistence
category endowed with a shift functor
\Sigma
and that

f_{1},f_{2}\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}(A,B)
, then, for all practical
purposes, we may replace
f_{i}
with the
\mathcal{C}_{0}
morphisms

\tilde{f}_{i}\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(\Sigma^{\alpha}A,B)
where

\tilde{f}_{i}=f_{i}\circ\eta_{0,-\alpha}
. The property

f_{1}\simeq_{r}f_{2}
is equivalent to

\tilde{f}_{1}\circ\eta_{r}^{\Sigma^{\alpha}A}\simeq_{0}\tilde{f}_{2}\circ\eta_{r}^{\Sigma^{\alpha}A}

which is a relation in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
.

(b) For a given persistence category
\mathcal{C}
, the existence of a shift
functor
\Sigma
on
\mathcal{C}
sometimes is a non-trivial additional structure. Such
a structure often happens to be available in geometric examples. At
the same time, there is an obvious way to formally complete any
persistence category
\mathcal{C}
to a larger persistence category

\tilde{\mathcal{C}}
that is endowed with a canonical shift functor. This is
achieved by formally adding objects
\Sigma^{r}X
for each
r\in\mathbb{R}

and
X\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
and defining morphisms such that the
relations in Remark
3.3.2
are satisfied.

3.4.
An example of a persistence category

We give an example of a persistence category that is constructed from persistence
\mathbf{k}
-modules. To some extent, this is the motivation of the definition of a persistence category. Recall that for a persistence
\mathbf{k}
-module
\mathbb{V}
, the notation
\mathbb{V}[r]
denotes another persistence
\mathbf{k}
-module which comes from an
r
-shift from
\mathbb{V}
in the sense that

\mathbb{V}[r]_{t}=\mathbb{V}_{r+t}\,\,\,\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,\,\,\,\iota^{\mathbb{V}[r]}_{s,t}=\iota^{\mathbb{V}}_{s+r,t+r}.

A persistence morphism
f:\mathbb{V}\to\mathbb{W}
is an
\mathbb{R}
-family of morphisms
f=\{f_{t}\}
such that it commutes with the persistence structure maps of
\mathbb{V}
and
\mathbb{W}
, i.e.,
f_{t}\circ\iota^{\mathbb{V}}_{s,t}=\iota^{\mathbb{W}}_{s,t}\circ f_{s}
. Similarly, one can define
r
-shifted persistence morphism
f[r]
where
(f[r])_{t}=f_{r+t}
.

Remark 3.4.1
.

There are cases of
\mathbb{V}
and
\mathbb{W}
such that the only
persistence
morphism from
\mathbb{V}
to
\mathbb{W}
is the zero morphism. For instance,
\mathbb{V}={\mathds{1}}_{[0,\infty)}
and
\mathbb{W}={\mathds{1}}_{[1,\infty)}
. Note that
\mathbb{W}=\mathbb{V}[-1]
. On the other hand, there always exists a well-defined persistence morphism from
\mathbb{V}[r]
to
\mathbb{V}[s]
for
r\leq s
, which is just
\iota^{\mathbb{V}}_{\cdot+r,\cdot+s}
.

Let
\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}
be the category of persistence
\mathbf{k}
-modules, then we claim that
\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}
can be enriched to be a persistence category
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}
. Indeed, let
{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}})={\rm Obj}(\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}})
, and for objects
\mathbb{V},\mathbb{W}
in
\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}
, define

{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}}(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{W}):=\left\{\{{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{W}[r])\}_{r\in\mathbb{R}};\{i_{r,s}\}_{r\leq s\in\mathbb{R}}\right\}.

(20)

Here,
{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}}^{r}(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{W})={\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{W}[r])
, and
{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}(\cdot,\cdot)
consists of persistence morphisms. For any
r\leq s
, the well-defined persistence morphism
\iota^{\mathbb{W}}_{\cdot+r,\cdot+s}:\mathbb{W}[r]\to\mathbb{W}[s]
induces structure maps
i_{r,s}:=\iota^{\mathbb{W}}_{\cdot+r,\cdot+s}\circ
in (
20
). Moreover, the composition
\circ_{(r,s)}:{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}}^{r}(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{V})\times{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}}^{s}(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{W})\to{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}}^{r+s}(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{W})
is defined by

(f,g)\mapsto g[r]\circ f

where we use the identification
{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{W})[r]={\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathbb{V}[r],\mathbb{W}[r])
for any
r\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, for the following diagram where
r\leq r^{\prime}
and
s\leq s^{\prime}
,

\textstyle{{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{V}[r])\times{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{W}[s])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ_{(r,s)}}
\scriptstyle{i_{r,r^{\prime}}\times i_{s,s^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{W}[r+s])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{r+s,r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{V}[r^{\prime}])\times{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{W}[s^{\prime}])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ_{(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})}}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{W}[r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}])}

(21)

we have

\displaystyle(\circ_{(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})}\circ(i_{r,r^{\prime}}\times i_{s,s^{\prime}}))(f,g)

\displaystyle=\circ_{(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})}(i_{r,r^{\prime}}(f),i_{s,s^{\prime}}(g))

\displaystyle=i_{s,s^{\prime}}(g)[r^{\prime}]\circ i_{r,r^{\prime}}(f)

\displaystyle=(\iota^{\mathbb{W}}_{\cdot+s,\cdot+s^{\prime}}\circ g)[r^{\prime}]\circ(\iota^{\mathbb{W}}_{\cdot+r,\cdot+r^{\prime}}\circ f)

\displaystyle=\iota^{\mathbb{W}}_{\cdot+r^{\prime}+s,\cdot+r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}}\circ g[r^{\prime}]\circ\iota^{\mathbb{V}}_{\cdot+r,\cdot+r^{\prime}}\circ f

\displaystyle=\iota^{\mathbb{W}}_{\cdot+r^{\prime}+s,\cdot+r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}}\circ\iota^{\mathbb{W}}_{\cdot+r+s,\cdot+r^{\prime}+s}\circ g[r]\circ f

\displaystyle=\iota^{\mathbb{W}}_{\cdot+r+s,\cdot+r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}}\circ(g[r]\circ f)=(\iota_{r+s,r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}}\circ\circ_{(s,t)})(f,g)

where the fifth equality is due to the fact that
g
is a persistence morphism (so, in particular, it commutes with the persistence structure maps). Therefore, the diagram (
21
) is commutative and
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}
is a persistence category by Definition
3.1.1
.

Since
{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}}^{0}(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{W})={\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{W})
, we know
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}_{0}=\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}
. An example of a persistence endofunctor on
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}
, denoted by
\Sigma^{\alpha}:\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}\to\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}
, is defined by

\Sigma^{\alpha}(\mathbb{V}):=\mathbb{V}[-{\alpha}]\,\,\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,\,\,\Sigma^{\alpha}(f)=f[-\alpha]

(22)

for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. By Definition
3.2.1
, we need to confirm the commutativity of the following diagram where
r\leq s
in
\mathbb{R}
,

\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}}^{r}(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{W})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{i_{r,s}}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}}^{r}(\Sigma^{\alpha}\mathbb{V},\Sigma^{\alpha}\mathbb{W})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{r,s}}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}}^{s}(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{W})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{\alpha}}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}}^{s}(\Sigma^{\alpha}\mathbb{V},\Sigma^{\alpha}\mathbb{W})}

Indeed, for any
f\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}}^{r}(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{W})(={\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{W}[r])
), we have

\displaystyle(\Sigma^{\alpha}\circ i_{r,s})(f)

\displaystyle=\Sigma^{\alpha}(\iota^{\mathbb{W}}_{\cdot+r,\cdot+s}\circ f)

\displaystyle=(\iota^{\mathbb{W}}_{\cdot+r,\cdot+s}\circ f)[-\alpha]

\displaystyle=(\iota^{\mathbb{W}}_{\cdot+r-\alpha,\cdot+s-\alpha})\circ f[-\alpha]=(i_{r,s}\circ\Sigma^{\alpha})(f)\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}}^{s}(\Sigma^{\alpha}\mathbb{V},\Sigma^{\alpha}\mathbb{W}).

Therefore,
\Sigma^{\alpha}
is a persistence endofunctor on
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}
for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
.

Consider a canonical shift functor on
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}
, denoted by
\Sigma:(\mathbb{R},+)\to\mathcal{P}{\rm Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}})
, defined by

\Sigma(\alpha):=\Sigma^{\alpha}\,\,\mbox{defined in (\ref{ex-per-fun})}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\eta_{\alpha,\beta}=\mathds{1}_{\,\cdot[-\alpha]}

for any
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
. Indeed, evaluate
\eta_{\alpha,\beta}
on any object
\mathbb{V}
,

\displaystyle(\eta_{\alpha,\beta})_{\mathbb{V}}=\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{V}[-\alpha]}

\displaystyle\in{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathbb{V}[-\alpha],\mathbb{V}[-\alpha])

\displaystyle={\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathbb{V}[-\alpha],\mathbb{V}[-\beta+\beta-\alpha])

\displaystyle={\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}}^{\beta-\alpha}(\mathbb{V}[-\alpha],\mathbb{V}[-\beta])

\displaystyle={\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}}^{\beta-\alpha}(\Sigma^{\alpha}\mathbb{V},\Sigma^{\beta}\mathbb{V}).

In other words,
\eta_{\alpha,\beta}
is a persistence natural transformation of shift
\beta-\alpha
by Definition
3.2.2
. Therefore,
\Sigma
defines a shift functor on
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}
.

For
r\geq 0
, recall that the notation
\eta_{r}^{\mathbb{V}}
in (
18
) denotes the composition
i_{-r,0}\circ(\eta_{r,0})_{\mathbb{V}}
. In particular, by definition above,
\eta_{r}^{\mathbb{V}}\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}}^{0}(\Sigma^{r}\mathbb{V},\mathbb{V})={\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathbb{V}[-r],\mathbb{V})
, and it equals the following composition

\mathbb{V}[-r]\xrightarrow{\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{V}[-r]}}\mathbb{V}[-r]\xrightarrow{\iota^{\mathbb{V}}_{\cdot+r,\cdot}\circ}\mathbb{V}

which is just
\iota^{\mathbb{V}}_{\cdot-r,\cdot}
, the persistence structure maps of
\mathbb{V}
. Assume that objects in
\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}
admit sufficient regularities so that they can be equivalently described via barcodes (see
[
18
]
), then, by (i) in Lemma
3.3.4
,
r
-acyclic objects in
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}
are precisely those persistence
\mathbf{k}
-modules with only finite-length bars in their barcodes, and its boundary depth, the length of the longest finite-length bar (see
[
42
,
43
]
and
[
44
]
), is no greater than
r
.

Remark 3.4.2
.

The way that we enrich the category
\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}
to
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}
as above, in order to admit more structures, is also investigated in the recent work
[
10
]
, Section 10. In particular, the morphism set defined in (
20
) coincides with the enriched morphism set in its Proposition 10.2, and
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{P}{\rm Mod}_{\mathbf{k}}}
is similar to
\underline{\rm Mod}_{R}^{P}
in its Proposition 10.3 (when taking
R=\mathbf{k}
and
P=\mathbb{R}
).

4.
Triangulated persistence categories

This section contains the main algebraic notion introduced in this
paper. Its purpose is to reflect triangulation properties in the
context of persistence categories. The basic properties of this
structure lead to the definition of a class of weighted triangles and
induced fragmentation pseudo-metrics on the set of objects of such a
category.

4.1.
Main definition

We will use consistently below the characterization of
r
-equivalence
in Lemma
3.3.3
as well as that of
r
-acyclics in Lemma

3.3.4
.

Definition 4.1.1
.

A
triangulated persistence category

is a persistence category
\mathcal{C}
endowed with a shift functor
\Sigma

such that the following three conditions are satisfied:

(i)

The
0
-level category
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is triangulated with a
translation automorphism denoted by
T
. Note that in
particular
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is additive and we further assume that the
restriction of the persistence structure of
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is
compatible with the additive structure on
\mathcal{C}_{0}
in the obvious
way. Specifically this means that

{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{r}(A\oplus B,C)={\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{r}(A,C)\oplus{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{r}(B,C)
for all

r\leq 0
and the persistence maps
i_{r,s}
,
r\leq s\leq 0

are compatible with this splitting. The same holds also for

{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{r}(A,B\oplus C)
.

(ii)

The restriction of
\Sigma^{r}
to

\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{C}_{0})
is a triangulated endofunctor of
\mathcal{C}_{0}

for each
r\in\mathbb{R}
. Note that each of the functors

\Sigma^{r}
, being a triangulated functor, is also assumed to be
additive. We further assume that all the natural transformations

\eta_{r,s}:\Sigma^{r}\to\Sigma^{s}
,
s,r\in\mathbb{R}
, are
compatible with the additive structure on
\mathcal{C}_{0}
.

(iii)

For any
r\geq 0
and any
A\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
, the
morphism
\eta^{A}_{r}:\Sigma^{r}A\to A
defined in (
18
) embeds into an exact triangle
of
\mathcal{C}_{0}

\Sigma^{r}A\xrightarrow{\eta^{A}_{r}}A\to K\to T\Sigma^{r}A

such
that
K
is
r
-acyclic.

Remark 4.1.2
.

(a) Given that
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is
triangulated, the functors
{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(X,-)
and
{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}(-,X)
are
exact for
s=0
. This property together with the relations in
Remark
3.3.2
imply that these functors are exact
for
all

s\in\mathbb{R}
.

(b) Condition (ii) requires in particular that
\Sigma
and

T
commute. Thus,
T\Sigma^{r}X=\Sigma^{r}TX
for each object
X

and for any
f\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(A,B)
we have

\Sigma^{r}Tf=T\Sigma^{r}f
. Additionally, each
\Sigma^{r}

preserves the additive structure of
\mathcal{C}_{0}
and it takes each exact
triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
to an exact triangle. Moreover, the
assumptions above imply that we have canonical isomorphisms

{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{r}(A\oplus B,C)\cong{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{r}(A,C)\oplus{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{r}(B,C),\ \;\;\forall r\in\mathbb{R},

and the persistence maps

i_{r,s}
are compatible with these isomorphisms. The same holds
also for
{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{r}(A,B\oplus C)
. Finally, the maps
\eta_{r}^{A}
,

A\in\text{Obj}(\mathcal{C}_{0})
,
r\geq 0
, are compatible with the
additive structure on
\mathcal{C}_{0}
.

Notice also that the functor
T
extends from
\mathcal{C}_{0}
to a functor
on
\mathcal{C}
. Indeed
T
is already defined on all the objects of
\mathcal{C}
as
well as on all the morphisms of shift
0
. For
f\in{\rm Mor}^{r}(A,B)

we define
Tf=T(f\circ(\eta_{r,0})_{A})\circ(\eta_{0,r})_{TA}
. It is easily seen that with this definition
T

is indeed a functor and it immediately follows that

T((\eta_{r,s})_{A})=(\eta_{r,s})_{TA}
for all objects
A
in
\mathcal{C}

and
r,s\in\mathbb{R}
. Further, by using the identifications in Remark

3.3.2
, it also follows that
T
is a persistence functor.
In particular, we have
\eta_{r}^{TA}=T\eta_{r}^{A}
for each object

A
in
\mathcal{C}
.

(c) Given that
0
-isomorphism preserves
r
-acyclicity - as noted
in §
3.1
, property (iii) does not depend on
the specific extension of
\eta_{r}^{A}
to an exact triangle.

(d) Given a triangulated category
\mathcal{D}
together with an
appropriate shift functor

\Sigma:(\mathbb{R},+)\to\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{D})
it is possible to
define a triangulated persistence category
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}

with the same objects as
\mathcal{D}
, that has
\mathcal{D}
as

0
-level and with morphism endowed with a persistence structure in
the unique way such that (
16
) and (
17
) are
satisfied with respect to the given shift functor
\Sigma
. We do
not give the details here but we will see an example
in §
6.4
.

Definition 4.1.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a triangulated persistence category. A map
f\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(A,B)
is said to be an
r-isomorphism (from
A
to
B
) if it embeds into an exact triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{0}

A\xrightarrow{f}B\to K\to TA

such that
K\simeq_{r}0
.

We write
f:A\simeq_{r}B
.

Remark 4.1.4
.

(a) If
f
is an
r
-isomorphism, then
f
is an
s
-isomorphism for any
s\geq r
.
It is not difficult to check, and we will see this explicitly in Remark
4.1.7
, that for
r=0
this definition is equivalent to the notion of
0
-isomorphism introduced before (namely isomorphism in the category
\mathcal{C}_{0}
).

(b) The relation
T(\eta_{r}^{K})=\eta_{r}^{TK}
implies that
TK
is
r
-acyclic if and only if
K
is
r
-acyclic and, therefore,
f
is an
r
-isomorphism if and only if
Tf
is one.

(c) From Definition
4.1.1
(iii) we see that for any
r\geq 0
and
A\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
we have

\eta^{A}_{r}:\Sigma^{r}A\simeq_{r}A~.~

Proposition 4.1.5
.

Any triangulated persistence category

\mathcal{C}
has the following properties.

(i)

If
f:A\to B
is an
r
-isomorphism, then there exist
\phi\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(B,\Sigma^{-r}A)
and
\psi\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(\Sigma^{r}B,A)
such that

\phi\circ f=\eta^{A}_{r}\,\,\mbox{in ${\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(A,\Sigma^{-r}A)$}\,\,\,\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,\,\,\,f\circ\psi=\eta^{B}_{r}\,\,\mbox{in ${\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(\Sigma^{r}B,B)$}.

The map
\psi
is called a right
r
-inverse of
f
and
\phi
is a left
r
-inverse of
f
.
They satisfy
\Sigma^{r}\phi\simeq_{r}\psi
.

(ii)

If
f
is an
r
-isomorphism, then any two left
r
-inverses
\phi,\phi^{\prime}
of
f
are themselves

r
-equivalent and the same conclusion holds for right
r
-inverses.

(iii)

If
f:A\simeq_{r}B
and
g:B\simeq_{s}C
, then
g\circ f:A\simeq_{r+s}C
.

Proof.

(i) We first construct
\phi
. In
\mathcal{C}_{0}
, the morphism

f:A\to B
embeds into an exact triangle

A\xrightarrow{f}B\xrightarrow{g}K\xrightarrow{h}TA
with

K\simeq_{r}0
. Using the fact that
\Sigma
and
T
commute, the
following diagram is easily seen to be commutative:

\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{r}^{K}}
\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{TA}_{r}}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-r}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{-r}h}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-r}TA}

(23)

Thus
\eta_{r}^{TA}\circ h=\Sigma^{-r}h\circ\eta_{r}^{K}=0
since

K
is
r
-acyclic (and so
\eta_{r}^{K}=0
). By rotating exact
triangles in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
we obtain a new
\mathcal{C}_{0}
-exact triangle

K\xrightarrow{h}TA\xrightarrow{Tf}TB\xrightarrow{Tg}TK
and
consider the diagram below (in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
):

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 7.60416pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-7.60416pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 25.2306pt\raise 5.43056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{h}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 50.77663pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 50.77663pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 114.68176pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Sigma^{-r}TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 191.47986pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 191.47986pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces~.~

The first square on the left commutes, so we
deduce the existence of a map

\tilde{\phi}\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(TB,\Sigma^{-r}TA)

that makes commutative the middle and right squares. The desired
left inverse of
f
is
\phi=T^{-1}\tilde{\phi}
. A similar argument
leads to the existence of
\psi
. We postpone the identity

\Sigma^{r}\phi\simeq_{r}\psi
after the proof of (ii).

(ii) If
\phi

\phi^{\prime}
are two left inverses of
f
then

(\phi-\phi^{\prime})\circ f=0
. Therefore

(\phi-\phi^{\prime})\circ\eta^{B}_{r}=(\phi-\phi^{\prime})\circ(f\circ\psi)=((\phi-\phi^{\prime})\circ f)\circ\psi=0.

Lemma
3.3.3
implies that
\phi\simeq_{r}\phi^{\prime}
. The same
argument works for right inverses. We now return to the identity

\Sigma^{r}\phi\simeq_{r}\psi
(with the notation at point (i)).
We have the following commutative diagram:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 12.86864pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-12.86864pt\raise 37.2228pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\psi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 72.86864pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 71.42975pt\raise-11.07677pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 24.85907pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5367pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta^{A}_{r}}$}}}\kern
