0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Sigma^{-r}A}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces~.~

Therefore,
\eta^{A}_{r}\circ\psi=\phi\circ{\eta^{B}_{r}}
. By the naturality properties of
\eta
we also have

\phi\circ{\eta^{B}_{r}}=\eta^{A}_{r}\circ\Sigma^{r}\phi
. Thus, by Lemma

3.3.3
,
\Sigma^{r}\phi\simeq_{r}\psi
.

(iii) We will make use of the following lemma.

Lemma 4.1.6
.

If
K\to K^{\prime\prime}\to K^{\prime}\to TK
is an
exact triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
,
K\simeq_{r}0
and
K^{\prime}\simeq_{s}0
, then

K^{\prime\prime}\simeq_{r+s}0
.

Proof of Lemma
4.1.6
.

We associate the following commutative diagram to the exact
triangle in the statement:

\textstyle{{\rm Mor}^{\alpha}(K^{\prime\prime},K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}^{\alpha}(K^{\prime\prime},K^{\prime\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}^{\alpha}(K^{\prime\prime},K^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}^{\alpha+s}(K^{\prime\prime},K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}^{\alpha+s}(K^{\prime\prime},K^{\prime\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}^{\alpha+s}(K^{\prime\prime},K^{\prime})}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}^{\alpha+r+s}(K^{\prime\prime},K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{n}
\textstyle{{\rm Mor}^{\alpha+r+s}(K^{\prime\prime},K^{\prime\prime})}

Here, the vertical
morphisms are the persistence structure maps. The
rightmost vertical map and the lower leftmost vertical one are
both
0

due to our hypothesis together with Lemma
3.3.4
. The
functor
{\rm Mor}^{\alpha}(K^{\prime\prime},-)
is exact which implies that

t\circ v=0
and, again by Lemma
3.3.4
, we deduce

K^{\prime\prime}\simeq_{r+s}0
.
∎

Returning to the proof of the proposition, point (iii) now follows
immediately by using the octahedral axiom to construct the following
commutative diagram in
\mathcal{C}_{0}

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g\circ f}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}}

with exact rows and columns and
applying Lemma
4.1.6
to the rightmost column.
∎

Remark 4.1.7
.

(a) Points (i) and (ii)
in Proposition
4.1.5
imply that the notion

0
-isomorphism
f:A\to B
, as given by Definition
4.1.3

for
r=0
, is equivalent to isomorphism in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
. In
particular, for
r=0
,
f
admits a unique inverse (up to
0
-isomorphism) in
{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(B,A)
.

(b) Point (iii) in Proposition
4.1.5
shows that

r
-isomorphism can not be expected to be an equivalence relation
(unless
r=0
).

Here are several useful corollaries. The first is an immediate consequence of Lemma
4.1.6
and the octahedral axiom in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
.

Corollary 4.1.8
.

If in the following commutative diagram in

\mathcal{C}_{0}
,

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{w}
\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Tu}
\textstyle{A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TA^{\prime}}

the two rows are
exact triangles,
u
is an
r
-isomorphism and
v
is an

s
-isomorphism, then
w
is an
(r+s)
-isomorphism.

Corollary 4.1.9
.

If
f:A\to B
is an
r
-isomorphism, then
its right inverse
\psi\in{\rm Mor}^{0}(\Sigma^{r}B,A)
(given by (i) in
Proposition
4.1.5
) is a
2r
-isomorphism. The same
conclusion holds for its left inverse.

Proof.

By the octahedral axiom (in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
), we have the
following commutative diagram,

\textstyle{\Sigma^{r}B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{r}B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{B}_{r}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K}

where
K^{\prime\prime}\to K^{\prime}\to K\to TK^{\prime\prime}

is exact. By (iii) in Definition
4.1.1
,
K^{\prime}\simeq_{r}0
. Therefore, by Lemma
4.1.6
,
K^{\prime\prime}\simeq_{2r}0
and
thus
\psi
is
2r
-isomorphism. A similar argument applies to the
left inverse of
f
.
∎

The next consequence is immediate but useful so we state it apart.

Corollary 4.1.10
.

If
f:A\to B
is an
r
-isomorphism, then
for any
u,u^{\prime}\in{\rm Mor}^{0}(B,C)
with
u\circ f=u^{\prime}\circ f
, we
have
u\simeq_{r}u^{\prime}
, i.e.,
u
and
u^{\prime}
are
r
-equivalent.
Similarly, if
v,v^{\prime}\in{\rm Mor}^{0}(D,A)
and
f\circ v=f\circ v^{\prime}
, then

v\simeq_{r}v^{\prime}
.

Corollary 4.1.11
.

Assume that the following diagram in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
,

\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathds{1}_{K}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{TK\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathds{1}_{TK}}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TK}

is commutative,
that the two rows are exact and that
K\simeq_{r}0
. Then the
induced morphism
f^{\prime}
is unique up to
r
-equivalence.

Proof.

Since
K\simeq_{r}0
, by definition,
\phi
is an

r
-isomorphism. For any two induced morphisms

f^{\prime}_{1},f^{\prime}_{2}\in{\rm Mor}^{0}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
, we have

f^{\prime}_{1}\circ\phi=f^{\prime}_{2}\circ\phi=\psi\circ f
and the conclusion
follows from Corollary
4.1.10
.
∎

Corollary 4.1.12
.

Let
\phi:A\to A^{\prime}
be an

r
-isomorphism. Then for any
f\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(A,B)
, there exists

f^{\prime}\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(A^{\prime},\Sigma^{-r}B)
such that the following diagram
commutes in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
.

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{B}_{r}}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-r}B}

Proof.

Since
\phi:A\to A^{\prime}
is an
r
-isomorphism, there
exists an left
r
-inverse denoted by
\psi:A^{\prime}\to\Sigma^{-r}A

such that
\psi\circ\phi=\eta^{A}_{r}
. Set

f^{\prime}:=\Sigma^{-r}f\circ\psi\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(A^{\prime},\Sigma^{-r}B)
.
∎

Similar direct arguments lead to the next consequence.

Corollary 4.1.13
.

Consider the following commutative diagram
in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
,

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}}

where
f\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(A,B)
,

f^{\prime}\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(A^{\prime}.B^{\prime})
and
\phi,\phi^{\prime}
are
r
-isomorphisms. Let

\psi,\psi^{\prime}
be any left inverses of
\phi,\phi^{\prime}

respectively. Then the following diagram is
r
-commutative

\textstyle{A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-r}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{-r}f}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-r}B}

in the sense that

\Sigma^{-r}f\circ\psi\simeq_{r}\psi^{\prime}\circ f^{\prime}
. A similar
conclusion holds for right inverses.

4.2.
Weighted exact triangles

The key feature of a triangulated persistence category
\mathcal{C}
is that
there is a natural way to associate weights to a class of triangles
larger than the exact triangles in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
.

Definition 4.2.1
.

A
strict exact triangle
\Delta
of
(strict) weight
r\geq 0
in
\mathcal{C}
is a diagram

A\xrightarrow{\bar{u}}B\xrightarrow{\bar{v}}C\xrightarrow{\bar{w}}\Sigma^{-r}TA

(24)

with
\bar{u}\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(A,B)
,
\bar{v}\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(B,C)
and

\bar{w}\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(C,\Sigma^{-r}TA)
such that there exists an
exact triangle

A\xrightarrow{u}B\xrightarrow{v}C^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w}TA
in

\mathcal{C}_{0}
, an
r
-isomorphism
\phi:C^{\prime}\to C
and a right
r
-inverse
of
\phi
denoted by
\psi:\Sigma^{r}C\to C^{\prime}
such that in the
following diagram

\textstyle{\Sigma^{r}C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{r}\bar{w}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\scriptstyle{\bar{v}}
\textstyle{C^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{w}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{TA}
\textstyle{C}

(25)

we have
\bar{u}=u
,
\bar{v}=\phi\circ v
and

\Sigma^{r}\bar{w}=w\circ\psi
. The weight of the triangle

\Delta
is denoted by
w(\Delta)=r
.

We will generally only talk about the weight of a strict exact
triangle and not the strict weight except when the distinction
will be of relevance, later on in the paper.

Remark 4.2.2
.

(a) Any exact triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is a
strict exact triangle of weight
0
since
\mathds{1}_{A}\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(A,A)
for any
A\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
. Conversely, it is a simple
exercise to see that a strict exact triangle of weight
0
is exact
as a triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
.

(b) Consider the following diagram

\textstyle{\Sigma^{r}B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{r}^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{r}\bar{v}}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{r}C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{r}\bar{w}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\scriptstyle{\bar{v}}
\textstyle{C^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{w}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{TA}_{r}}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{w}}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-r}TA}

which is derived
from the commutative diagram (
25
). The two squares in
the diagram are not commutative, in general, but they are

r
-commutative. Indeed, since
\phi
is an
r
-isomorphism let

\tilde{\psi}:C\to\Sigma^{-r}C^{\prime}
be a left
r
-inverse of
\phi
.
As
\psi
is a right
r
-inverse of
\phi
we deduce from Proposition

4.1.5
(i) that
\Sigma^{-r}\psi\simeq_{r}\tilde{\psi}
. Therefore,

\bar{w}\circ\phi=\Sigma^{-r}w\circ\Sigma^{-r}\psi\circ\phi\simeq_{r}\Sigma^{-r}w\circ\tilde{\psi}\circ\phi=\Sigma^{-r}w\circ\eta_{r}^{C^{\prime}}=\eta_{r}^{TA}\circ w
. Using Corollary

4.1.10
, we also see that

\psi\circ\Sigma^{r}\bar{v}\simeq_{r}v\circ\eta_{r}^{B}
because

\phi\circ\psi\circ\Sigma^{r}\bar{v}=\phi\circ v\circ\eta_{r}^{B}
.

(c) Because
T
commutes with
\Sigma
and with the natural
transformations
\eta
, it immediately follows that this functor
preserves strict exact triangles as well as their weight.

Example 4.2.3
.

Recall that the map

\Sigma^{r}A\xrightarrow{\eta^{A}_{r}}A
embeds into an exact triangle
in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
,

\Sigma^{r}A\xrightarrow{\eta^{A}_{r}}A\xrightarrow{v}K\xrightarrow{w}T\Sigma^{r}A
, where
K
is
r
-acyclic.
We claim that the diagram

\Sigma^{r}A\xrightarrow{\eta^{A}_{r}}A\xrightarrow{v}K\xrightarrow{0}TA

is a strict exact triangle of weight

r
. Indeed, we have the following commutative diagram,

\textstyle{\Sigma^{r}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{K}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{r}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{A}_{r}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\scriptstyle{v}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{w}
\scriptstyle{\mathds{1}_{K}}
\textstyle{T\Sigma^{r}A}
\textstyle{K}

where the right upper triangle is commutative since
K
is

r
-acyclic (so
\eta^{K}_{r}=0
by Lemma
3.3.4
(i)). Moreover,
\mathds{1}_{K}
is an

r
-isomorphism (we recall
T\Sigma^{r}A=\Sigma^{r}TA
).

Note that also the following diagram

\Sigma^{r}A\xrightarrow{\eta^{A}_{r}}A\xrightarrow{}0\xrightarrow{}TA

is a strict exact triangle of weight
r
.

Proposition 4.2.4

(Weight invariance)
.

Strict exact
triangles satisfy the following two properties:

(i) If strict exact triangles
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}
are

0
-isomorphic, then
w(\Delta)=r
if and only if

w(\Delta^{\prime})=r
.

(ii) If

\Delta:A\xrightarrow{\bar{u}}B\xrightarrow{\bar{v}}C\xrightarrow{\bar{w}}\Sigma^{-r}TA
satisfies
w(\Delta)=r
,
then

\Delta^{\prime}:A\xrightarrow{\bar{u}}B\xrightarrow{\bar{v}}C\xrightarrow{\bar{w}^{\prime}}\Sigma^{-r-s}TA
satisfies

w(\Delta^{\prime})=r+s
for
s\geq 0
, where
\bar{w}^{\prime}
is the
composition

C\xrightarrow{\bar{w}}\Sigma^{-r}TA\xrightarrow{\eta_{s}^{\Sigma^{-r}TA}}\Sigma^{-r-s}TA~.~

Proof.

(i) Two triangles in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
are
0
-isomorphic if they
are related through a triangle isomorphism in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
. The property
claimed here immediately follows from the fact that, within
\mathcal{C}_{0}
,
all
0
-isomorphisms admit inverses.

(ii) By definition, there exists a commutative diagram,

\textstyle{\Sigma^{r}C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{r}\bar{w}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{u}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\scriptstyle{\bar{v}}
\textstyle{C^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{w}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{TA}
\textstyle{C}

Consider
\psi^{\prime}\in{\rm Mor}^{r}(\Sigma^{r+s}C,C^{\prime})

defined by
\psi^{\prime}=\psi\circ\eta^{\Sigma^{r}C}_{s}
. Then
\psi^{\prime}

is an
(r+s)
-right inverse of
\phi
. Consider the following
diagram

\textstyle{\Sigma^{r+s}C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{r+s}\bar{w}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{u}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\scriptstyle{\bar{v}}
\textstyle{C^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{w}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{TA}
\textstyle{C}

where
\bar{w}^{\prime}:C\to\Sigma^{-r-s}TA
is defined by

\bar{w}^{\prime}=\eta^{\Sigma^{-r}TA}_{s}\circ\bar{w}
and notice that the
right upper triangle is commutative.
∎

Proposition 4.2.5

(Weighted rotation property)
.

Given a
strict exact triangle

\Delta:A\xrightarrow{\bar{u}}B\xrightarrow{\bar{v}}C\xrightarrow{\bar{w}}\Sigma^{-r}TA

satisfying
w(\Delta)=r
,
there exists a triangle

R(\Delta):B\xrightarrow{\bar{v}}C\xrightarrow{\bar{w}^{\prime}}\Sigma^{-r}TA\xrightarrow{-\bar{u}^{\prime}}\Sigma^{-2r}TB

(26)

satisfying
w(R(\Delta))=2r
, where
\bar{w}^{\prime}\simeq_{r}\bar{w}

and
\bar{u}^{\prime}
is the composition

\bar{u}^{\prime}:\Sigma^{-r}TA\xrightarrow{\Sigma^{-r}T\bar{u}}\Sigma^{-r}TB\xrightarrow{\eta_{r}^{\Sigma^{-r}TB}}\Sigma^{-2r}TB~.~

We call
R(\Delta)
the (first) positive rotation of
\Delta
.

Proof.

By definition, there exists a commutative diagram,

\textstyle{\Sigma^{r}C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{r}\bar{w}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{u}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\scriptstyle{\bar{v}}
\textstyle{C^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{w}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{TA}
\textstyle{C}

where

A\xrightarrow{\bar{u}}B\xrightarrow{v}C^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w}TA
is
an exact triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
and
\psi
is a right

r
-inverse of
\phi
. By the rotation property of
\mathcal{C}_{0}
,

B\xrightarrow{v}C^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w}TA\xrightarrow{-T\bar{u}}TB

is an exact triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
. We now construct the
following diagram in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
in which the upper squares will be
commutative and the lower square
r
-commutative:

\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\scriptstyle{\mathds{1}_{B}}
\textstyle{C^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{w}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-T\bar{u}}
\scriptstyle{\bar{\phi}}
\textstyle{TB\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathds{1}_{TB}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{v}}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{w^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{-r}\psi}
\textstyle{TA^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\bar{\psi}}
\textstyle{TB}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-r}C^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{-r}w}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-r}TA}

(27)

Here the second row of maps comes from embedding

B\xrightarrow{\bar{v}}C
into an exact triangle

B\xrightarrow{\bar{v}}C\xrightarrow{w^{\prime}}A^{\prime\prime}\to TB
for some

A^{\prime\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
and
A^{\prime}=T^{-1}A^{\prime\prime}
. The map
\bar{\phi}
is then
induced by the functoriality of triangles in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
and is an

r
-isomorphism by Corollary
4.1.8
. So far this gives the
upper three squares of the diagram and their commutativity. To
construct the lower square, let
\bar{\psi}
be a left
r
-inverse
of
\bar{\phi}

(i.e.
\bar{\psi}\circ\bar{\phi}=\eta^{TA}_{r}
). By
Corollary
4.1.9
,
\bar{\psi}
is a
2r
-isomorphism.

We claim that the lower square in diagram (
27
)
is
r
-commutative, and therefore we have

\bar{\psi}\circ w^{\prime}\simeq_{r}\Sigma^{-r}w\circ\Sigma^{-r}\psi=\bar{w}
.

Indeed, let
\psi^{\prime}
be a left
r
-inverse of
\phi
. By
using the commutativity of the middle upper square in
diagram (
27
), we deduce

\bar{\psi}\circ w^{\prime}\circ\phi=\Sigma^{-r}w\circ\psi^{\prime}\circ\phi
. As
\phi
is an
r
-isomorphism we obtain

\bar{\psi}\circ w^{\prime}\simeq_{r}\Sigma^{-r}w\circ\psi^{\prime}\simeq_{r}\Sigma^{-r}w\circ\Sigma^{-r}\psi=\bar{w},

(28)

because, by Proposition
4.1.5
, we have

\Sigma^{-r}\psi\simeq_{r}\psi^{\prime}
. This shows the lower square is

r
-commutative and the related
r
-identity.

We next consider the following diagram

\textstyle{\Sigma^{r}TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{\phi}\circ\eta^{TA}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{2r}\bar{u}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{v}}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{w^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\bar{w}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{TA^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\bar{\psi}}
\textstyle{TB}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-r}TA}

where

\bar{u}^{\prime}=\Sigma^{-2r}(u^{\prime}\circ\bar{\phi}\circ\eta_{r}^{TA})
and

\bar{w}^{\prime}=\bar{\psi}\circ w^{\prime}
. Given that
\bar{\psi}
is a

2r
-isomorphism, this means that we have a strict exact triangle of
weight
2r
of the form:

B\xrightarrow{\bar{v}}C\xrightarrow{\bar{w}^{\prime}}TA^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\bar{u}^{\prime}}\Sigma^{-2r}TB

We already know

\bar{w}^{\prime}=\bar{\psi}\circ w^{\prime}\simeq_{r}\bar{w}
. On the other hand,

\Sigma^{2r}\bar{u}^{\prime}=u^{\prime}\circ(\bar{\phi}\circ\eta^{TA}_{r})=-T\bar{u}\circ\eta^{TA}_{r}=-\eta^{\Sigma^{r}TB}_{r}\circ\Sigma^{r}T\bar{u}

which concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.2.6
.

A perfectly similar argument also
shows that there exists a strict exact triangle of strict weight

2r
and of the form:

R^{-1}(\Delta):T^{-1}\Sigma^{r}C\to A\to B\to\Sigma^{-r}C

which is the (first) negative rotation of
\Delta
. Note that
R^{-1}(R(\Delta))\neq R(R^{-1}(\Delta))\neq\Delta
.

Remark 4.2.7
.

Proposition
4.2.5
describes a rotation of weighted
exact triangles that does
not
preserve weights. Indeed, the
rotation of the weight
r
triangle
\Delta
from
Proposition
4.2.5
has weight
2r
. It is not clear to
what extent one can improve this. Ideally, one would like to be
able to rotate
\Delta
into a weighted exact triangle

B\xrightarrow{}C\xrightarrow{}\Sigma^{-r}TA\xrightarrow{}\Sigma^{-r}TB
of the
same
weight
r
. There is some
evidence, coming from symplectic topology, indicating that in
certain circumstances this might be possible
(see §
6.6.8
). However, the algebraic setting in
this paper, in particular the definition of weighted exact
triangles, might be too general to render this feasible, at least
without additional assumptions on
\Delta
.

Proposition 4.2.8

(Weighted octahedral formula)
.

Given
two strict exact triangles

\Delta_{1}:E\xrightarrow{\beta}F\xrightarrow{\alpha}X\xrightarrow{k}\Sigma^{-r}TE

and

\Delta_{2}:X\xrightarrow{u}A\xrightarrow{\gamma}B\xrightarrow{b}\Sigma^{-s}TX

with
w(\Delta_{1})=r
and

w(\Delta_{2})=s
, there exists diagram

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TE\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TE\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T\beta}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{u\circ\alpha}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{id}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TF\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\Sigma^{-s}T\alpha)\circ\eta^{TF}_{s}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-s}TX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{-s}(Tk)}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-r}TE\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-r-s}T^{2}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-r-s}T^{2}E}

(29)

with all squares commutative except for the right bottom one that is

r
-anti-commutative, such that the triangles

\Delta_{3}:F\to A\to C\to TF
and

\Delta_{4}:TE\to C\to B\to\Sigma^{-r-s}T^{2}E
are strict exact
with
w(\Delta_{3})=0
and
w(\Delta_{4})=r+s
.

By forgetting the
\Sigma
’s (or assuming that
r=s=0
) this is
equivalent to the usual octahedral axiom in a triangulated category
(namely
\mathcal{C}_{0}
) and the right bottom square is commutative up to
sign (or anti-commutative).

Proof of Proposition
4.2.8
.

By definition, there are two commutative diagrams,

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{r}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{r}k}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{TE}

\textstyle{\Sigma^{s}B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{s}b}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\bar{\phi}}
\textstyle{TX}
\textstyle{B}

(30)

with
\phi
an
r
-isomorphism and
\bar{\phi}
an
s
-isomorphism
and
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{3}
are, their right
r
and
s
-inverses,
respectively. By the octahedral axiom in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
, we construct the
following diagram commutative except for the right bottom square
that is anti-commutative:

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TE\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TE\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\prime}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathds{1}_{A}}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{w}
\textstyle{TF\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T\alpha^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u\circ\phi}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\textstyle{B^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{TX^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T\delta}
\textstyle{TE\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Thus

,

.

. The map

. We fix a right

. The

)) and notice that

is a right

.

We are now able to define the triangle

:

.

It is easy to check that all the squares in

), except the right bottom one, are commutative.

,
which is equivalent to

. Given that the

, we have the following

∎

Given a triple of maps

\Delta:A\xrightarrow{u}B\xrightarrow{v}C\xrightarrow{w}D
with
shifts
\left\lceil u\right\rceil,\left\lceil v\right\rceil,\left\lceil w\right\rceil\in\mathbb{R}
it is useful to
introduce a special notation for an associated triple in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
,
denoted by
\Sigma^{s_{1},s_{2},s_{3},s_{4}}\Delta
, for

s_{1},s_{2},s_{3},s_{4}\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying the following relations

\displaystyle-s_{1}+s_{2}+\left\lceil u\right\rceil

\displaystyle\leq 0

\displaystyle-s_{2}+s_{3}+\left\lceil v\right\rceil

\displaystyle\leq 0

\displaystyle-s_{3}+s_{4}+\left\lceil w\right\rceil

\displaystyle\leq 0.

The triple
\Sigma^{s_{1},s_{2},s_{3},s_{4}}\Delta
has the form

\Sigma^{s_{1}}A\xrightarrow{\bar{u}}\Sigma^{s_{2}}B\xrightarrow{\bar{v}}\Sigma^{s_{3}}\xrightarrow{\bar{w}}\Sigma^{s_{4}}D

(33)

where
\bar{u}
is the composition of the composition

\Sigma^{s_{1}}A\xrightarrow{(\eta_{s_{1},0})_{A}}A\xrightarrow{u}B\xrightarrow{(\eta_{0,s_{2}})_{B}}\Sigma^{s_{2}}B
and the persistence
structure map
i_{-s_{1}+s_{2},0}
, i.e.,

\bar{u}=i_{-s_{1}+s_{2}+\left\lceil u\right\rceil,0}\left((\eta_{0,s_{2}})_{B}\circ u\circ(\eta_{s_{1},0})_{A}\right).

(34)

The definitions of
\bar{v}
and
\bar{w}
are similar and, in
particular,
\left\lceil\bar{u}\right\rceil=\left\lceil\bar{v}\right\rceil=\left\lceil\bar{w}\right\rceil=0
. The
inequalities above ensure that the resulting triangle
(
33
) has all morphisms in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
.

For
s_{1}=s_{2}=s_{3}=s_{4}=k
(which implies that

\left\lceil u\right\rceil,\left\lceil v\right\rceil,\left\lceil w\right\rceil\leq 0
) we denote, for
brevity,

\Sigma^{k}\Delta:=\Sigma^{s_{1},s_{2},s_{3},s_{4}}\Delta~.~

Remark 4.2.9
.

Assume that

\Delta:A\xrightarrow{u}B\xrightarrow{v}C\xrightarrow{w}\Sigma^{-r}TA
is strict exact of weight
w(\Delta)=r

(a) It is a simple exercise to show that the triangle

\Sigma^{k}\Delta:\Sigma^{k}A\to\Sigma^{k}B\to\Sigma^{k}C\to\Sigma^{-r+k}TA
is strict exact and

w\left(\Sigma^{k}\Delta\right)=r
.

(b) For
s\geq 0
, Proposition
4.2.4
(ii) claims that

\Sigma^{0,0,0,-s}\Delta
is strict exact of weight
r+s
. It is
again an easy exercise to see that
\Sigma^{0,0,-s,-s}\Delta
is
strict exact of weight
r+s
.

Proposition 4.2.10

(Functoriality of triangles)
.

Consider two
strict exact triangles as below with
f\in{\rm Mor}^{0}(A_{1},A_{2})
and

g\in{\rm Mor}^{0}(B_{1},B_{2})

\textstyle{\Delta_{1}:}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

,

. Then there exists a morphism

r

The proof is left as exercise.

Proposition 4.2.11
.

Let

\Delta:A\xrightarrow{\bar{u}}B\xrightarrow{\bar{v}}C\xrightarrow{\bar{w}}\Sigma^{-r}TA
be a strict exact of weight

r
in
\mathcal{C}
and let

4.2.1
. For any

0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise-63.35114pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 25.07524pt\raise-57.51225pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bar{u}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 54.40048pt\raise-63.35114pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 54.40048pt\raise-63.35114pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 86.81252pt\raise-57.51225pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bar{v}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 116.13776pt\raise-63.35114pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 116.13776pt\raise-63.35114pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 150.71664pt\raise-57.51225pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bar{w}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 171.82549pt\raise-63.35114pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 171.82549pt\raise-63.35114pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Sigma^{-r}TA}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces~.~

Denote

h_{1}=(\eta_{r}^{A},\eta_{r}^{B},\psi,\mathds{1}_{TA})
and

h_{1}^{\prime}=(\mathds{1}_{A},\mathds{1}_{B},\phi,\eta_{r}^{TA})
. Notice that
h_{1}
as
well as
h^{\prime}_{1}
are not morphisms of triangles because the bottom
right-most square is only
r
-commutative, and the same is true for
the middle top square - as discussed in Remark
4.2.2

(b). Let
h=h_{1}\circ\eta_{r}
and
h^{\prime}=\eta_{r}\circ h^{\prime}_{1}

(where we view
\eta_{r}
as a quadruple of morphisms of the form

\eta_{r}^{-}
). It follows that both
h
and
h^{\prime}
are morphisms of
triangles. Moreover, given that
\phi\circ\psi=\eta_{r}^{C}
, it is
clear that
h^{\prime}\circ h=\eta_{3r}
. The other composition

h\circ\Sigma^{2r}h^{\prime}
has one term of the form

\psi\circ\eta_{r}\circ\Sigma^{3r}\phi\circ\eta_{r}
so, by
Proposition
4.1.5
, this coincides with
\eta_{3r}^{C^{\prime}}

as claimed.
∎

Remark 4.2.12
.

We have seen above that any strict exact triangle of weight
r
is
isomorphic, in the precise sense of Proposition
4.2.11
,
to an exact triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
. It is also useful to have a
converse of this result. Indeed, it is easy to show that if

\Delta:A\to B\to C\to TA
is a triangle with all morphisms in

\mathcal{C}_{0}
and if there is a morphism of triangles

h:\Delta\to\Delta^{\prime}
with
\Delta^{\prime}
an exact triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{0}

and with each component of
h
an
s
-isomorphism, then there exist

k,t\geq 0
such that the triangle
\Sigma^{0,0,-k,-k-t}\Delta
is
strict exact in
\mathcal{C}
. By combining this remark with the statement
in Proposition
4.2.11
and using that the natural maps

\eta_{r}^{-}
are
r
-isomorphisms, one can easily see that the
same statement remains true in the more general case when
\Delta^{\prime}

is strict exact in
\mathcal{C}
.

4.3.
Fragmentation pseudo-metrics on

\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})

In a triangulated persistence category there is a natural notion of
iterated-cone decomposition, similar to the corresponding notion in
the triangulated setting from §
2
.

Definition 4.3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a triangulated
persistence category, and
X\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
. An
iterated
cone decomposition

D
of
X
with
linearization

(X_{1},X_{2},...,X_{n})
where
X_{i}\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
consists of a
family of strict exact triangles in
\mathcal{C}

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Delta_{1}:&X_{1}\to 0\to Y_{1}\to\Sigma^{-r_{1}}TX_{1}\\
\Delta_{2}:&X_{2}\to Y_{1}\to Y_{2}\to\Sigma^{-r_{2}}TX_{2}\\
\Delta_{3}:&X_{3}\to Y_{2}\to Y_{3}\to\Sigma^{-r_{3}}TX_{3}\\
&\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\vdots\\
\Delta_{n}:&X_{n}\to Y_{n-1}\to X\to\Sigma^{-r_{n}}TX_{n}\end{array}\right.

The (strict) weight of such a cone decomposition is defined by

w(D)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w(\Delta_{i})~.~

The linearization of
D
is denoted by
\ell(D)=(X_{1},...,X_{n})
.

Proposition 4.3.2
.

Assume that
X
admits an iterated
cone decomposition
D
with linearization
(X_{1},...,X_{n})
and for
some
i\in\{1,...,n\}
,
X_{i}
admits an iterated cone
decomposition
D^{\prime}
with linearization
(A_{1},...,A_{k})
. Then
X

admits an iterated cone decomposition
D^{\prime\prime}
of linearization

(X_{1},...,X_{i-1},TA_{1},...,TA_{k},X_{i+1},...,X_{n}).

(35)

Moreover, the weights of these cone decompositions satisfy

w(D^{\prime\prime})=w(D)+w(D^{\prime})
.

A cone decomposition
D^{\prime\prime}
as in the statement of Proposition
(
35
) is called a refinement of the cone
decomposition
D
with respect to
D^{\prime}
.

Example 4.3.3
.

A single strict exact triangle

A\to B\to X\to\Sigma^{-r}TA
can be regarded as a cone
decomposition
D
of
X
with linearization
(T^{-1}B,A)
such that

w(D)=r
. Assume that
A
fits into a second strict exact triangle

E\to F\to A\to\Sigma^{-s}TE
of weight
s
. Thus we have a
cone-decomposition of
A
,
D^{\prime}
, with linearization
(T^{-1}F,E)
,

w(D^{\prime})=s
. Diagram (
29
) from Proposition
4.2.8

yields the following commutative diagram,

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TE\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X}

for some object

Y\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
. In particular, we obtain a strict exact
triangle
TE\to Y\to X\to\Sigma^{-r-s}T^{2}E
of weight

r+s
. Thus, we have a refinement of
D
with respect to
D^{\prime}
as
follows,

D^{\prime\prime}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}T^{-1}B\to 0\to B\to B\\
F\to B\to Y\to TF\\
TE\to Y\to X\to\Sigma^{-r-s}TE\end{array}\right.

Moreover,
w(D^{\prime\prime})=r+s=w(D)+w(D^{\prime})
.

Proof of Proposition
4.3.2
.

By
definition, the cone decomposition
D
consists of a family of
strict exact triangles in
\mathcal{C}
as follows,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}&\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\vdots\\
\Delta_{i-1}:&X_{i-1}\to Y_{i-2}\to Y_{i-1}\to\Sigma^{-r_{i-1}}X_{i-1}\\
\Delta_{i}:&X_{i}\,\,\,\,\,\,\to Y_{i-1}\to Y_{i}\,\,\,\,\,\,\to\Sigma^{-r_{i}}X_{i}\\
\Delta_{i+1}:&X_{i+1}\to Y_{i}\,\,\,\,\,\,\to Y_{i+1}\to\Sigma^{-r_{i+1}}X_{i+1}\\
&\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\vdots\\
\end{array}\right.

We aim to replace the triangle
\Delta_{i}
by a sequence of strict
exact triangles

\overline{\Delta}_{j}:A_{j}\to B_{j-1}\to B_{j}\to\Sigma^{-s_{j}}TA_{j}

for
j\in\{1,...,k\}
with
B_{0}=Y_{i-1}
,
B_{k}=Y_{i}
and
such that

\sum_{j=1}^{k}w(\overline{\Delta}_{j})=w(\Delta_{i})+w(D^{\prime}).

(36)

In this case, the ordered family of strict exact triangles

(\Delta_{1},...,\Delta_{i-1},\overline{\Delta}_{1},...,\overline{\Delta}_{k},\Delta_{i+1},...,\Delta_{n})
form the
refinement
D^{\prime\prime}
, and (
36
) implies that

w(D^{\prime\prime})=\sum_{j\in\{1,...,n\}\backslash\{i\}}w(\Delta_{j})+\sum_{j=1}^{k}w(\overline{\Delta}_{j})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}w(\Delta_{j})+w(D^{\prime})=w(D)+w(D^{\prime})

(37)

as claimed.

In order to obtain the desired sequence of strict exact triangles we
focus on
\Delta_{i}
and, to shorten notation, we rename its terms by

A=X_{i}
,
B=Y_{i-1}
,
C=Y_{i}
and
r=r_{i}
so that, with this
notation,
\Delta_{i}
is a strict exact triangle

A\to B\to C\to\Sigma^{-r}TA
.

We now fix notation for the cone decomposition
D^{\prime}
of
A=X_{i}
. It
consists of the following family of strict exact triangles,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Delta^{\prime}_{1}:&A_{1}\to 0\to Z_{1}\to\Sigma^{-s_{1}}TA_{1}\\
\Delta^{\prime}_{2}:&A_{2}\to Z_{1}\to Z_{2}\to\Sigma^{-s_{2}}TA_{2}\\
&\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\vdots\\
\Delta^{\prime}_{k-1}:&A_{k-1}\to Z_{k-2}\to Z_{k-1}\to\Sigma^{-s_{k-1}}TA_{k-1}\\
\Delta^{\prime}_{k}:&A_{k}\to Z_{k-1}\to A\to\Sigma^{-s_{k}}TA_{k}\end{array}\right.

We will apply Proposition
4.2.8
iteratively. The first
step is the following commutative diagram obtained from
(
29
),

\textstyle{A_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TA_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z_{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathds{1}_{B}}
\textstyle{B_{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C}

for some

B_{k-1}\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
. Define

\overline{\Delta}_{k}:TA_{k}\to B_{k-1}\to C\to\Sigma^{-r-s_{k}}T^{2}A_{k}~.~

We have

w(\overline{\Delta}_{k})=r+s_{k}=w(\Delta_{i})+w(\Delta^{\prime}_{k}).

(38)

We then consider the following commutative diagram again obtained
from (
29
),

\textstyle{A_{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TA_{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z_{k-2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{k-2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z_{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{k-1}}

(39)

for some
B_{k-2}\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
. Define

\overline{\Delta}_{k-1}
to be the strict exact triangle:

\overline{\Delta}_{k-1}:TA_{k-1}\to B_{k-2}\to B_{k-1}\to\Sigma^{-s_{k-1}}T^{2}A_{k-1}~.~

Then

w(\overline{\Delta}_{k-1})=s_{k-1}=w(\Delta^{\prime}_{k-1}).

(40)

Inductively, we obtain
B_{i}\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
, strict exact
triangles

\overline{\Delta}_{i}:TA_{i}\to B_{i-1}\to B_{i}\to\Sigma^{-s_{i}}T^{2}A_{i}

(41)

for
2\leq i\leq k-1
such that

w(\overline{\Delta}_{i})=s_{i}=w(\Delta^{\prime}_{i}).

(42)

The final step lies in the consideration of the following diagram,

\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TA_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{1}}

(43)

for some
B_{0}\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
. Define
\overline{\Delta}_{1}
to be
the strict triangle

\overline{\Delta}_{1}:TA_{1}\to B\to B_{1}\to TA_{1}~.~

Then

w(\overline{\Delta}_{1})=w(\Delta^{\prime}_{1})=s_{1}~.~

(44)

Together, the ordered family

(\overline{\Delta}_{1},...,\overline{\Delta}_{k})
form the desired
sequence of strict exact triangles. Finally, the
equalities (
38
), (
42
)
and (
44
) yield

\sum_{j=1}^{k}w(\overline{\Delta}_{j})=s_{1}+s_{2}+...+s_{k}+r=\sum_{j=1}^{k}w(\Delta^{\prime}_{j})+w(\Delta_{i})=w(D^{\prime})+w(\Delta_{i})

as claimed in (
36
).
∎

Let
\mathcal{F}\subset{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
be a family of objects of
\mathcal{C}
.
For two objects
X,X^{\prime}\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
, define just as in
§
2
,

\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(X,X^{\prime})=\inf\left\{w(D)\,\Bigg|\,\begin{array}[]{ll}\mbox{$D$ is an
iterated cone decomposition}\\
\mbox{of $X$ with
linearization}\ \mbox{$(F_{1},...,T^{-1}X^{\prime},...,F_{k})$}\\
\mbox{ where $F_{i}\in\mathcal{F}$, $k\in\mathbb{N}$}\end{array}\right\}.

(45)

Corollary 4.3.4
.

With the definition of
\delta^{\mathcal{F}}
in
(
45
), we have the following inequality:

\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(X,X^{\prime})\leq\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(X,X^{\prime\prime})+\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(X^{\prime\prime},X^{\prime})

for any
X,X^{\prime},X^{\prime\prime}\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

For any
\epsilon>0
, there are cone decompositions
D
of

X
and
D^{\prime}
of
X^{\prime\prime}
respectively such that

w(D)\leq\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(X,X^{\prime\prime})+\epsilon\,\,\,\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,\,\,w(D^{\prime})\leq\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(X^{\prime\prime},X^{\prime})+\epsilon

with linearizations

\ell(D)=(F_{1},...,T^{-1}X^{\prime\prime},...,F_{s})
and

\ell(D^{\prime})=(F^{\prime}_{1},...,T^{-1}X^{\prime},...,F^{\prime}_{k})
, respectively,

F_{i},F^{\prime}_{j}\in\mathcal{F}
. That means that
T^{-1}X^{\prime\prime}
has a corresponding
cone decomposition
T^{-1}D^{\prime}
with linearization

\ell(T^{-1}D^{\prime})=(T^{-1}F^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,T^{-2}X^{\prime},\ldots,T^{-1}F^{\prime}_{k})
. Proposition
4.3.2
implies that there
exists a cone decomposition
D^{\prime\prime}
of
X
that is a refinement of
D

with respect to
T^{-1}D^{\prime}
such that

\ell(D^{\prime\prime})=(F_{1},\ldots,F^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,T^{-1}X^{\prime},\ldots F^{\prime}_{k},\ldots,F_{s})
and

w(D^{\prime\prime})=w(D)+w(D^{\prime})\leq\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(X,X^{\prime\prime})+\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(X^{\prime\prime},X^{\prime})+2\epsilon

which implies the claim.
∎

Finally, there are also fragmentation pseudo-metrics specific to this
situation with properties similar to those in Proposition

2.0.4
.

Definition 4.3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a triangulated
persistence category and let
\mathcal{F}\subset{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
. The
fragmentation pseudo-metric

d^{\mathcal{F}}:{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})\times{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})\to[0,\infty)\cup\{+\infty\}

associated to
\mathcal{F}
is defined by:

d^{\mathcal{F}}(X,X^{\prime})=\max\{\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(X,X^{\prime}),\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(X^{\prime},X)\}.

Remark 4.3.6
.

(a) It is clear from Corollary

4.3.4
that
d^{\mathcal{F}}
satisfies the triangle inequality and,
by definition, it is symmetric. It is immediate to see that

d^{\mathcal{F}}(X,X)=0
for all objects
X
(this is because of the
existence of the exact triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
,
T^{-1}X\to 0\to X
).
It is of course possible that this pseudo-metric be degenerate and
it is also possible that it is not finite.

(b) If
X\in\mathcal{F}
, then
\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(TX,0)=0
because of the exact
triangle
X\to 0\to TX
. On the other hand,
\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(0,TX)
is
not generally trivial. However, the exact triangle
TX\to TX\to 0

shows that, if
TX\in\mathcal{F}
, then
\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(0,TX)=0
.

(c) It follows from the previous point that if
\mathcal{F}={\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
,
then
d^{\mathcal{F}}(X,X^{\prime})=0
(in other words, the pseudo-metric

d^{\mathcal{F}}(-,-)
is completely degenerate). More generally,
if the family
\mathcal{F}
is
T
invariant (in the sense that if
X\in\mathcal{F}
,
then
T^{\pm}X\in\mathcal{F}
), then
T
is an isometry relative to the
pseudo-metric
d^{\mathcal{F}}
and
d^{\mathcal{F}}(X,X^{\prime})=0
for all
X,X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
.

(d) The remark
2.0.5
(c) applies also in this
setting in the sense that we may define at this triangulated
persistence level fragmentation pseudo-metrics

\underline{d}^{\mathcal{F}}
given by (the symmetrization of)
formula (
7
) but making use of weighted triangles
in
\mathcal{C}
instead of the exact triangles in the triangulated
category
\mathcal{D}
.

Recall that by assumption
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is triangulated and thus
additive. Therefore, for any two objects

X,X^{\prime}\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C}_{0})={\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
, the direct sum

X\oplus X^{\prime}
is a well-defined object in
{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proposition 4.3.7
.

For any

A,B,A^{\prime},B^{\prime}\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
, we have

d^{\mathcal{F}}(A\oplus B,A^{\prime}\oplus B^{\prime})\leq d^{\mathcal{F}}(A,A^{\prime})+d^{\mathcal{F}}(B,B^{\prime}).

Proof.

The proof follows easily from the following lemma.

Lemma 4.3.8
.

Let

\Delta:A\to B\to C\to\Sigma^{-r}TA
and

\overline{\Delta}:\overline{A}\to\overline{B}\to\overline{C}\to\Sigma^{-s}T\overline{A}
be two strict exact triangles with

w(\Delta)=r
and
w(\Delta^{\prime})=s
. Then

\Delta^{\prime\prime}:A\oplus\overline{A}\to B\oplus\overline{B}\to C\oplus\overline{C}\to\Sigma^{-\max\{r,s\}}TA\oplus T\overline{A}

is a strict exact triangle with

w(\Delta^{\prime\prime})=\max\{r,s\}
.

Proof of Lemma
4.3.8
.

By definition, there are two commutative diagrams,

\textstyle{\Sigma^{r}C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{TA}
\textstyle{C}

\textstyle{\Sigma^{s}\overline{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{\psi}}
\textstyle{\overline{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\overline{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\overline{C^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{\phi}}
\textstyle{T\overline{A}}
\textstyle{\overline{C}}

This yields the following commutative
diagram,

\textstyle{\Sigma^{\max\{r,s\}}C\oplus\overline{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi\oplus\bar{\psi}}
\textstyle{A\oplus\overline{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\oplus\overline{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{\prime}\oplus\overline{C^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi\oplus\bar{\phi}}
\textstyle{TA\oplus T\overline{A}}
\textstyle{C\oplus\overline{C}}

and it is easy to check that

\phi\oplus\bar{\phi}
is a

\max\{r,s\}
-isomorphism.
∎

Returning to the proof of the proposition, it suffices to prove

\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(A\oplus B,A^{\prime}\oplus B^{\prime})\leq\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(A,A^{\prime})+\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(B,B^{\prime})
. For any
\epsilon>0
, by definition, there exist
cone decompositions
D
and
D^{\prime}
of
A
and
B
respectively with

\ell(D)=(F_{1},...,F_{i-1},T^{-1}A^{\prime},F_{i+1},...,F_{s})
and

\ell(D^{\prime})=(F^{\prime}_{1},...,T^{-1}B^{\prime},...,F^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}})
such that

w(D)\leq\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(A,A^{\prime})+\epsilon\,\,\,\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,\,\,\,w(D^{\prime})\leq\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(B,B^{\prime})+\epsilon.

The desired cone decomposition of
A\oplus B
is defined as follows.

D^{\prime\prime}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}F_{1}\to 0\to E_{1}\to\Sigma^{-r_{1}}TF_{1}\\
F_{2}\to E_{1}\to E_{2}\to\Sigma^{-r_{2}}TF_{2}\\
\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\vdots\\
F_{s}\to E_{s-1}\to A\to\Sigma^{-r_{s}}TF_{s}\\
F^{\prime}_{1}\to A\oplus 0\to A\oplus E^{\prime}_{1}\to\Sigma^{-r^{\prime}_{1}}TF^{\prime}_{1}\\
F^{\prime}_{2}\to A\oplus E^{\prime}_{1}\to A\oplus E^{\prime}_{2}\to\Sigma^{-r^{\prime}_{2}}TF^{\prime}_{s+2-i}\\
\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\vdots\\
T^{-1}B^{\prime}\to A\oplus E^{\prime}_{j}\to A\oplus E^{\prime}_{j+1}\to\Sigma^{-r^{\prime}_{B^{\prime}}}B^{\prime}\\
\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\vdots\\
F^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}\to A\oplus E^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}-1}\to A\oplus B\to\Sigma^{-r^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}}TF^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}\end{array}\right.

(46)

Here we identify
0\oplus F^{\prime}_{i}=F^{\prime}_{i}
. The first
s
-triangles
come from the decomposition of
A
and the following
s^{\prime}
triangles
are associated, using Lemma
4.3.8
, to the respective
triangles in the decomposition of
B
and to the triangle

0\to A\to A
(of weight
0
). It is obvious that

w(D^{\prime\prime})=w(D)+w(D^{\prime})
and thus

\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(A\oplus B,A^{\prime}\oplus B^{\prime})\leq\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(A,A^{\prime})+\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(B,B^{\prime})
.
∎

The next statement
is an immediate consequence of Proposition
4.3.7
.

Corollary 4.3.9
.

The set
{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
with the topology induced by the fragmentation pseudo-metric
d^{\mathcal{F}}
is an
H
-space relative to the operation

(A,B)\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})\times{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})\to A\oplus B\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})~.~

4.3.1.
Proof of Proposition

2.0.4

We now return to the setting in §
2
. Thus,

\mathcal{D}
is triangulated category,
w
is a triangular weight on

\mathcal{D}
in the sense of Definition
2.0.1
, and
the quantities
w(D)
(associated to an iterated cone-decomposition

D
),
\delta^{\mathcal{F}}
,
d^{\mathcal{F}}
are defined as in
§
2
.

The first (and main) step is to establish a result similar to
Proposition
4.3.2
. Namely, if
X
admits an iterated
cone decomposition
D
with linearization
(X_{1},...,X_{n})
and some

X_{i}
admits a decomposition
D^{\prime}
with linearization

(A_{1},...,A_{k})
, then
X
admits an iterated cone decomposition

D^{\prime\prime}
with linearization

(X_{1},...,X_{i-1},TA_{1},...,TA_{k},X_{i+1},...,X_{n})
and

w(D^{\prime\prime})\leq w(D)+w(D^{\prime})

(47)

For convenience, recall from §
2
that the
expression of
w(D)
is:

w(D)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w(\Delta_{i})-w_{0}

(48)

where
w_{0}=w(0\to X\xrightarrow{id}X\to 0)
(for all
X
). To show
(
47
) we go through exactly the same construction of
the refinement
D^{\prime\prime}
of the decomposition
D
with respect to
D^{\prime}
, as
in the proof of
4.3.2
, assuming now that all shifts are
trivial along the way. The analogue of diagram (
43
)
that appears in the last step of the construction of
D^{\prime\prime}
remains
possible in this context due to the point (ii) of Definition

2.0.1
. By tracking the respective weights along the
construction and using Remark
2.0.2
(b) to estimate
the weight of
TA_{1}\to B\to B_{1}\to T^{2}A_{1}
from
(
43
) we deduce (with the notation in the proof of
Proposition
4.3.2
)

\sum_{j=1}w(\bar{\Delta}_{j})\leq w(D^{\prime})+w(\Delta_{i})-w_{0}

which implies (
47
). Once formula
(
47
) established, it immediately follows that

\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(-,-)
satisfies the triangle
inequality. Further, because the weight of a cone-decomposition is
given by (
48
), it follows that the
cone-decomposition
D
of
X
with linearization
(T^{-1}X)
given by
the single exact triangle
T^{-1}X\to 0\to X\to X
is of weight

w(D)=0
. As a consequence,
\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(X,X)=0
. It follows
that
d^{\mathcal{F}}(-,-)
is a pseudo-metric as claimed at the point
(i) of Proposition
2.0.4
.

Assuming now that
w
is subadditive, the same type of decomposition
as in equation (
46
) can be constructed to show that

\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(A\oplus B,A^{\prime}\oplus B^{\prime})\leq\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(A,A^{\prime})+\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(B,B^{\prime})+w_{0}
which
implies the claim. ∎

5.
Triangulated structure of
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
and triangular weights

The purpose of this section is to further explore the structure of
the limit category
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
associated to a triangulated persistence category
\mathcal{C}
.
We will see that
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
is triangulated and that it carries a triangular weight, in the sense of §
2
, induced by the persistence structure.

5.1.
Exact triangles in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
and main algebraic result

Assume that
\mathcal{C}
is a persistence category and recall its
\infty
-level
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}

from Definition
3.1.4
: its objects are the same as those of
\mathcal{C}
and its morphisms
are
{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}(A,B)=\varinjlim_{\alpha\to\infty}{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}(A,B)
for any two objects
A,B
of
\mathcal{C}
. For a morphism
\bar{f}
in
\mathcal{C}
we denote by
[\bar{f}]
the corresponding morphism in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
and if
f=[\bar{f}]
,
f\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}
,
\bar{f}\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}
,
we say that
\bar{f}
represents
f
. We use the same terminology for diagrams (including triangles)
in
\mathcal{C}
in relation to corresponding diagrams in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
in the sense that a diagram in
\mathcal{C}
represents one in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
if the nodes in the two cases are the same and the morphisms of the diagram in
\mathcal{C}
represent the corresponding ones in the
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
diagram. Clearly, all
r
-commutativities and
r
-isomorphisms in
\mathcal{C}
become, respectively, commutativities and isomorphisms in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
. For instance, if
K
is
r
-acyclic, then
K
is isomorphic to
0
in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
.

The hom-sets of
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
admit a natural filtration as follows.
For any
A,B\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty})
, and
f\in{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}(A,B)
, let the
spectral invariant of
f
be given by:

\sigma(f):=\inf\left\{k\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}\,\bigg|\,\mbox{$f=\left[\bar{f}\right]$ for some $\bar{f}\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{k}(A,B)$}\right\}

and

{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}^{\leq\alpha}(A,B)=\{f\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}(A,B)\,|\,\sigma(f)\leq\alpha\}.

Assume from now on that
\mathcal{C}
is a triangulated persistence category.
It is easy to see that in this case
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
is
additive. Moreover, as explained in §
4.3
,
\mathcal{C}
is
endowed with a family of strict exact triangles. The purpose of this
section is to extract from the properties of the strict exact
triangles in
\mathcal{C}
a triangulated structure on
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
. This is
not quite immediate because a morphism
\bar{f}:A\to B
in
\mathcal{C}
of
strictly positive shift
\left\lceil\bar{f}\right\rceil>0
can not be completed to a
strict exact triangle. As a consequence, the definition of the exact
triangles in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
is necessarily more subtle than
just taking the image in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
of the strict exact triangles
in
\mathcal{C}
because we need to be able to complete to exact triangles
those morphisms
f
in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
with
\sigma(f)>0
.

Before proceeding with the relevant definition we notice that the
shift functor
\Sigma
associated to
\mathcal{C}
(see Definition

4.1.1
) induces a similar functor

\Sigma:(\mathbb{R},+)\to\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty})
. We will continue to
use the same notation for the
r
-shifts
\Sigma^{r}
and the natural
transformations
\eta_{r,s}:\Sigma^{r}\to\Sigma^{s}
. At the
same time, in contrast to morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
, there is no meaning to
the “amount of shift” for a morphism in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
(though one
can associate to such a morphism its spectral invariant as above).
Similarly, the functor
T
(which is defined as in
Remark
4.1.2
(b) on all of
\mathcal{C}
) also induces a similar
functor on
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
. Given a triple of maps

\Delta:A\to B\to C\to D
in
\mathcal{C}
we will make use of the shifted
triple
\Sigma^{s_{1},s_{2},s_{3},s_{4}}\Delta
as defined in
(
33
) and we will use the same notation for similar
triples in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
. Note however that in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
the
inequalities relating the
s_{i}
’s and the shifts of
u,v,w
are no
longer relevant (in fact, do not make sense) and the shift

\Sigma^{s_{1},s_{2},s_{3},s_{4}}
will be used in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}

without these constraints.

Definition 5.1.1
.

A triangle

\Delta:A\xrightarrow{u}B\xrightarrow{v}C\xrightarrow{w}TA

in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
is called
exact
if there exists a
diagram

\bar{\Delta}:A\xrightarrow{\bar{u}}B\xrightarrow{\bar{v}}C\xrightarrow{\bar{w}}TA
in
\mathcal{C}
that represents
\Delta
, such
that the shifts
\left\lceil\bar{u}\right\rceil,\left\lceil\bar{v}\right\rceil,\left\lceil\bar{w}\right\rceil

are all non-negative and the shifted triangle

\widetilde{\Delta}=\Sigma^{0,-\left\lceil\bar{u}\right\rceil,-\left\lceil\bar{u}\right\rceil-\left\lceil\bar{v}\right\rceil,-\left\lceil\bar{u}\right\rceil-\left\lceil\bar{v}\right\rceil-\left\lceil\bar{w}\right\rceil}\bar{\Delta}

(49)

is strict exact in
\mathcal{C}
. The
unstable weight
of
\Delta
,

w_{\infty}(\Delta)
, is the infimum of the strict weights

w(\widetilde{\Delta})
of all the strict exact triangles

\widetilde{\Delta}
as above. Finally, the
weight
of

\Delta
,
\bar{w}(\Delta)
, is given by:

\bar{w}(\Delta)=\inf\{\ w_{\infty}(\Sigma^{s,0,0,s}\Delta)\ |\ s\geq 0\}~.~

The convention is that if there does not exist
\widetilde{\Delta}
as
in the definition, then
w_{\infty}(\Delta)=\infty
. Notice that,
from the definition of the strict weights in
\mathcal{C}
, the strict weight
of
\widetilde{\Delta}
is

\left\lceil\bar{u}\right\rceil+\left\lceil\bar{v}\right\rceil+\left\lceil\bar{w}\right\rceil
. It is not
difficult to show that (see Remark
5.1.12
(b)) a triangle

\Delta
in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
is exact if and only if
\bar{w}(\Delta)
is
finite. Moreover, by definition,
\bar{w}(\Delta)\leq w_{\infty}(\Delta)
, and Example
5.1.9
below shows that this inequality can be strict.

For the weight
\bar{w}
of exact triangles in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
defined as above, recall that
\bar{w}_{0}
denotes the normalization constant in Definition
2.0.1
(ii). The main result is the following:

Theorem 5.1.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a triangulated persistence category. The limit category

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
together with the class of exact triangles defined in

5.1.1
is triangulated and
\bar{w}
is a subadditive
triangular weight on
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
with
\bar{w}_{0}=0
.

A persistence refinement of a triangulated category
\mathcal{D}
is a
TPC,
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
, such that

\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\infty}=\mathcal{D}
. The triangular weight

\bar{w}
as in Theorem
5.1.2
is called the persistence
weight induced by the respective refinement.The following consequence
of Theorem
5.1.2
is immediate from the general
constructions in §
2
.

Corollary 5.1.3
.

If a small triangulated category
\mathcal{D}
admits a
TPC refinement
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
, then

\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{D})
is endowed with a family of
fragmentation pseudo-metrics
\bar{d}^{\mathcal{F}}
, defined as in
§
2
, associated to the persistence weight

\bar{w}
induced by the refinement
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
and it
has an H-space structure with respect to the induced topology.

Remark 5.1.4
.

(a) We have seen in §
4.3
, in particular
Definition
4.3.5
, that for a TPC
\mathcal{C}
there
are fragmentation pseudo-metrics
d^{\mathcal{F}}
defined on

\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
. The metrics
\bar{d}^{\mathcal{F}}

associated to the persistence weight
\bar{w}
on
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
,
through the construction in §
2
, are defined
on the same underlying set,
\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
. The
relation between them is

\bar{d}^{\mathcal{F}}\leq d^{\mathcal{F}}

for any family of
objects
\mathcal{F}
. The interest to work with

\bar{d}^{\mathcal{F}}
rather than with
d^{\mathcal{F}}
is that
if
\mathcal{F}
is a family of triangular generators of

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
, and is closed to the action of
T
, then

\bar{d}^{\mathcal{F}}
is finite (see Remark

2.0.5
).

(b) As discussed in Remark
6
(c) (and also in

4.3.6
(d)), in this setting too we may define a simpler
(but generally larger) fragmentation pseudo-metric of the type

\underline{\bar{d}}^{\mathcal{F}}
.

We postpone the proof of Theorem
5.1.2
to §
5.2

and §
5.3
. Here, we pursue with a few remarks
shedding some light on Definition
5.1.1
.

Remark 5.1.5
.

The shifted triangle
(
49
) has the form

\widetilde{\Delta}:A\xrightarrow{u^{\prime}}\Sigma^{-\left\lceil\bar{u}\right\rceil}B\xrightarrow{v^{\prime}}\Sigma^{-\left\lceil\bar{u}\right\rceil-\left\lceil\bar{v}\right\rceil}C\xrightarrow{w^{\prime}}\Sigma^{-\left\lceil\bar{u}\right\rceil-\left\lceil\bar{v}\right\rceil-\left\lceil\bar{w}\right\rceil}TA

where the morphisms
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}
and
w^{\prime}

are:

\displaystyle u^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\eta_{0,-\left\lceil\bar{u}\right\rceil})_{B}\circ\bar{u}

\displaystyle v^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\eta_{0,-\left\lceil\bar{u}\right\rceil-\left\lceil\bar{v}\right\rceil})_{C}\circ\bar{v}\circ(\eta_{-\left\lceil\bar{u}\right\rceil,0})_{B}

\displaystyle w^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\eta_{0,-\left\lceil\bar{u}\right\rceil-\left\lceil\bar{v}\right\rceil-\left\lceil\bar{w}\right\rceil})_{TA}\circ\bar{w}\circ(\eta_{-\left\lceil\bar{u}\right\rceil-\left\lceil\bar{v}\right\rceil,0})_{C}.

In particular,
\left\lceil u^{\prime}\right\rceil=\left\lceil v^{\prime}\right\rceil=\left\lceil w^{\prime}\right\rceil=0
.

Example 5.1.6
.

Assume that

\Delta:A\xrightarrow{\bar{u}}B\xrightarrow{\bar{v}}C\xrightarrow{\bar{w}}\Sigma^{-r}TA
is a strict exact triangle of
weight
r
in
\mathcal{C}
. Consider the following triangle in

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
,

\Delta_{\infty}:A\xrightarrow{u}B\xrightarrow{v}C\xrightarrow{w}TA

where
u=[\bar{u}]
,
v=[\bar{v}]
and

w=[C\xrightarrow{\bar{w}}\Sigma^{-r}TA\xrightarrow{(\eta_{-r,0})_{TA}}TA]
. We claim that

\Delta_{\infty}
is an exact triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
and

w_{\infty}(\Delta_{\infty})\leq r
. Indeed, by construction,

\Delta_{\infty}
is represented by

\bar{\Delta}_{\infty}:A\xrightarrow{\bar{u}}B\xrightarrow{\bar{v}}C\xrightarrow{(\eta_{-r,0})_{A}\circ\bar{w}}TA
and
\left\lceil\bar{u}\right\rceil=\left\lceil\bar{v}\right\rceil=0
,

\left\lceil(\eta_{-r,0})_{A}\circ\bar{w}\right\rceil=r+0=r
. The shifted
triangle

\widetilde{\Delta}_{\infty}=\Sigma^{0,0,0,-r}\bar{\Delta}_{\infty}
obviously equals
\Delta
, the initial strict
exact triangle of weight
r
. Thus,

[\widetilde{\Delta}_{\infty}]=\Delta_{\infty}
and

w(\widetilde{\Delta}_{\infty})=r
. Therefore,
\Delta_{\infty}

is exact in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
and
w_{\infty}(\Delta_{\infty})\leq r
.

Remark 5.1.7
.

A special case of the situation in
Example
5.1.6
is worth emphasizing. Any exact
triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
, induces an exact triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
with
unstable weight equal to
0
. This implies that any morphism

f\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}(A,B)
with
\sigma(f)\leq 0
can be
completed to an exact triangle of unstable weight
0
in

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
. Indeed, we first represent
f
by a morphism

\bar{f}\in{\rm Mor}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)
with
\alpha\leq 0
. If

\alpha\not=0
, we shift
\bar{f}
up using the persistence
structure maps and denote
\bar{f}^{\prime}=i_{\alpha,0}(\bar{f})
. We
obviously have
[\bar{f}^{\prime}]=f
. We then complete
\bar{f}^{\prime}
to an
exact triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
. The image of this triangle in

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
is exact, of unstable weight
0
, and has
f
as the
first morphism in the triple.

Example 5.1.8
.

Consider the strict exact triangle

\Delta:A\to 0\to\Sigma^{-r}TA\xrightarrow{id}\Sigma^{-r}TA

in
\mathcal{C}
, which is of weight
r\geq 0
(see
Remark
4.2.9
(b)). Let
\Delta_{\infty}
be the
following triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}

\Delta_{\infty}:A\to 0\to\Sigma^{-r}TA\xrightarrow{\eta_{-r,0}}TA.

We claim that if

\sigma(id_{A})=0
, then

\bar{w}(\Delta_{\infty})=w_{\infty}(\Delta_{\infty})=r
. Indeed,
assume
w_{\infty}(\Delta_{\infty})=s<r
. Therefore there exists
a strict exact triangle in
\mathcal{C}
of the form

A\to 0\to\Sigma^{-r^{\prime}}TA\xrightarrow{w^{\prime}}\Sigma^{-s}TA,

with
r^{\prime}\geq r
, of weight
s
and such that
\left\lceil w^{\prime}\right\rceil=0
,

[\eta_{-s,0}\circ w^{\prime}\circ\eta_{-r,-r^{\prime}}]=[\eta_{-r,0}]
. Notice

\left\lceil(\eta_{-s,0}\circ w^{\prime}\circ\eta_{-r,-r^{\prime}})\right\rceil=r-r^{\prime}+s
. Thus, as

r^{\prime}\geq r>s
, we deduce
\sigma([\eta_{-r,0}])<r
. By writing

id_{A}=\eta_{0,-r}\circ\eta_{-r,0}
we deduce

\sigma(id_{A})<0
. We conclude
w_{\infty}(\Delta_{\infty})=r
. We
next consider a triangle
\Sigma^{k,0,0,k}\Delta_{\infty}
:

\Sigma^{k}A\to 0\to\Sigma^{-r}TA\to\Sigma^{k}TA

and rewrite it as

\Delta^{\prime}:B\to 0\to\Sigma^{-r-k}TB\to TB

with
B=\Sigma^{k}A
. Suppose that
w_{\infty}(\Delta^{\prime})<r+k
,
then
\sigma(id_{B})<0
by the previous argument. This implies

\sigma(id_{A})<0
and again contradicts our assumption. Thus

w_{\infty}(\Delta^{\prime})=r+k
and
\bar{w}(\Delta_{\infty})=r
.

Example 5.1.9
.

Let
f\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}(A,B)
and
suppose that
\sigma(f)>0
. We will see here that we can extend

f
to an exact triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
, of unstable weight

\leq\sigma(f)+\epsilon
(for any
\epsilon>0
) but, at the same
time, no triangle extending
f
has unstable weight less than

\sigma(f)
. Fix
\sigma(f)<t\leq\sigma(f)+\epsilon
and
let
\bar{f}\in{\rm Mor}^{t}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)
be a representative of
f
.
Consider the composition

f^{\prime}:A\xrightarrow{\bar{f}}B\xrightarrow{(\eta_{0,-t})_{B}}\Sigma^{-t}B
. Then
f^{\prime}\in{\rm Mor}^{0}(A,\Sigma^{-t}B)
. There exists
an exact triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{0}

\Delta:A\xrightarrow{f^{\prime}}\Sigma^{-t}B\xrightarrow{v^{\prime}}C\xrightarrow{w}TA

for some
C\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C}_{0})
. In
particular
w\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(C,TA)
. Next, consider the
following triangle

\Sigma^{0,0,0,-t}\Delta:A\xrightarrow{f^{\prime}}\Sigma^{-t}B\xrightarrow{v^{\prime}}C\xrightarrow{w^{\prime}}\Sigma^{-t}TA,\,\,\,\,\mbox{where $w^{\prime}=\eta_{t}^{TA}\circ w$}.

By
Remark
4.2.9
(b),

\Sigma^{0,0,0,-t}\Delta
is a strict exact triangle in
\mathcal{C}
of
weight
t
. Finally, consider the following triangle in
\mathcal{C}
,
obtained by shifting up the last three terms of
\Sigma^{0,0,0,-t}\Delta
:

\bar{\Delta}:A\xrightarrow{\bar{f}=(\eta_{-t,0})_{B}\circ f^{\prime}}B\xrightarrow{v:=\Sigma^{t}v^{\prime}}\Sigma^{t}C\xrightarrow{w:=\Sigma^{t}w^{\prime}}TA.

Its image in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
is the
triangle

\Delta_{\infty}:A\xrightarrow{f=[\bar{f}]}B\xrightarrow{[v]}\Sigma^{t}C\xrightarrow{[w]}TA

(50)

and is exact. In the terminology of Definition
5.1.1
,
the representative
\tilde{\Delta}
of
\Delta_{\infty}
is the
strict exact triangle
\Sigma^{0,0,0,-t}\Delta
. In particular,

w_{\infty}(\Delta_{\infty})\leq t
. Notice that Definition

5.1.1
immediately implies that any triangle

\Delta^{\prime\prime}:A\xrightarrow{f}B\to D\to TA
in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
satisfies

w_{\infty}(\Delta^{\prime\prime})\geq\sigma(f)
(because, with the notation
of the definition, the weight of the triangle
\tilde{\Delta}
in
that definition is at least
\left\lceil\bar{u}\right\rceil
). At the same time

\bar{w}(\Delta_{\infty})=0
because

\Sigma^{t,0,0,t}(\Delta_{\infty})
has as
representative

\Sigma^{t}\Delta:\Sigma^{t}A\to B\to\Sigma^{t}C\to\Sigma^{t}TA

which is exact in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
.

Example 5.1.10
.

Let
\Delta:A\to B\to C\to TA
be an
exact triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
. It is clear that, in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
and
for
s\leq 0
, the corresponding triangles of the form

\Delta_{s}:\Sigma^{s}A\to B\to C\to\Sigma^{s}TA
(defined using
the (pre)-composition of the maps in
\Delta
with the appropriate

\eta
’s) have the property
\bar{w}(\Delta_{s})=0
. On the other
hand, if
s>0
this is no longer the case, in general. Indeed,
assuming
s>0
and
\bar{w}(\Delta_{s})=0
it follows that for some
possibly even larger
r>0
we have
w_{\infty}(\Delta_{r})=0
. That
means that there is an exact triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
of the form

\Sigma^{r}A\to B\to C\to\Sigma^{r}TA
. This triangle can be
compared to the initial
\Delta
and it follows that

\eta_{r}^{A}:\Sigma^{r}A\to A
is a
0
-isomorphism which implies
that
\sigma(\mathds{1}_{A})\leq-r\leq-s
. Using this remark, we can
revisit the triangle
\Delta_{\infty}
from Example
5.1.9

and deduce (from the rotation property of exact triangles) that if
we consider a triangle
\Delta_{\infty}^{s}
obtained by replacing

\Sigma^{t}C
with
\Sigma^{s}C
with
s>t
in
\Delta_{\infty}
in
equation (
50
) (and using the appropriate
pre/compositions with the
\eta
’s for the maps in the triangle),
then
\bar{w}(\Delta_{\infty}^{s})>0
.

Example 5.1.11
.

Let
\Delta:A\to 0\to\Sigma^{r}TA\to TA
be a triangle in

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
with the last map (the class of)
\eta^{TA}_{r}
and
with
r\geq 0
. Assuming
\sigma(\mathds{1}_{A})=0
, we want to notice that

w_{\infty}(\Delta)=r
and
\bar{w}(\Delta)=0
. The fact that

\bar{w}(\Delta)=0
is obvious because

\Sigma^{r,0,0,r}\Delta:\Sigma^{r}A\to 0\to\Sigma^{r}TA\to\Sigma^{r}TA
is exact in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
. Now assume that

w_{\infty}(\Delta)=s<r
. Then there is a triangle

A\to 0\to\Sigma^{r-s^{\prime}}TA\xrightarrow{u}\Sigma^{-s}TA
which is
strict exact in
\mathcal{C}
and with
s\geq s^{\prime}
. Using now the definition
of strict exact triangles and the existence of the exact triangle

A\to 0\to TA\to TA
in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
, we deduce that there exists an

s
-isomorphism
\phi:TA\to\Sigma^{r-s^{\prime}}TA
with a right inverse

\psi:\Sigma^{s+r-s^{\prime}}TA\to TA
that coincides with
\eta^{TA}
in

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
. As a result,
\phi
coincides with

\eta^{TA}_{0,r-s^{\prime}}
in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
and thus

0=\left\lceil\phi\right\rceil\geq r-s^{\prime}
(because, due to
\sigma(\mathds{1}_{A})=0
, we
have that
\sigma([\eta^{TA}_{0,r-s^{\prime}}])=r-s^{\prime}
). Therefore,

s^{\prime}\geq r
which contradicts
s<r
.

Remark 5.1.12
.

(a) The definition of the exact triangles in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
is
designed precisely to allow for the construction in Example

5.1.9
. This is quite different compared to the case when
the spectral invariant of
f
is non positive (compare with Remark

5.1.7
) because the persistence structure maps can be used
to “shift” up but not down. It also follows from Example

5.1.9
that for
f\in{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}(A,B)
with

\sigma(f)\geq 0
we have:

\sigma(f)=\inf\{\ w_{\infty}(\Delta)\ |\ \Delta:A\xrightarrow{f}B\to C\to TA\ \}~.~

(51)

(b) It is not difficult to see that if

w_{\infty}(\Sigma^{s,0,0,s}\Delta)\leq r
for a triangle
\Delta

in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
and
s\geq 0
, then
w_{\infty}(\Delta)\leq r+s
. Thus, a triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
is exact if and only if its
weight
\bar{w}
is finite.

5.2.
The category
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
is
triangulated

We have noticed before that

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
is additive and the functor
T
has been extended to all
of
\mathcal{C}
(as discussed in Remark
4.1.2
(b)) in a way that
commutes with
\Sigma
and thus induces an automorphism of

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
. We now start to check the axioms of a triangulated
category as listed in Section 10.2 in Weibel
[
46
]

for the class of exact triangles in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
as introduced in
Definition
5.1.1
.

Axiom TR1
. For any
[f]\in{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}(A,B)
, there
exists an exact triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
in the form of

A\xrightarrow{[f]}B\to C\to TA
for some

C\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty})
. This is due to Example
5.1.9
.
For any
A\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty})
, the triangle

A\xrightarrow{\mathds{1}_{A}}A\to 0\to TA
is exact in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
and of
unstable weight
0
. This follows from Remark
5.1.7
. The
verification of this first axiom is completed by the following
statement.

Lemma 5.2.1
.

Given an exact triangle
\Delta
in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
, any other
triangle
\Delta^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
such that

\Delta^{\prime}\simeq\Delta
(in the sense that all the vertical
morphisms are isomorphisms in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
) is also an exact
triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
.

Proof.

Pick a representative
\bar{\Delta}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}
of

\Delta^{\prime}
as well as the associated triangle
\widetilde{\Delta}^{\prime}

in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
, given as in Definition
5.1.1
. By
definition
\Delta
admits a representative
\bar{\Delta}
such that

\widetilde{\Delta}:\Sigma^{0,s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}}\bar{\Delta}
is
strict exact in
\mathcal{C}
. Because
\Delta^{\prime}
and
\Delta
are isomorphic
in
C_{\infty}
, there is some very large
s>0
and a morphism of
triangles
h:\widetilde{\Delta}^{\prime}\to\Sigma^{-s}\widetilde{\Delta}

such that all vertical components of
h
are

s
-isomorphisms. Clearly,
\Sigma^{-s}\widetilde{\Delta}
is strict
exact and, by Remark
4.2.12
, it follows that there are

k,t>0
such that
\Sigma^{0,0,-k,-k-t}\widetilde{\Delta}^{\prime}
is
strict exact. But this means that
\Delta^{\prime}
is exact in

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
.
∎

Axiom TR2
. Suppose
