\Delta:A\xrightarrow{u}B\xrightarrow{v}C\xrightarrow{w}TA
is
an exact triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
. Notice that its first positive
rotation

R(\Delta):B\xrightarrow{v}C\xrightarrow{w}TA\xrightarrow{-Tu}TB
is also an exact triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
. Indeed, by definition,
there exists a triangle

\bar{\Delta}:A\xrightarrow{\bar{u}}B\xrightarrow{\bar{v}}C\xrightarrow{\bar{w}}TA
representing
\Delta
such that the shifted
triangle

\widetilde{\Delta}:A\xrightarrow{u^{\prime}}\Sigma^{-t_{1}}B\xrightarrow{v^{\prime}}\Sigma^{-t_{1}-t_{2}}C\xrightarrow{w^{\prime}}\Sigma^{-r}TA

(where we put
t_{1}=\left\lceil\bar{u}\right\rceil
,
t_{2}=\left\lceil\bar{v}\right\rceil
,

t_{3}=\left\lceil\bar{w}\right\rceil
and
r=t_{1}+t_{2}+t_{3}
) is a strict exact
triangle in
\mathcal{C}
of weight
r
. By Proposition
4.2.5
, the
first positive rotation

R(\widetilde{\Delta}):\Sigma^{-t_{1}}B\xrightarrow{v^{\prime}}\Sigma^{-t_{1}-t_{2}}C\xrightarrow{w^{\prime\prime}}\Sigma^{-r}TA\xrightarrow{u^{\prime\prime}}\Sigma^{-t_{1}-2r}TB

is a strict exact
triangle in
\mathcal{C}
of weight
2r
. We have

\displaystyle u^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=-(\eta_{0,-t_{1}-2r})_{B}\circ\bar{u}\circ(\eta_{-r,0})_{A}

\displaystyle v^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\eta_{0,-t_{1}-t_{2}})_{C}\circ\bar{v}\circ(\eta_{-t_{1},0})_{B}

\displaystyle w^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\simeq_{r}w=(\eta_{0,-r})_{A}\circ\bar{w}\circ(\eta_{-t_{1}-t_{2},0})_{C}.

Consider a new triangle:

\Sigma^{t_{1}}R(\widetilde{\Delta}):B\xrightarrow{\Sigma^{t_{1}}v^{\prime}}\Sigma^{-t_{2}}C\xrightarrow{\Sigma^{t_{1}}w^{\prime\prime}}\Sigma^{-r+t_{1}}TA\xrightarrow{\Sigma^{t_{1}}u^{\prime\prime}}\Sigma^{-2r}TB.

(52)

By Remark
4.2.9
(1),

\Sigma^{t_{1}}R(\widetilde{\Delta})
is also a strict exact triangle
in
\mathcal{C}
of weight
2r
. By shifting up this triangle we get to

B\to C\to TA\to TB
that represents
R(\Delta)
and thus

w_{\infty}(R(\Delta))\leq 2r
. In a similar way, using Remark

4.2.6
, it follows that the negative rotation of
\Delta
,

R^{-1}(\Delta)
, is exact.

Axiom TR3
. Completing a commutative square into a morphism of
triangles follows from Proposition
4.2.10
. Namely, consider
the
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
diagram:

\textstyle{\Delta:}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\prime}:}
\textstyle{A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TA^{\prime}}

with rows exact triangles and with the left square commutative. We
need to construct
c:C\to C^{\prime}
making commutative the last two squares.
We consider the associated strict exact triangles
\widetilde{\Delta}

and
\widetilde{\Delta}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}
, as in Definition

5.1.1
. We use Proposition
4.2.10
to obtain a
morphism of triangles of the form

h:\widetilde{\Delta}\to\Sigma^{-k,-k,-k,-k-s}\Delta^{\prime}
for

k,s\in\mathbb{R}
sufficiently big,
h=(\bar{a},\bar{b},\bar{c},\bar{a}^{\prime})
. In particular, the morphism
\bar{c}
has the form

\bar{c}:C\to\Sigma^{-m}C^{\prime}
for a certain large
m\in\mathbb{R}
. We
consider the composition
c^{\prime}=(\eta_{-m,0})_{C^{\prime}}\circ\bar{c}
(where we
recall
(\eta_{-m,0})_{C^{\prime}}:\Sigma^{-m}C^{\prime}\to C^{\prime}
) and we put

c=[c^{\prime}]
. The needed commutativities follow from the ones in
Proposition
4.2.10
.

Remark 5.2.2

(Five Lemma)
.

A further useful consequence of this argument is that, if the
morphisms
a
and
b
are isomorphisms (in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
), then so
is
c
. Indeed, if
a
and
b
are isomorphisms, then

\bar{a},\bar{b}
are
t
-isomorphisms for
t
large and, by
Corollary
4.1.8
,
\bar{c}
is a
2t
-isomorphism which implies
that
c
is an isomorphism. Note that usually the five lemma
comes as a corollary of a triangulated structure on a category,
however here we will use it in order to show that
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
is
triangulated.

Axiom TR4
. Given exact triangles

\Delta_{1}:U\to V\to Z\to TU
and

\Delta_{2}:U\to V^{\prime}\to Z^{\prime}\to TU
,
\Delta_{3}:V\to V^{\prime}\to W\to TV

in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
commuting as in the diagram below, there exists an
exact triangle
\Delta_{4}:Z\to Z^{\prime}\to W\to TZ
in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}

completing the diagram (with the bottom right-most square
anti-commutative).

\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TU\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TU\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TU\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TV\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TZ\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T^{2}U}

(53)

We will give the proof in the next section where we will also show
that the weight
\bar{w}
satisfies the weighted octahedral axiom from
Definition
2.0.1
(i).

5.3.
An exotic triangular weight on

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}

If an additive category
\mathcal{D}
together with an automorphism

T
and a class of distinguished triangles satisfies the Axioms TR1,
TR2, TR3 and the Five Lemma (in the form in Remark
5.2.2
)
and also the property in Definition
2.0.1
(i),
without reference to the weight
w
and without the inequality
(
1
), then it satisfies TR4 and thus it is
triangulated. This is a simple exercise in manipulating exact
triangles that we leave to the reader. We have already seen in
§
5.2
that
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
together with the
functor
T
induced from
\mathcal{C}
and the class of exact triangles from
Definition
5.1.1
satisfies TR1, TR2, TR3 and the Five
Lemma. Thus, if we show that the weighted octahedral axiom is
satisfied by
\bar{w}
in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
(relative to our class of
exact triangles) we deduce that
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
is
triangulated. Moreover, if
\bar{w}
also satisfies the normalization
in Definition
2.0.1
(ii), then
\bar{w}
is a
triangular weight on
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
. Finally, to complete the claim in
Theorem
5.1.2
we also need to show that
\bar{w}
is
subadditive and
\bar{w}_{0}=0
.

We start below with the weighted octahedral axiom and will end with
the normalization property.

Lemma 5.3.1
.

The class of exact triangles in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
and the weight

\bar{w}
as defined in Definition
5.1.1
satisfies the
weighted octahedral axiom from Definition
2.0.1

(i).

Proof.

Recall that given the exact triangles

\Delta_{1}:A\to B\to C\to TA
and
\Delta_{2}:C\to D\to E\to TC

in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
we need to show that there are exact triangles:

\Delta_{3}:B\to D\to F\to TB
and

\Delta_{4}:TA\to F\to E\to T^{2}A
making the diagram below
commute, except for the right-most bottom square that anti-commutes,

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TB\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TC\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T^{2}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T^{2}A}

and such that

\bar{w}(\Delta_{3})+\bar{w}(\Delta_{4})\leq\bar{w}(\Delta_{1})+\bar{w}(\Delta_{2})
.

In
\mathcal{C}
, there are triangles
\bar{\Delta}_{1}:A\to B\to C\to TE

with non-negative morphisms shifts
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}
and

\bar{\Delta}_{2}:C\to D\to E\to TC
with non-negative morphisms
shifts
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}
that represent
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}

respectively and such that the associated triangles

\widetilde{\Delta}_{1}:A\to\Sigma^{-t_{1}}B\to\Sigma^{-t_{1}-t_{2}}C\to\Sigma^{-r}TA\,\,\,\,\,\mbox{where $r=t_{1}+t_{2}+t_{3}$}

and

\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}:C\to\Sigma^{-k_{1}}D\to\Sigma^{-k_{1}-k_{2}}E\to\Sigma^{-s}TC\,\,\,\,\,\mbox{where
$s=k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}$}

are strict exact triangles in
\mathcal{C}
of
weight
r
and
s
, respectively. Consider

\Sigma^{-t_{1}-t_{2}}\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}
,

\Sigma^{-t_{1}-t_{2}}\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}:\Sigma^{-t_{1}-t_{2}}C\to\Sigma^{-k_{1}-t_{1}-t_{2}}D\to\Sigma^{-k_{1}-k_{2}-t_{1}-t_{2}}E\to\Sigma^{-s-t_{1}-t_{2}}TC.

The weighted octahedral property for strict exact triangles in
\mathcal{C}

in Proposition
4.2.8
implies that we can construct the
following commutative diagram in
\mathcal{C}
(with the bottom right square
which is
r
-anti-commutative).

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-t_{1}}B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-k_{1}-t_{1}-t_{2}}D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-t_{1}}TB\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-t_{1}-t_{2}}C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-k_{1}-t_{1}-t_{2}}D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-k_{1}-k_{2}-t_{1}-t_{2}}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-t_{1}-t_{2}-s}TC\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-r}TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-r-s}T^{2}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-r-s}T^{2}A}

(54)

Here the triangle

\Delta_{3}^{\prime}:\Sigma^{-t_{1}}B\to\Sigma^{-k_{1}-t_{1}-t_{2}}D\to D^{\prime}\to\Sigma^{-t_{1}}TB
is an exact triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
. The triangle

\Delta_{3}^{\prime\prime}:\Sigma^{k_{1}+t_{2}}B\to D\to\Sigma^{k_{1}+t_{1}+t_{2}}D^{\prime}\to\Sigma^{k_{1}+t_{2}}TB
obtained by
shifting up
\Delta_{3}^{\prime}
by
k_{1}+t_{1}+t_{2}
is also exact in

\mathcal{C}_{0}
. Let
[\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{3}]
be the image of this triangle in

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
. We put
F=\Sigma^{k_{1}+t_{1}+t_{2}}D^{\prime}
and take

\Delta_{3}
to be the triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}

\Delta_{3}:B\to D\to F\to TB

obtained by applying

\Sigma^{-k_{1}-t_{2},0,0,-k_{1}-t_{2}}
to
[\Delta_{3}^{\prime\prime}]
. We
obviously have
w_{\infty}([\Delta_{3}^{\prime\prime}])=0
and thus

\bar{w}(\Delta_{3})=0
.

The next step is to identify the triangle
\Delta_{4}
. Proposition

4.2.8
implies that the third column in
(
54
):

\Delta_{4}^{\prime}:TA\to\Sigma^{-k_{1}-t_{1}-t_{2}}F\to\Sigma^{-k_{1}-k_{2}-t_{1}-t_{2}}E\to\Sigma^{-r-s}T^{2}A

is a
strict exact triangle in
\mathcal{C}
. We let
[\Delta^{\prime}_{4}]
be the image
of this triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
and let
\Delta_{4}
be given by
applying
\Sigma^{0,k_{1}+t_{1}+t_{2},k_{1}+k_{2}+t_{1}+t_{2},r+s}

to
[\Delta^{\prime}_{4}]
:

\Delta_{4}:TA\to F\to E\to T^{2}A~.~

We deduce from Definition
5.1.1
that

w_{\infty}(\Delta_{4})\leq r+s
and thus also

\bar{w}(\Delta_{4})\leq r+s
.

The commutativity required in the statement follows from that
provided by Proposition
4.2.8
for (
54
).
∎

Remark 5.3.2
.

For the triangle
\Delta_{3}

produced in this proof it is easy to see that

w_{\infty}(\Delta_{3})\leq k_{1}+t_{2}
. Therefore we have:

w_{\infty}(\Delta_{3})+w_{\infty}(\Delta_{4})\leq(k_{1}+t_{2})+(r+s)\leq 2(r+s)=2(w_{\infty}(\Delta_{1})+w_{\infty}(\Delta_{2})).

Thus
the weight
w_{\infty}
satisfies a weak form of the weighted
octahedral axiom.

The next step in proving Theorem
5.1.2
is to show the
normalization property in Definition
2.0.1
(ii). This
property is satisfied with the constant
\bar{w}_{0}=0
. Indeed, any
triangle
0\to X\to X\to 0
and all its rotations are exact in

\mathcal{C}_{0}
and thus they are of unstable weight equal to
0
. The last
verification needed is to see that, if
B=0
in the diagram of the
weighted octahedral axiom, then the triangle
\Delta_{3}
-
constructed in the proof of the Lemma - can be of the form:

\Delta_{3}:0\to D\to D\to 0
. This is trivially satisfied in our
construction because if
B=0
we may take
t_{1}=t_{2}=0
and the
triangle

\Delta^{\prime}_{3}:0\to\Sigma^{-k_{1}}D\xrightarrow{\mathds{1}}\Sigma^{-k_{1}}D\to 0
.

Finally, to finish the proof of Theorem
5.1.2
we need to
show that
\bar{w}
is sub-additive. Thus, assuming that

\Delta:A\to B\to C\to TA
is exact in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
and
X

is an object in
\mathcal{C}
, then

\bar{w}(X\oplus\Delta)\leq\bar{w}(\Delta)
where the triangle

X\oplus\Delta
has the form

X\oplus\Delta:A\to X\oplus B\to X\oplus C\to TA
. We consider the
strict exact triangle in
\mathcal{C}

\widetilde{\Delta}:A\to\Sigma^{-s_{1}}B\to\Sigma^{-s_{1}-s_{2}}C\to\Sigma^{-s_{1}-s_{2}-s_{3}}TA

associated to
\Delta
as in Definition
5.1.1
with

s_{i}\geq 0
,
1\leq i\leq 3
. Consider the triangle

\Delta^{\prime}:0\to\Sigma^{-s_{1}}X\xrightarrow{\eta_{s_{2}}^{X}}\Sigma^{-s_{1}-s_{2}}X\to 0~.~

This triangle is obtained from the
exact triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
,

0\to\Sigma^{-s_{1}}X\xrightarrow{\mathds{1}}\Sigma^{-s_{1}}X\to 0
by
applying
\Sigma^{0,0,-s_{2},-s_{2}}
and it is of strict weight

\leq s_{2}
. By Lemma
4.3.8
we have

w(\Delta^{\prime}\oplus\widetilde{\Delta})\leq w(\widetilde{\Delta})
. We
now notice that
\Delta^{\prime}\oplus\widetilde{\Delta}
can be viewed as
obtained from
X\oplus\Delta
by applying

\Sigma^{0,-s_{1},-s_{1}-s_{2},-s_{1}-s_{2}-s_{3}}
and thus

w_{\infty}(X\oplus\Delta)\leq w(\widetilde{\Delta})
which implies
the claim. The proof for
\Delta\oplus X
is similar.

5.4.
Fragmentation pseudo-metrics and their
non-degeneracy.

The purpose of this section is to rapidly review some of the
properties of the persistence fragmentation pseudo-metrics. We start
by recalling the main definitions, we then discuss some algebraic
properties and conclude with a discussion of non-degeneracy.

5.4.1.
Persistence fragmentation pseudo-metrics, review of
main definitions

Assume that
\mathcal{C}
is a TPC. We have defined in §
4.3
and §
5.1
three types of
similarly defined measurements on the objects of
\mathcal{C}
that, after
symmetrization, define fragmentation pseudo-metrics on

\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
. In §
4.3
this construction
uses directly the weight
w
of the strict exact triangles in
\mathcal{C}

(making use of Proposition
4.2.8
) and it gives rise to
pseudo-metrics
d^{\mathcal{F}}
as in Definition
4.3.5

as well as a simplified version
\underline{d}^{\mathcal{F}}

mentioned in Remark
4.3.6
(d).

In §
5.1
the aim is to consider weights on the
triangles of the category
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
. This category is
triangulated - this is the main part of Theorem
5.1.2
-
and its exact triangles are endowed with an unstable weight

w_{\infty}
as well as with a (smaller) stable weight
\overline{w}
,
as given by Definition
5.1.1
. Working in the category

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
has a significant advantage compared to the category

\mathcal{C}
because, by contrast to
\mathcal{C}
, in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
any morphism can
be completed to an exact triangle of finite weight. Moreover, in

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
exact triangles have the standard form expected in a
triangulated category and do not involve shifts. As a consequence,
we will focus here on the fragmentation pseudo-metrics defined using

w_{\infty}
and, mainly,
\overline{w}
.

An important remark at this point is that the unstable weight

w_{\infty}
does not satisfy the weighted octahedral axiom (but only
its weak form as discussed in Remark
5.3.2
) and thus only
the pseudo-metrics of the form
\underline{\bar{d}}^{\mathcal{F}}
can
be defined using it. By contrast,
\overline{w}
does satisfy the
weighted octahedral axiom and there is a pseudo-metric

\bar{d}^{\mathcal{F}}
associated to it through the construction in
§
2
. Both
\overline{w}
and
w_{\infty}
are
subadditive and satisfy the normalization property in Definition

2.0.1
with
w_{0}=0
.

To eliminate possible ambiguities we recall the definitions of the two
relevant pseudo-metrics here. Both of them are based on considering a
sequence of exact triangles in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
as below:

\textstyle{Y_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ldots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y_{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ldots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y_{n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Delta_{1}}
\textstyle{\Delta_{i+1}}
\textstyle{\Delta_{n}}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

(55)

where the dotted arrows represent maps
Y_{i}\to TX_{i}
. We fix a
family of objects
\mathcal{F}
in
\mathcal{C}
with
0\in\mathcal{F}
and
now:

\underline{\bar{\delta}}^{\mathcal{F}}(X,X^{\prime})=\inf\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{\infty}(\Delta_{i})\,\Bigg|\,\begin{array}[]{ll}\mbox{$\Delta_{i}$ are successive exact triangles as in (\ref{eq:iterated-tr2})}\\
\mbox{with $Y_{0}=X^{\prime}$,
$X=Y_{n}$ and $X_{i}\in\mathcal{F}$, $n\in\mathbb{N}$}\end{array}\right\}

(56)

\bar{\delta}^{\mathcal{F}}(X,X^{\prime})=\inf\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{w}(\Delta_{i})\,\Bigg|\,\begin{array}[]{ll}\mbox{$\Delta_{i}$ are as in (\ref{eq:iterated-tr2}) with $Y_{0}=0$, $X=Y_{n}$, $X_{i}\in\mathcal{F}$, }\\
\mbox{$n\in\mathbb{N}$ except for some $j$ such that $X_{j}=T^{-1}X^{\prime}$}\end{array}\right\}~.~

(57)

Finally, the pseudo-metrics
\underline{\bar{d}}^{\mathcal{F}}
and
\bar{d}^{\mathcal{F}}
are obtained
by symmetrizing
\underline{\bar{\delta}}^{\mathcal{F}}
and
\bar{\delta}^{\mathcal{F}}
, respectively:

\underline{\bar{d}}^{\mathcal{F}}(X,X^{\prime})=\max\{\underline{\bar{\delta}}^{\mathcal{F}}(X,X^{\prime}),\underline{\bar{\delta}}^{\mathcal{F}}(X^{\prime},X)\},\ \ \bar{d}^{\mathcal{F}}(X,X^{\prime})=\max\{\bar{\delta}^{\mathcal{F}}(X,X^{\prime}),\bar{\delta}^{\mathcal{F}}(X^{\prime},X)\}~.~

5.4.2.
Algebraic properties.

There are many fragmentation pseudo-metrics of persistence type
associated to the same weight, depending on the choices of family

\mathcal{F}
. In fact, the choices available are even more abundant
for the following two reasons:

i.

triangular weights themselves can be mixed. For instance, if

\mathcal{C}
is a TPC, there is a triangular weight of the form

\overline{w}^{+}=\overline{w}+w_{fl}
that is defined on

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
(where
w_{fl}
is the flat weight defined in §
2
).

ii.

fragmentation metrics themselves can also be mixed. If

d^{\mathcal{F}}
and
d^{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
are two fragmentation
pseudo-metrics (whether defined with respect to the same weight or
not), then the following expressions

\alpha~d^{\mathcal{F}}+\beta~d^{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
with

\alpha,\beta\geq 0
as well as

\max\{d^{\mathcal{F}},d^{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}\}
are also
pseudo-metrics.

In essence, while it is not easy to produce interesting sub-additive
triangular weights on a triangulated category, once such a weight is
constructed - as in the case of the persistence weight
\overline{w}

defined on
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
(where
\mathcal{C}
is a TPC) - one can associate to
it a large class of pseudo-metrics, either by combining the weight
with the flat one and/or by “mixing” the pseudo-metrics associated
to different families
\mathcal{F}
. Another useful (and obvious)
property relating the pseudo-metrics
d^{\mathcal{F}}
and

d^{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
associated to the same triangular weight is that:

iii.

If
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
, then

d^{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}\leq d^{\mathcal{F}}
.

5.4.3.
Vanishing and non-degeneracy of fragmentation
metrics.

We fix here a TPC denoted by
\mathcal{C}
together with the associated weights
and pseudo-metrics, as above. We will assume that
0\in\mathcal{F}
. We will denote by
\bar{d}^{\emptyset}
the
pseudo-metric associated to the family consisting of only the element

0
. In view of point iii. above
\bar{d}^{\emptyset}
is an upper
bound for all the pseudo-metrics
\bar{d}^{\mathcal{F}}
.

It is obvious, as noticed in Remark
4.3.6
, that, in general

\bar{d}^{\mathcal{F}}
is degenerate and, for instance, if

\mathcal{F}=\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
then

\bar{d}^{\mathcal{F}}\equiv 0
. The rest of Remark
4.3.6

also continues to apply to
\bar{d}^{\mathcal{F}}
. We list below some
other easily proven properties. We assume for all the objects
X

involved here that
\sigma(\mathds{1}_{X})=0
and we will use the calculations
in Examples
5.1.8
,
5.1.9
,
5.1.10
and

5.1.11
. Recall the notion of
r
-isomorphism from Definition

4.1.3
, in particular, this is a morphism in

\mathcal{C}_{0}
. A
0
-isomorphism is simply an isomorphism in the
category
\mathcal{C}_{0}
and is denoted by
\equiv
.

i.

If
X\equiv X^{\prime}
, then
\bar{d}^{\mathcal{F}}(X,X^{\prime})=0=\underline{\bar{d}}^{\mathcal{F}}(X,X^{\prime})
for any family

\mathcal{F}
.

ii.

If
\bar{\delta}^{\emptyset}(X,X^{\prime})=s
, then

s=\inf\{r\in\mathbb{R}\ |\ \exists\ \phi:\Sigma^{k}X^{\prime}\to X\ \ \mbox{$r$-isomorphism},\ k\geq 0\}
.

iii.

If
\underline{\bar{\delta}}^{\emptyset}(X,X^{\prime})=s
, then

s=\inf\{r\in\mathbb{R}\ |\ \exists\ \phi:\Sigma^{k}X^{\prime}\to X\ \ \mbox{$r$-isomorphism},\ r\geq k\geq 0\}
.

iv.

We have
\bar{d}^{\emptyset}(X,\Sigma^{r}X)=r
for any

r\in\mathbb{R}
(this follows from Examples
5.1.8
and

5.1.11
).

Thus
\bar{d}^{\emptyset}
is finite for objects that are isomorphic
in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
and
\bar{d}^{\emptyset}(X,X^{\prime})
is the optimal
upper-bound
r
such there are
s
-isomorphisms in
\mathcal{C}
with

s\leq r
, from some positive shift of
X
to
X^{\prime}
and, similarly,
from some positive shift of
X^{\prime}
to
X
. To some extent,
\bar{d}^{\emptyset}
can be viewed as an abstract analogue of the interleaving distance in the persistence module theory (cf. Section 1.3 in
[
35
]
and Proposition
6.2.2
below). For

\underline{\bar{d}}^{\emptyset}
there is an additional constraint
that the respective shifts should be also bounded by
r
. As a consequence:

v.

if
\bar{d}^{\emptyset}(X,X^{\prime})=0
, then
X
and
X^{\prime}
are

0
-isomorphic up to shift. Moreover, if
X
and
X^{\prime}
are not

0
-isomorphic, they are both periodic in the sense that there exist

k
and
k^{\prime}
(not both null) and
0
-isomorphisms

\Sigma^{k}X\to X
,
\Sigma^{k^{\prime}}X^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
.

vi.

if
\underline{\bar{d}}^{\emptyset}(X,X^{\prime})=0
, then
X\equiv X^{\prime}
.

In summary, this means that the best we can expect from the
fragmentation pseudo-metrics is that they should be non-degenerate on the
space of
0
-isomorphism types. From now on, we will say that a
fragmentation pseudo-metric is non-degenerate if this is the
case. Assuming no periodic objects exist, the metric

\bar{d}^{\emptyset}
is non-degenerate in this sense. However, the
distance it measures for two objects that are not isomorphic in

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
is infinite. On the other hand, a metric such as

\bar{d}^{\mathcal{F}}
(as well as

\underline{\bar{d}}^{\mathcal{F}}
) where
\mathcal{F}
is a family
of triangular generators of
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
is finite but is in general
degenerate.

The last point we want to raise in this section is that mixing
fragmentation pseudo-metrics can sometimes produce non-degenerate
ones. We will see an example of this sort in the symplectic section §
6.5
,
but we end here by describing a more general, abstract argument. Fix
two families
\mathcal{F}_{i}
,
i=1,2
of generators of

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
. Consider the mixed pseudo-metric defined by

\bar{d}^{\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2}}=\max\{\bar{d}^{\mathcal{F}_{1}},\bar{d}^{\mathcal{F}_{2}}\}~.~

(58)

The idea is that if these two families are “separated” in a strong
sense, then the mixed metric is non-degenerate. For instance, denote
by
\mathcal{F}_{i}^{\Delta}
the subcategory of
\mathcal{C}_{0}
that is
generated by
\mathcal{F}_{i}
. Now assume that

\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{\Delta})\cap\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{F}_{2}^{\Delta})=\{0\}
(this is of course quite
restrictive). We now claim that

\underline{\bar{d}}^{\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2}}
is
non-degenerate and that
\bar{d}^{\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2}}

satisfies a weaker non-degeneracy conditions which is that

\bar{d}^{\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2}}(X,0)=0
if and only if

X\equiv 0
. This latter fact follows immediately by noticing that

\bar{d}^{\mathcal{F}_{i}}(X,0)=0
means that

X\in\mathcal{F}_{i}^{\Delta}
and we leave the former as an exercise.

6.
Examples

6.1.
Filtered dg-categories

The key property of dg-categories, introduced in
[
9
]
(see also

[
20
]
), is that they admit natural, pre-triangulated
closures. The
0
-cohomological category of this closure is
triangulated. We will see here that there is a natural notion of
filtered dg-categories. Such a category also admits a
pre-triangulated closure, defined using filtered twisted complexes,
following closely
[
9
]
. Its
0
-cohomological category is a
triangulated persistence category.

6.1.1.
Basic definitions

Following standard convention we will work in a co-homological setting
and we keep all the sign conventions as in
[
9
]
. For our
purposes it is convenient to view a filtered cochain complex over the
field
\mathbf{k}
as a triple
(X,\partial,\ell)
consisting of a cochain
complex
(X,\partial)
and a filtration function

\ell:X\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
such that for any
a,b\in X
and

\lambda\in\mathbf{k}\backslash\{0\}
,

\ell(\lambda a+b)\leq\max\{\ell(a),\ell(b)\}
,

\ell(a)=-\infty
if and only if
a=0
, and

\ell(\partial a)\leq\ell(a)
. We denote

X^{\leq r}=\{x\in X\,|\,\ell(x)\leq r\}\subset X
the
filtration induced on
X
by the filtration function
l
. Clearly,

X^{\leq r}
is again a filtered cochain complex. The family

\{X^{\leq r}\}
determines the function
\ell
. The cohomology of a
filtered cochain complex is a persistence module:

V^{r}(X)=H(X^{\leq r};\mathbf{k})
whose structural maps
i_{r,s}
are
induced by the inclusions

\iota_{r,s}:X^{\leq r}\hookrightarrow X^{\leq s}
,
r\leq s
. We have
omitted here the grading, as is customary. In case it needs to be
indicated we write, for instance,

[V^{r}(X)]^{i}=H^{i}(X^{\leq r};\mathbf{k})
. We denote this (graded)
persistence module by
\mathbb{V}(X)
,

\mathbb{V}(X)=(V^{r}(X),i_{r,s})~.~

(59)

Given two filtered cochain complexes
X=(X,\partial^{X},\ell_{X})
and

Y=(Y,\partial^{Y},\ell_{Y})
, their tensor product is a filtered
cochain complex
(X\otimes Y,\partial^{\otimes},\ell_{\otimes})

given by
(X\otimes Y)_{k}=\bigoplus_{i+j=k}(X_{i}\otimes Y_{j})
and

\partial^{\otimes}(x\otimes y)=\partial^{X}(x)\otimes y+(-1)^{|x|}x\otimes\partial^{Y}(y)\ ,\ \ell_{\otimes}(a\otimes b)=\ell_{X}(a)+\ell_{Y}(b)~.~

(60)

If
(X,\ell_{X})
and
(Y,\ell_{Y})
are filtered vector spaces, we call a linear map
\phi:X\to Y

r
-filtered if
\ell_{Y}(\phi(x))\leq\ell_{X}(x)+r
for all
x\in X
. A
0
-filtered map is sometimes
called (for brevity) filtered. For more background on this formalism, see
[
44
]
.

The next definition is an obvious analogue of the notion of dg-category in
[
9
]
§1.

Definition 6.1.1
.

A
filtered dg-category
is a
preadditive category
\mathcal{A}
where

(i)

for any
A,B\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{A})
the hom-set

{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,B)
is a filtered cochain complex with
filtrations denoted by
{\rm Hom}_{A}^{\leq r}(A,B)
such that for
each identity element we have
\ell(\mathds{1}_{A})=0
and
\mathds{1}_{A}
is
closed.

(ii)

the composition is a filtered chain map:

{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(B,C)\otimes{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,B)\xrightarrow{\circ}{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,C)~;~

(iii)

for any inclusions
\iota^{AB}_{r,r^{\prime}}
and

\iota^{BC}_{s,s^{\prime}}
, the composition morphism satisfies the
compatibility condition

\iota^{BC}_{s,s^{\prime}}(g)\circ\iota^{AB}_{r,r^{\prime}}(f)=\iota^{AC}_{r+s,r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}}(g\circ f)
for any

f\in{\rm Hom}^{\leq r}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,B)
and

g\in{\rm Hom}^{\leq s}_{\mathcal{A}}(B,C)
.

Remark 6.1.2
.

A filtered dg-category is trivially a persistence category by
forgetting the boundary maps on each
{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,B)
. Explicitly, for any
A,B\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{A})
,
define
E_{AB}:(\mathbb{R},\leq)\to{\rm Vect}_{\mathbf{k}}
by

E_{AB}(r)={\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\leq r}(A,B)
and

E_{AB}(i_{r,s})=\iota_{r,s}:{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\leq r}(A,B)\to{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\leq s}(A,B)
.

The (co)homology category of a filtered dg-category
\mathcal{A}
, denoted by

{\rm H}(\mathcal{A})
, is a category with

{\rm Obj}({\rm H}(\mathcal{A}))={\rm Obj}(\mathcal{A})

and, for any

A,B\in{\rm Obj}({\rm H}(\mathcal{A}))
,

{\rm Hom}_{{\rm H}(\mathcal{A})}(A,B):=\mathbb{V}({\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,B))=\Bigl(\bigl\{H^{*}({\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\leq r}(A,B))\bigr\}_{r\in\mathbb{R}},\{i_{r,s}\}_{r\leq s}\Bigr),

is the
persistence module as described in (
59
). It is
immediate to see that for any filtered dg-category
\mathcal{A}
, its (co)homology
category
{\rm H}(\mathcal{A})
is a (graded) persistence category.

6.1.2.
Twisted complexes

It is easy to construct a formal shift-completion
of a dg-category.

Definition 6.1.3
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a filtered dg-category. The
shift completion

\Sigma\mathcal{A}
of
\mathcal{A}

is a filtered dg-category such that:

(i)

The objects of
\Sigma\mathcal{A}
are

{\rm Obj}(\Sigma\mathcal{A})=\left\{\Sigma^{r}A[d]\,|\,A\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{A}),\,r\in\mathbb{R}\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,d\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}

(61)

such that
\Sigma^{0}A=A
,
\Sigma^{s}(\Sigma^{r}A)=\Sigma^{r+s}A
,
A[0]=A
,
(A[d_{1}])[d_{2}]=A[d_{1}+d_{2}]
,
(\Sigma^{r}A)[d]=\Sigma^{r}(A[d])
, for any
r,s\in\mathbb{R}
and
d_{1},d_{2},d\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(ii)

For any
\Sigma^{r}A[d_{A}],\Sigma^{s}B[d_{B}]\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{A})
, the hom-set
{\rm Hom}(\Sigma^{r}A[d_{A}],\Sigma^{s}B[d_{B}])
is a filtered cochain complex with the same underlying cochain complex of
{\rm Hom}(A,B)
but with degree shifted by
d_{B}-d_{A}
and filtration function
\ell_{\Sigma^{r}A[d_{A}]\,\Sigma^{s}B[d_{B}]}=\ell_{AB}+s-r
.

Remark 6.1.4
.

It is immediate to check that
\Sigma\mathcal{A}
as given in Definition
6.1.3
is still a filtered dg-category.

The category
\Sigma\mathcal{A}
carries a natural functor
\Sigma:(\mathbb{R},+)\to\mathcal{P}{\rm End}(\Sigma\mathcal{A})
defined on objects by
\Sigma^{r}(A)=\Sigma^{r}A
and with an obvious definition on morphisms such that
\Sigma^{r}
is filtration preserving and for
r\leq s
, the natural transformations
\eta_{r,s}:\Sigma^{r}\to\Sigma^{s}
are such that
(\eta_{r,s})_{A}:\Sigma^{r}A\to\Sigma^{s}A
is
induced by the identity map
id_{A}
for each
A\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{A})
.
In this context we have a natural definition of (one-sided) twisted complexes obtained by adjusting to the filtered case the Definition 1 in §4
[
9
]
.

Definition 6.1.5
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a filtered dg-category. A filtered (one-sided) twisted complex of
\Sigma\mathcal{A}
is a pair
A=\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\Sigma^{r_{i}}A_{i}[d_{i}],q=(q_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\right)
such that the following conditions hold.

(i)

\Sigma^{r_{i}}A_{i}[d_{i}]\in{\rm Obj}(\Sigma\mathcal{A})
, where
r_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
and
d_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(ii)

q_{ij}\in{\rm Hom}_{\Sigma\mathcal{A}}(\Sigma^{r_{j}}A_{j}[d_{j}],\Sigma^{r_{i}}A_{i}[d_{i}])
is of degree
1
, and
q_{ij}=0
for
i\geq j
.

(iii)

d_{\rm Hom}q_{ij}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}q_{ik}\circ q_{kj}=0
.

(iv)

For any
q_{ij}
,
\ell_{\Sigma^{r_{j}}A_{j}[d_{j}]\Sigma^{r_{i}}A_{i}[d_{i}]}(q_{ij})\leq 0
.

Remark 6.1.6
.

We will mostly work with
filtered
one-sided twisted complexes as defined above but, more generally, the pair
A=\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}[r_{i}],q=(q_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\right)
subject only to (i),(ii), (iii) is called a one-sided twisted complex.

It is easy to see that there are at least as many filtered one-sided twisted complexes as one-sided twisted complexes as it follows from the statement below whose proof we leave to the reader.

Lemma 6.1.7
.

Given a twisted complex
\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}[d_{i}],q=(q_{ij})\right)
, there exist
(r_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
such that condition (iv) in Definition
6.1.5
is satisfied for the filtration shifted twisted complex
\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\Sigma^{r_{i}}A_{i}[d_{i}],q=(q_{ij})\right)
.

6.1.3.
Pre-triangulated completion.

We will see next that the filtered twisted complexes over
\mathcal{A}
form a category that provides a (pre-)triangulated
closure of
\mathcal{A}
. The
0
-cohomology category of this completion is a triangulated persistence category.

Definition 6.1.8
.

Given a filtered dg-category
\mathcal{A}
,
define its
filtered pre-triangulated completion
, denoted by

Tw(\mathcal{A})
, to be a category with the following properties.

(i)

Its objects are,

{\rm Obj}(Tw(\mathcal{A})):=\{\mbox{filtered one-sided twisted
complex of $\Sigma\mathcal{A}$}\}.

(ii)

For
A=\left(\bigoplus\Sigma^{r_{j}}A_{j}[d_{j}],q\right)

and
A^{\prime}=\left(\bigoplus\Sigma^{r^{\prime}_{i}}A^{\prime}_{i}[d^{\prime}_{i}],q^{\prime}\right)
in

{\rm Obj}(Tw(\mathcal{A}))
, a morphism
f\in{\rm Hom}_{Tw(\mathcal{A})}(A,A^{\prime})

is a matrix of morphisms in
\mathcal{A}
denoted by

f=(f_{ij}):A\to A^{\prime}
, where

f_{ij}\in{\rm Hom}_{\Sigma\mathcal{A}}\left(\Sigma^{r_{j}}A_{j}[d_{j}],\Sigma^{r^{\prime}_{i}}A^{\prime}_{i}[d^{\prime}_{i}]\right).

(iii)

The hom-differential is defined as follows. For any

f\in{\rm Hom}_{Tw(\mathcal{A})}(A,A^{\prime})
as in (ii) above, define

d_{Tw\mathcal{A}}(f):=(d_{\rm Hom}f_{ij})+q^{\prime}f-(-1)^{l}fq

(62)

where

{\rm deg}(f_{ij})=l
and the right-hand side is written
in matrix form. The composition
f^{\prime}\circ f
is given by
the matrix multiplication.

Lemma 6.1.9
.

Given a filtered dg-category

\mathcal{A}
, its filtered pre-triangulated completion
Tw(\mathcal{A})
is a filtered
dg-category.

Proof.

The main step is to notice that there exists a
filtration function on
{\rm Hom}_{Tw(\mathcal{A})}(A,A^{\prime})
for any

A,A^{\prime}\in{\rm Obj}(Tw(\mathcal{A}))
. For any

f=(f_{ij})\in{\rm Hom}_{Tw(\mathcal{A})}(A,A^{\prime})
,
set

\ell_{AA^{\prime}}(f)=\max_{i,j}\left\{\ell_{\Sigma^{r_{j}}A_{j}[d_{j}]\,\Sigma^{r^{\prime}_{i}}A^{\prime}_{i}[d^{\prime}_{i}]}(f_{ij})\right\}.

(63)

It is easily checked that
\ell_{AA^{\prime}}
is a filtration function as
well as the other required properties.
∎

The first step towards triangulation is to define an appropriate cone
of a morphism.

Definition 6.1.10
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a filtered dg-category and

Tw(\mathcal{A})
be its pre-triangulated completion. Let

A=\left(\bigoplus\Sigma^{r_{j}}A_{j}[d_{j}],q=(q_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\right)
,

A^{\prime}=\left(\bigoplus\Sigma^{r^{\prime}_{i}}A^{\prime}_{i}[d^{\prime}_{i}],q=(q^{\prime}_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq m}\right)
be two objects of
Tw(\mathcal{A})
and let

f:A\to A^{\prime}
be a closed, degree preserving, morphism. Define the

\lambda
-filtered mapping cone
of
f
, where

\lambda\geq\ell_{AA^{\prime}}(f)
, by

{\rm Cone}^{\lambda}(f):=\left(\bigoplus_{i}\Sigma^{r^{\prime}_{i}}A^{\prime}_{i}[d^{\prime}_{i}]\oplus\bigoplus_{j}\Sigma^{r_{j}+\lambda}A_{j}[d_{j}-1],q_{\rm co}\right)\,\,\mbox{where}\,\,q_{\rm co}=\begin{pmatrix}q^{\prime}&f\\
0&q\end{pmatrix},

(64)

where
q^{\prime},q,f
are all block matrices.

Remark 6.1.11
.

(1) The condition

\lambda\geq\ell_{AA^{\prime}}(f)
guarantees that

{\rm Cone}^{\lambda}(f)
is indeed a filtered one-sided twisted
complex over
\Sigma\mathcal{A}
. Therefore,
Tw(\mathcal{A})
is closed under taking
degree-shifts, filtration-shifts, and filtered mapping cones of
(degree preserving) closed morphisms.

(2) Notice that a
\lambda
-filtered cone can also be written as a

0
-filtered cone but for a different map.

(3) Given a filtered dg-category
\mathcal{A}
it is easy to see that every
object in
Tw(\mathcal{A})
can be obtained from objects in
\Sigma\mathcal{A}
by
taking iterated filtered
0
-filtered mapping cones.

The
0
-cohomological category associated to a dg-category is a
triangulated category. The next result is the analogue in the filtered
case.

Proposition 6.1.12
.

If
\mathcal{A}
is a filtered dg-category
and
Tw(\mathcal{A})
is its filtered pre-triangulated completion, then the
degree-
0
cohomology category
{\rm H}^{0}(Tw(\mathcal{A}))
is a triangulated
persistence category.

In view of this result, it is natural to call a filtered dg-category

\mathcal{A}
pre-triangulated if the inclusion

\mathcal{A}\hookrightarrow Tw(\mathcal{A})
is an equivalence of filtered
dg-categories.

Corollary 6.1.13
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a filtered pre-triangulated
dg-category. Then its degree-0 cohomology category
{\rm H}^{0}(\mathcal{A})

is a triangulated persistence category.

Proof of Proposition
6.1.12
.

It is trivial to notice that the category
H^{0}(Tw(\mathcal{A}))
is a
persistence category. It is endowed with an obvious shift functor as
defined in §
6.1.2
. The first thing to check at
this point is that the
0
-level category
[H^{0}(Tw(\mathcal{A}))]_{0}
with
the same objects as
H^{0}(Tw(\mathcal{A}))
and only with the shift

0
-morphisms is triangulated - see Definition
4.1.1
. The
family of triangles that will provide the exact ones are the
triangles of the form

A\xrightarrow{f}B\xrightarrow{i}\mathrm{Cone}^{0}(f)\xrightarrow{\pi}A[-1]

associated to the

0
-cones, as given in Definition
6.1.10
. From this point on
checking that
[H^{0}(Tw(\mathcal{A}))]_{0}
is triangulated comes down to
the usual verifications showing that the
H^{0}
of a dg-category is
triangulated, with a bit of care to make sure that the relevant
homotopies preserve filtration. We leave this verification to the
reader. It is then automatic that
\Sigma^{r}
is triangulated when
restricted to
[H^{0}(Tw(\mathcal{A}))]_{0}
. The last step is to show that
the morphism
\eta_{r}^{A}:\Sigma^{r}A\to A
has an
r
-acyclic
cofiber in
Tw(\mathcal{A})
. In this context, of filtered dg-categories, an
object
K
is
r
-acyclic if the identity

\mathds{1}_{K}\in{\rm{Hom}}_{Tw(\mathcal{A})}(K,K)
is a boundary of some element

\eta\in{\rm{Hom}}_{Tw(\mathcal{A})}^{\leq r}(K,K)
.

The map
\eta^{A}_{r}\in{\rm Mor}^{0}_{Tw(\mathcal{A})}(\Sigma^{r}A,A)
is
induced by the identity. By definition

{\rm Cone}^{0}(\eta^{A}_{r})=A\oplus\Sigma^{r}A[-1]
and

q_{\rm co}=\begin{pmatrix}q&\eta^{A}_{r}\\
0&q^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}

where
q
is the structural map of the twisted complex
A
and

q^{\prime}=\Sigma^{r}q
.

Consider a homotopy

K=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
(\eta_{0,r})_{A}&0\end{pmatrix}:{\rm Cone}^{0}(\eta^{A}_{r})\to{\rm Cone}^{0}(\eta^{A}_{r})[-1].

Note that
\ell(K)=r
. We have

\displaystyle dK

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}q&\eta^{A}_{r}\\
0&q^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
(\eta_{0,r})_{A}&0\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
(\eta_{0,r})_{A}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}q&\eta^{A}_{r}\\
0&q^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\mathds{1}_{A}&0\\
q^{\prime}\circ(\eta_{0,r})_{A}-(\eta_{0,r})_{A}\circ q&\mathds{1}_{\Sigma^{r}A[-1]})\end{pmatrix}=\mathds{1}_{{\rm Cone}^{0}(\eta^{A}_{r})}.

because
\Sigma^{r}q\circ(\eta_{0,r})_{A}=(\eta_{0,r})_{A}\circ q
and this
concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 6.1.14
.

It is easy to see that in the filtered dg-category
Tw(\mathcal{A})
we can
replicate all the constructions in §
4
at the chain
level, similarly to the definition of
r
-acyclic objects mentioned
inside the proof above.

6.2.
Filtered cochain complexes.

In this section we discuss the main example of a filtered dg-category,
the category of filtered co-chain complexes. As we shall see, this is
pre-triangulated and thus, in view of Corollary
6.1.13
, its
homotopy category is a triangulated persistence category.

We will work over a field
\mathbf{k}
and will denote the resulting category
by
\mathcal{FK}_{\mathbf{k}}
. The objects of this category are filtered
cochain complexes
(X,\partial,\ell)
where
(X,\partial)
is a
cochain complex and
\ell
is a filtration function, as in
§
6.1.1
. Given two filtered cochain complexes

(X,\partial_{X},\ell_{X})
and
(Y,\partial_{Y},\ell_{Y})
the
morphisms
{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{FK}_{\mathbf{k}}}(X,Y)
are linear graded maps

f:X\to Y
such that the quantity

\ell(f)=\inf\{r\in\mathbb{R}\ |\ \ell_{Y}(f(x))\leq\ell_{X}(x)+r,\forall x\in X\}

(65)

is finite. The filtration function on
{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{FK}_{k}}(X,Y)

is then defined through (
65
). The differential on

{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{FK}_{k}}(X,Y)
is given, as usual, by

\partial(f)=\partial_{Y}\circ f-(-1)^{|f|}f\circ\partial_{X}

and it obviously preserves filtrations. The composition of morphisms
is also obviously compatible with the filtration and therefore

\mathcal{FK}_{\mathbf{k}}
is a filtered dg-category.

There is a natural shift functor on
\mathcal{FK}_{\mathbf{k}}
defined by

\Sigma:(\mathbb{R},+)\to\mathcal{P}{\rm End}(\mathcal{FK}_{k})
by

\Sigma^{r}(X,\partial,\ell_{X})=(X,\partial,\ell_{X}+r),\,\,\,\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,\,\,\,\Sigma^{r}(f)=f

for any

f\in{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{FK}_{k}}(X,Y)
. Moreover, for
r,s\in\mathbb{R}
,
there is a natural transformation from
\Sigma^{r}
to
\Sigma^{s}

induced by the identity.

Assume that

f:(X,\partial_{X},\ell_{X})\longrightarrow(Y,\partial_{Y},\ell_{Y})
is a cochain morphism such that

\ell(f)\leq 0
. In this case, the usual cone construction

\mathrm{Cone}(f)=(Y\oplus X[-1],\partial_{\mathrm{co}})
with

\partial_{\mathrm{co}}=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{Y}&f\\
0&\partial_{X}\end{pmatrix}

produces a filtered complex and fits
into a triangle of maps with
\ell\leq 0
:

X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{i}\mathrm{Cone}(f)\xrightarrow{\pi}X[-1]~.~

The standard properties of this construction immediately imply that
the dg-category
\mathcal{FK}_{\mathbf{k}}
is pre-triangulated and thus the

0
-cohomological category,
H^{0}\mathcal{FK}_{\mathbf{k}}
, is a
triangulated persistence category.

It is useful to make explicit some of the properties of this category:

i.

The objects of
H^{0}\mathcal{FK}_{\mathbf{k}}
are filtered cochain
complexes
(X,\partial_{X},\ell_{X})
.

ii.

The morphisms in
{\rm Mor}_{H^{0}\mathcal{FK}_{\mathbf{k}}}^{r}(X,Y)

are cochain maps

f:(X,\partial_{X},\ell_{X})\to(Y,\partial_{Y},\ell_{Y})
such that

\ell(f)\leq r
up to chain homotopy
h:f\simeq f^{\prime}
with

\ell(h)\leq r
.

iii.

A filtered complex
(K,\partial_{K},\ell_{K})
is

r
-acyclic if the identity
\mathds{1}_{K}
is chain homotopic to
0

through a chain homotopy
h:\mathds{1}_{K}\simeq 0
with
\ell(h)\leq r
.

iv.

The construction of weighted exact triangles as well as
their properties can be pursued in this context by following closely
the scheme in §
4.2
.

v.

The limit category
[H^{0}\mathcal{FK}_{\mathbf{k}}]_{\infty}
has
as morphisms chain homotopy classes of cochain maps (where
both the cochain maps and the homotopies are assumed to be of
bounded shifts). Its objects are still filtered cochain
complexes. It is triangulated, with translation functor
TX=X[-1]
,
as expected.

Remark 6.2.1
.

The example of the dg-category

\mathcal{FK}_{\mathbf{k}}
can be extended in a number of ways and we
mention a couple of them here.

(a) Assume that we fix a filtered dg-category
\mathcal{A}
. There
is a natural notion of filtered (left/right) module
\mathcal{M}

over
\mathcal{A}
. Such modules together with filtered maps
relating them form a new filtered dg-category denoted by

\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathcal{A}}
. The
0
-cohomology category associated
to this filtered dg-category,
H^{0}\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathcal{A}}
, is
pre-triangulated because the category
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathcal{A}}

is naturally endowed with a shift functor, just like

\mathcal{FK}_{\mathbf{k}}
, as well as with an appropriate
cone-construction over filtered, closed, degree preserving
morphisms.

(b) Similarly to (a), we may take
\mathcal{A}
to be a filtered

A_{\infty}
-category and consider the category of filtered modules,

\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathcal{A}}
, over
\mathcal{A}
. Again this is a
filtered dg-category and it is pre-triangulated (the formalism
required to establish this fact appears in
[
8
]
,
in a version dealing with weakly filtered structures).

As mentioned in the beginning of Introduction §
1
,
there exists a quantitative comparison between two filtered cochain
complexes
X,Y
, called the bottleneck distance and denoted by

d_{\rm bot}(X,Y)
. Here, we directly use the barcode language
from
[
2
]
or
[
44
]
to define
d_{\rm bot}(X,Y)
. Explicitly, by Proposition 7.4 in
[
44
]
, there is
a filtered isomorphism as follows,

X\simeq\bigoplus_{[a,+\infty)\in\mathcal{B}(X)}E_{1}(a)\oplus\bigoplus_{[c,d)\in\mathcal{B}(X)}E_{2}(c,d)

(66)

where
E_{1}(a),E_{2}(c,d)\in{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{FK}_{\mathbf{k}})
are
filtered cochain complexes defined by

E_{1}(a)=((\cdots\to 0\to\mathbf{k}\left<x\right>\to 0\to\cdots),\ell(x)=a).

and

E_{2}(c,d)=((\cdots\to 0\to\mathbf{k}\left<y\right>\xrightarrow{\partial}\mathbf{k}\left<x\right>\to 0\to\cdots),\;\ell(y)=c,\ell(x)=d),

where
c\geq d
,

\partial(y)=\kappa x
for some
0\neq\kappa\in\mathbf{k}
. The notation
\mathcal{B}(X)
in (
66
) stands
for a collection of finite or semi-infinite intervals or
\mathbb{R}
. In
what follows, sometimes for brevity, denote by
E_{*}(I)
either

E_{1}(a)
or
E_{2}(c,d)
for the corresponding interval

I=[a,+\infty)
or
I=[c,d)
in
\mathcal{B}(X)
. Importantly,

\mathcal{B}(X)
is well-defined, up to filtered isomorphism, by
Theorem A in
[
44
]
. Then
d_{\rm bot}(X,Y)
is defined as the
infimum
\tau
satisfying the following conditions: there exist some
subsets consisting of certain “short intervals”

\mathcal{B}(X)_{\rm short}\subset\mathcal{B}(X)
and

\mathcal{B}(Y)_{\rm short}\subset\mathcal{B}(Y)
such that

(i)

each short interval
[c,d)
satisfies
2(d-c)\leq\tau
;

(ii)

there is a bijection

\sigma:\mathcal{B}(X)\backslash\mathcal{B}(X)_{\rm short}\to\mathcal{B}(Y)\backslash\mathcal{B}(Y)_{\rm short}
;

(iii)

if
\sigma([a,b))=[c,d)
, then
\max\{|a-c|,|b-d|\}\leq\tau
;

(iv)

if
\sigma([a,\infty))=[c,\infty)
, then
|a-c|\leq\tau
.

In what follows, we assume that the cardinalities of barcodes

\#|\mathcal{B}(X)|
and
\#|\mathcal{B}(Y)|
are both finite. The
following result compares the fragmentation pseudo-metric
d^{\mathcal{F}}

defined in Definition
4.3.5
with the bottleneck distance

d_{\rm bot}
defined above.

Proposition 6.2.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}=H^{0}\mathcal{FK}_{\mathbf{k}}
and

\mathcal{F}\subset{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C})
be a subset containing
0
. Then

d^{\mathcal{F}}(X,Y)\leq C_{X,Y}d_{\rm bot}(X,Y),

where

C_{X,Y}=4\{\#|\mathcal{B}(X)|,\#|\mathcal{B}(Y)|\}+1
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the conclusion when
\mathcal{B}(X)

and
\mathcal{B}(Y)
have the same cardinality of the infinite-length
bars (otherwise by definition
d_{\rm bot}(X,Y)=+\infty
and the
conclusion holds trivially). Let
\tau:=d_{\rm bot}(X,Y)+\epsilon

for an arbitrarily small
\epsilon>0
. Since
d^{\mathcal{F}}(\cdot,\cdot)
is
invariant under filtered isomorphisms (applied to either of
its two inputs) then by (
66
) and by reordering summands
we obtain:

\displaystyle d^{\mathcal{F}}(X,Y)

\displaystyle\leq d^{\mathcal{F}}\left(\bigoplus_{I\in\mathcal{B}(X)\backslash\mathcal{B}(X)_{\rm short}}E_{*}(I),\bigoplus_{\sigma(I)\in\mathcal{B}(Y)\backslash\mathcal{B}(Y)_{\rm short}}E_{*}(\sigma(I))\right)

\displaystyle+d^{\mathcal{F}}\left(\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{B}(X)_{\rm short}}E_{2}(J),\bigoplus_{J^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(Y)_{\rm short}}E_{2}(J^{\prime})\right),

where the inequality is given by the triangle inequality of
d^{\mathcal{F}}

with respect to the direct sum, see Proposition

4.3.7
. For
d^{\mathcal{F}}
with short intervals, both

\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{B}(X)_{\rm short}}E_{2}(J)
and

\bigoplus_{J^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(Y)_{\rm short}}E_{2}(J^{\prime})
are acyclic
objects in
\mathcal{C}
, therefore by (i) in the definition
d_{\rm bot}

above, triangles

0\to 0\to\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{B}(X)_{\rm short}}E_{2}(J)\to 0\,\,\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,\,\,0\to 0\to\bigoplus_{J^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(Y)_{\rm short}}E_{2}(J^{\prime})\to 0

are
weight-
\frac{\tau}{2}
exact triangles (here we identify

\Sigma^{\lambda}0
with
0
for any shift
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
). Thus,

\displaystyle d^{\mathcal{F}}\left(\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{B}(X)_{\rm short}}E_{2}(J),\bigoplus_{J^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(X)_{\rm short}}E_{2}(J^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\leq d^{\mathcal{F}}\left(\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{B}(X)_{\rm short}}E_{2}(J),0\right)

\displaystyle+d^{\mathcal{F}}\left(0,\bigoplus_{J^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(X)_{\rm short}}E_{2}(J^{\prime})\right)\leq\tau.

On the other hand, by Proposition
4.3.7
again, for

d^{\mathcal{F}}
with non-short intervals, we have

d^{\mathcal{F}}\left(\bigoplus_{I\in\mathcal{B}(X)\backslash\mathcal{B}(X)_{\rm short}}E_{*}(I),\bigoplus_{\sigma(I)\in\mathcal{B}(Y)}E_{*}(\sigma(I)\right)\leq\sum_{I\in\mathcal{B}(X)\backslash\mathcal{B}(X)_{\rm short}}d^{\mathcal{F}}(E_{*}(I),E_{*}(\sigma(I)).

Since
d_{\rm bot}(X,Y)<+\infty
, the bijection

\sigma
will always map a finite interval to a finite interval, a
semi-infinite interval to a semi-infinite interval, so it suffice to
consider the following two cases.

Case I.
Estimate
d^{\mathcal{F}}(E_{1}(a),E_{1}(c))
. We
need to build a desired cone decomposition. Without loss of
generality, assume
a\geq c
. Then the identity map

\left<x\right>_{E_{1}(a)}\to\left<x\right>_{E_{1}(c)}
(with
negative
filtration shift) implies that the triangle

E_{1}(a)\to E_{1}(c)\to K\to E_{1}(a)
is weight-0 exact triangle (in
fact in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
) where
K
is the filtered mapping cone. Then in the
following cone decomposition (with linearization
(0,E_{1}(c))
),

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}0\to 0\to K\to 0\\
E_{1}(c)\to K\to E_{1}(a)\to E_{1}(c)\end{array}\right.

the first triangle is
a weight-
(c-a)
exact triangle since it is readily to verify that

K
is
(c-a)
-acyclic. Then

\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(E_{1}(a),E_{1}(c))\leq(c-a)+0\leq\tau
by (iv) in
the definition
d_{\rm bot}
above. On the other hand, consider the
following cone decomposition with linearization
(0,E_{1}(a),0)

(note that by definition
\Sigma^{-(a-c)}E_{1}(a)=E_{1}(c)
),

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}0\to 0\to 0\to 0\\
E_{1}(a)\to 0\to\Sigma^{-(a-c)}E_{1}(a)\to\Sigma^{-(a-c)}E_{1}(a)\\
0\to E_{1}(c)\to E_{1}(c)\to 0\end{array}\right.

(67)

where the second triangle has weight
a-c>0
by Remark

4.2.9
(b). Therefore,

\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(E_{1}(c),E_{1}(a))\leq 0+(a-c)+0\leq\tau
, which
implies that

d^{\mathcal{F}}(E_{1}(a),E_{1}(c))\leq\tau.

(68)

Case II.
Estimate
d^{\mathcal{F}}(E_{2}(a,b),E_{2}(c,d))
. We will carry on the estimation as follows,

d^{\mathcal{F}}(E_{2}(a,b),E_{2}(c,d))\leq d^{\mathcal{F}}(E_{2}(a,b),E_{2}(c,b))+d^{\mathcal{F}}(E_{2}(c,b),E_{2}(d,b)).

Moreover, we will only estimate

d^{\mathcal{F}}(E_{2}(a,b),E_{2}(c,b))
with
a\geq c
, and other situations
can be done in a similar and symmetric way. Similarly to Case I
above, consider the following cone decomposition

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}0\to 0\to K\to 0\\
E_{2}(c,b)\to K\to E_{2}(a,b)\to E_{2}(c,b)\end{array}\right.

where

E_{2}(a,b)\to E_{2}(c,b)
is the identity map

\left<x\right>_{E_{2}(a,b)}\to\left<x\right>_{E_{2}(c,b)}
(and
similarly to the generator
y
) with a negative filtration shift and

K
is the cone. Since
K
is
(a-c)
-acyclic, we have

\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(E_{2}(a,b),E_{2}(c,b))\leq 0+(a-c)\leq\tau
. On the
other hand,

\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(E_{2}(c,b),E_{2}(a,b))\leq\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(E_{2}(c,b),\Sigma^{a-c}E_{2}(c,b))+\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(E_{2}(a,b+a-c),E_{2}(a,b)),

where
\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(E_{2}(c,b),\Sigma^{a-c}E_{2}(c,b))\leq a-c
by a
similar cone decomposition as in (
67
). Meanwhile, since

b+a-c\geq b
, the identity map from
E_{2}(a,b+a-c)
to
E_{2}(a,b)

(with negative filtration shift) yields

\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(E_{2}(a,b+a-c),E_{2}(a,b))\leq a-c
. Therefore, together
we have, by (iii) in the definition
d_{\rm bot}
above,

d^{\mathcal{F}}(E_{2}(a,b),E_{2}(c,b))\leq 2(a-c)\leq 2\tau,

which
implies

d^{\mathcal{F}}(E_{2}(a,b),E_{2}(c,d))\leq 4\tau.

(69)

Therefore, by (
68
) and (
69
) together, we have

\displaystyle d^{\mathcal{F}}(X,Y)

\displaystyle\leq\#|\mathcal{B}(X)\backslash\mathcal{B}(X)_{\rm short}|\cdot 4\tau+\tau

\displaystyle\leq(4\#|\mathcal{B}(X)\backslash\mathcal{B}(X)_{\rm short}|+1)(d_{\rm bot}(X,Y)+\epsilon)

\displaystyle\leq(4\min\{\#|\mathcal{B}(X)|,\#|\mathcal{B}(Y)|\}+1)(d_{\rm bot}(X,Y)+\epsilon)

where the last inequality holds since
\sigma
is a bijection by
(ii) in the definition
d_{\rm bot}
above. Let
\epsilon\to 0
, and we
complete the proof.
∎

6.3.
Topological spaces
+

There are many topological categories, consisting of topological
spaces endowed with additional structures (indicated by the
+
in the
title of the subsection), that can be analyzed with the tools
discussed before. We will discuss here two elementary examples. They
both fit the following scheme: we will have a triple consisting of a
(small) category
\mathcal{K}
, an endofunctor

T_{\mathcal{K}}:\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{K}
and a class of triangles

\Delta_{\mathcal{K}}
, in
\mathcal{K}
of the form

A\to B\to C\to T_{\mathcal{K}}A~.~

In these cases the objects of
\mathcal{K}
have an underlying
structure as topological spaces and, similarly, the morphisms in

\mathcal{K}
are continuous maps, the functor
T_{\mathcal{K}}

corresponds to the suspension of spaces.

The aim is to define fragmentation pseudo-metrics on the objects of

\mathcal{K}
by first associating a weight with some reasonable
properties to the triangles in
\Delta_{\mathcal{K}}
,

w_{\mathcal{K}}:\Delta_{\mathcal{K}}\to\mathbb{R}
, and then defining
quantities
\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(X,Y)
and

\underline{\delta}^{\mathcal{F}}(X,Y)
as in, respectively,
(
4
) and (
7
), only taking into account
decompositions appealing to triangles

\Delta_{i}\in\Delta_{\mathcal{K}}
. Notice that

\delta^{\mathcal{F}}
is not generally defined in this setting as its
definition requires to desuspend spaces. On the other hand, as soon as

w_{\mathcal{K}}
is defined,
\underline{\delta}^{\mathcal{F}}
is
defined as well as the pseudo-metric
\underline{d}^{\mathcal{F}}

obtained by its symmetrization (see Remark
2.0.5
(c)).
Based on the various constructions discussed earlier in the paper,
there are two approaches to define a weight
\bar{w}_{\mathcal{K}}

(that is not flat) and they both require some more structure:

A.

The additional structure in this case is a functor

\Phi:\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
where
\mathcal{C}
is a TPC, in the
examples below
\mathcal{C}=H^{0}\mathcal{FK}_{\mathbf{k}}
- the triangulated
persistence homotopy category of filtered cochain
complexes. We also require that
\Phi
commutes with
T
(at
least up to some natural equivalence) and that for each

\Delta\in\Delta_{\mathcal{K}}
the image
\Phi(\Delta)
of

\Delta
, is exact in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
(and thus

\bar{w}(\Phi(\Delta))<\infty
where
\bar{w}
is the persistence
weight introduced in Definition
5.1.1
). In this case
for each
\Delta\in\Delta_{\mathcal{K}}
we put

\bar{w}_{\mathcal{K}}(\Delta)=\bar{w}(\Phi(\Delta))~.~

B.

This second approach requires first that the morphisms

{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{K}}(A,B)
are endowed with a natural increasing
filtration compatible with the composition. Secondly, there should
be a shift functor

\Sigma_{\mathcal{K}}:(\mathbb{R},+)\to{\rm End}(\mathcal{K})
compatible with
the filtration on morphisms and that commutes with

T_{\mathcal{K}}
. Moreover, the triangles in
\Delta_{\mathcal{K}}

have to be part of a richer structure such as a model category or a
Waldhausen category (that is compatible with the functor

\Sigma_{\mathcal{K}}
). In this case, the definition of weighted
triangles can be pursued following the steps in
§
4.2
, but at the space level, without moving
to an algebraic category. This approach goes beyond the scope of
this paper and will not be pursued here.

Remark 6.3.1
.

Of course, it is also possible to mix in some sense the two
approaches mentioned before. For instance, in the two examples below
the category
\mathcal{K}
carries a shift functor
\Sigma_{\mathcal{K}}
as
at
B
but also a functor
\Phi
as at
A
such that
\Phi
commutes
with the shift functors in the domain and target. In that case we
can use
\Phi
to pull back to
\mathcal{K}
more of the structure
and weights in
\mathcal{C}
(of course, this remains less precise than
constructing weights at the space level).

6.3.1.
Topological spaces with action functionals.

We will discuss here a category denoted by

\mathcal{AT}op_{\ast}
. The objects of this category are pairs

(A,f_{A})
where
A
is a pointed topological space and

f_{A}:A\to\mathbb{R}
is a continuous function bounded from below by the
value
f_{A}(\ast_{A})
of
f_{A}
at the base point
\ast_{A}
of

A
. We will refer to
f_{A}
as the action functional associated to

A
. The morphisms in this category are pointed continuous maps

u:A\to B
such that there exists
r\in\mathbb{R}
with the property that

f_{B}(u(x))\leq f_{A}(x)+r\ ,\ \forall x\in A
.

We will see that there is a natural contravariant
functor

\Phi:\mathcal{AT}op_{\ast}\to[H^{0}\mathcal{FK}_{\mathbf{k}}]_{\infty}

(70)

inducing a weight
\bar{w}_{\mathcal{AT}op_{\ast}}
and the associated
pseudo-metrics
\underline{d}^{\mathcal{F}}
on

\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{AT}op_{\ast})
along the lines of point A
above.

Remark 6.3.2
.

The condition on
f_{A}
being bounded from below is one possible
choice in this construction. Its role is to allow the constant map

u:(A,f_{A})\to(B,f_{B})
to be part of the morphisms of

\mathcal{AT}op_{\ast}
.

Before proceeding with the construction of the functor
\Phi
we
discuss some features of
\mathcal{AT}op_{\ast}
. Notice first that
the morphisms are filtered with the
r
-th stage being

{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{AT}op_{*}}^{\leq r}(A,B)=\{u:A\to B\ |\ u\ \mathrm{continuous},\ \ f_{B}(u(x))\leq f_{A}(x)+r\ ,\ \forall x\in A\}~.~

There is an obvious functor

\Sigma_{\mathcal{AT}op_{\ast}}:(\mathbb{R},+)\to\mathcal{AT}op_{\ast}

defined by
\Sigma^{s}(A,f_{A})=(A,f_{A}+s)
which is the identity on
morphisms. The next step is to define the translation functor

T_{\mathcal{AT}op_{\ast}}
. At the underlying topological level this
is just the topological suspension but we need to be more precise
about the action functional. Given an object
(A,f_{A})
we first
define the cone
(CA,f_{CA})
. We take
CA
to be the reduced cone,
in other words the quotient topological space

CA=A\times[0,1]/(A\times\{1\}\cup\ast_{A}\times[0,1])
. To
define
f_{CA}
we first consider the homotopy

h_{A}:A\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
,

h_{A}(x,t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}f_{A}(x)\ \ \ \mathrm{if}\ \ \ 0\leq t\leq\frac{1}{2}\\
(2-2t)(f_{A}(x)-f_{A}(\ast_{A}))+f_{A}(\ast_{A})\ \ \ \mathrm{if}\ \ \ \frac{1}{2}\leq t\leq 1\end{array}\right.~.~

The map
f_{CA}:CA\to\mathbb{R}
is induced by
h_{A}
. We now define the
reduced suspension,
SA=CA/A\times\{0\}
and take
f_{SA}
to be the
map induced to the quotient by the homotopy

h^{\prime}_{A}:A\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
,

h^{\prime}_{A}(x,t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}2t(f_{A}(x)-f_{A}(\ast_{A}))+f_{A}(\ast_{A})\ \ \ \mathrm{if}\ \ \ 0\leq t\leq\frac{1}{2}\\
(2-2t)(f_{A}(x)-f_{A}(\ast_{A}))+f_{A}(\ast_{A})\ \ \ \mathrm{if}\ \ \ \frac{1}{2}\leq t\leq 1\end{array}\right.~.~

We put
T(A,f_{A})=(SA,f_{SA})
. It is immediate to see that
T

extends to a functor on
\mathcal{AT}op_{\ast}
and that it commutes
with
\Sigma
. Moreover, both
\Sigma
and
T
so defined commute and
are compatible with the filtration of the morphisms in the sense that
they take
{\rm Mor}^{\leq r}
to
{\rm Mor}^{\leq r}
for each
r
. Moreover,
composition of morphisms is also compatible with the filtrations in
the sense that it takes

{\rm Mor}^{\leq r_{1}}(B,C)\times{\rm Mor}^{\leq r_{2}}(A,B)
to

{\rm Mor}^{\leq r_{1}+r_{2}}(A,C)
.

We now define the class of exact triangles

\Delta_{\mathcal{AT}op_{\ast}}
. For this we consider a morphism

u:(A,f_{A})\to(B,f_{B})
and we first define its cone

\mathrm{Cone}(u)
. As a topological space this is, as expected, the
quotient topological space
(B\cup CA)/\sim
where the equivalence
relation
\sim
is generated by
f(x)\sim x\times\{0\}
. The base
point of
\mathrm{Cone}(u)
is the same as that of
B
. The action
functional
f_{\mathrm{Cone}(u)}
is induced to the respective
quotient by :

G(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}f_{B}(x)\ \ \ \mathrm{if}\ \ \ x\in B\\
(1-2t)(f_{B}(u(y))-f_{B}(\ast_{B}))+2t(f_{A}(y)-f_{A}(\ast_{A}))+f_{B}(\ast_{B})\ \mathrm{if}\ \ x=(y,t)\in A\times[0,\frac{1}{2}]\\
(2-2t)(f_{A}(y)-f_{A}(\ast_{A}))+f_{B}(\ast_{B})\ \mathrm{if}\ \ x=(y,t)\in A\times[\frac{1}{2},1]\end{array}\right..

There is an obvious inclusion
i:(B,f_{B})\to\mathrm{Cone}(u)
as
well as a projection
p:\mathrm{Cone}(u)\to TA
(that contracts
B

to a point). This map belongs to our class of morphisms because the
functional
u
is bounded from below. The class

\Delta_{\mathcal{AT}op_{\ast}}
consists of triangles
\Delta
:

\Delta:A\xrightarrow{u}B\xrightarrow{i}\mathrm{Cone}(u)\xrightarrow{p}TA~.~

(71)

We finally construct the functor

\Phi:\mathcal{AT}op_{\ast}\to[H^{0}\mathcal{FK}_{\mathbf{k}}]_{\infty}
.
This functor will be contravariant, since the objects of

\mathcal{FK}_{\mathbf{k}}
are cochain complexes (rather than chain
complexes).

First we fix some notation: for a pointed topological space
X
we
denote by
\tilde{C}_{\ast}(X)
the reduced singular chain complex of

X
with coefficients in
\mathbf{k}
and by
\tilde{C}^{\ast}(X)
the reduced
singular cochain complex (we denote without
\tilde{(-)}
the
non-reduced chain/cochain complexes) and if
Y\subset X
is a pointed
subspace, then
\tilde{C}_{\ast}(X,Y)
and
\tilde{C}^{\ast}(X,Y)
are
the relative (co)chains. Consider an object of
\mathcal{AT}op_{*}
,

(A,f_{A})
, and let
A^{\leq r}=(f_{A})^{-1}(-\infty,r]
. Notice
that the spaces
A^{\leq r}
are pointed (if non-void). There is a
filtration of
C^{\ast}(A)
defined by:

\tilde{C}^{\ast}(A)^{\leq r}=\mathrm{im}[\tilde{C}^{\ast}(A,A^{\leq r})\to\tilde{C}^{\ast}(A)]

(these are the cochains in
A
that vanish over the singular chains of

A^{\leq r}\subset A
). It is clear that the cochain differential
preserves this filtration. Finally, we define the functor
\Phi
.
For each object
(A,f_{A})
of
\mathcal{AT}op_{\ast}
we take

\Phi(A,f_{A})
to consist of the cochain complex

\tilde{C}^{\ast}(A)
together with the filtration

\{\tilde{C}^{\ast}(A)^{\leq r}\}
defined above. For a morphism

u:(A,f_{A})\to(B,f_{B})
we take
\Phi(u)=[\tilde{C}^{\ast}(u)]

where
[-]
represents the cochain-homotopy class of the respective
cochain morphism.

The definition of the morphisms in
\mathcal{AT}op_{\ast}
implies
that
\Phi(u)
is indeed a morphism in

[H^{0}\mathcal{FK}_{\mathbf{k}}]_{\infty}
. Moreover, because we are using
everywhere reduced cochain complexes (and we work in the pointed
category), we have that
\Phi(\Delta)
is exact in

[H^{0}\mathcal{FK}_{\mathbf{k}}]_{\infty}
for each of the triangles in

\Delta_{\mathcal{AT}op_{\ast}}
. Further, the functor
\Phi
also
interchanges the shift functors in the domain and the target.

In all cases, the weight
\bar{w}_{\mathcal{AT}op_{\ast}}
is well
defined as well as the associated fragmentation pseudo-metrics

\underline{d}^{\mathcal{F}}(-,-)
on the objects of

\mathcal{AT}op_{\ast}
. Roughly speaking, these fragmentation pseudo-metrics measure how much “weight” we need to obtain a given topological space via successive cone attachments of spaces in
\mathcal{F}
.

Remark 6.3.3
.

(a) The choice of the class

\Delta_{\mathcal{AT}op_{\ast}}
given above is quite restrictive
with the consequence that the resulting pseudo-metrics are often
infinite. One alternative is to enlarge this class to all triangles
in
\mathcal{AT}op_{\ast}
that are homotopy equivalent to those in
the initial class through maps (and homotopies) of filtration
0
.

(b) From some points of view, working in the
pointed
category
of spaces endowed with an action functional is not natural. Other
choices are possible, in particular some such that the translation
functor
T
more closely imitates dynamical stabilization.

(c) The restriction of
\Phi
to compact topological spaces admits
an obvious lift to
H^{0}\mathcal{FK}_{\mathbf{k}}
. However, without such a
restriction, such a lift does not seem to be available in full
generality.

6.3.2.
Metric spaces

The category

\mathcal{M}etr_{0}
that we will consider here has as objects
path-connected metric spaces
(X,d_{X})
of finite diameter. The
morphisms are Lipschitz maps. These are maps
\phi:X\to Y
such that
there exists a constant
c\in[0,\infty)
, called the Lipschitz
constant of
u
, with the property that

d_{Y}(\phi(x),\phi(y))\leq c~d_{X}(x,y)
for all
x,y\in X
.

Remark 6.3.4
.

The finite diameter condition
imposed here - indicated by the subscript
0
-
is necessary for some of the constructions below. The
connectivity assumption is more a matter of convenience.

We will construct a functor as in (
70
) with one
main modification. For convenience, we prefer defining a covariant
functor and thus our target category will not be a category of cochain
complexes but rather one of filtered chain complexes (the
passage from one to the other is formal, replacing
C^{*}
by

C_{-*}
and vice-versa). We will denote the category of filtered
chain complexes over
\mathbf{k}
by
\mathcal{FK}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{k}}
. This behaves just
as a usual dg-category except that the differential on the space of
morphisms is of degree
-1
. With this change, we will construct:

\Phi^{\prime}:\mathcal{M}etr_{0}\to[H_{0}\mathcal{FK}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{k}}]_{\infty}~.~

(72)

as well as related structures on
\mathcal{M}etr_{0}
, as at point A at
the beginning of the section (see also Remark
6.3.1
).

We start by noting that there is an obvious increasing filtration of
the morphisms in
\mathcal{M}etr_{0}

with

{\rm Mor}_{\mathcal{M}etr_{0}}^{\leq r}(X,Y)=\{u:X\to Y\ |\ \mathrm{the\ Lipschitz\ constant\ of}\ u\ \mathrm{is}\ \leq e^{r}\}~.~

It is immediate to see that this filtration is compatible
with composition. There is also a functor

\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}etr_{0}}:(\mathbb{R},+)\to\mathcal{M}etr_{0}
defined by
rescaling the metric,
\Sigma^{s}(A,d_{A})=(A,e^{s}d_{A})
, and
which is the identity on morphisms. As in the example in the previous
section, we next will define the translation functor

T_{\mathcal{M}etr_{0}}
and the class of triangles

\Delta_{\mathcal{M}etr_{0}}
. The first step is to construct the
metric cone
C^{\prime}A
for an object
(A,d_{A})
in our
class. Topologically, the cone
C^{\prime}A
will be this time the unreduced cone over
A
. Thus it is defined by

C^{\prime}A=A\times[0,1]/A\times\{0\}
. To define the metrics
d_{C^{\prime}A}
,
first let
D_{A}
be the diameter of
A
. We then put

d_{C^{\prime}A}((x,t),(y,t^{\prime}))=\frac{D_{A}}{2}|t-t^{\prime}|+\min\{t,t^{\prime}\}~d_{A}(x,y).

(73)

It is immediate to see that this does indeed define a metric on

C^{\prime}A
. A similar construction is available to construct
T(A,d_{A})
. Topologically, we will define first the - non-reduced -
suspension,
S^{\prime}A
, as the topological quotient of

A\times[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
with
A\times\{-1/2\}

identified to a point
S
and
A\times\{+1/2\}
identified to a
different point
N
. We now define
d_{S^{\prime}A}
by

d_{S^{\prime}A}((x,t),(y,t^{\prime}))=\frac{D_{A}}{2}|t-t^{\prime}|+\min\{\frac{1}{2}-|t|,\frac{1}{2}-|t^{\prime}|\}~d_{A}(x,y)

(74)

and again it is immediate to see that this defines a metric on

S^{\prime}A
. We now put
T(A,d_{A})=(S^{\prime}A,d_{S^{\prime}A})
. The next step is to
define the triangles in
\Delta_{\mathcal{M}etr_{0}}
. For this we
assume
u:(A,d_{A})\to(B,d_{B})
is a morphism in our category and
we want to define the (non-reduced) cone of
u
,

\mathrm{Cone}^{\prime}(u)
. Topologically, this is, as usual,

B\cup C^{\prime}A/[\{x\}\times\{0\}\sim u(x)\ |\ x\in A]
. To define a
metric on
\mathrm{Cone}^{\prime}(u)
we notice first that given a
map
g:X\to Y
and a pseudo-metric
d_{Y}
on
Y
, there is a
pull-back pseudo-metric on
X
given by

g^{\ast}d_{Y}(a,b)=d_{Y}(g(a),g(b))
. We now let

A^{\prime}=u(A)\subset B
and we denote by
\bar{u}:A^{\prime}\hookrightarrow B
the
inclusion. Notice that
\mathrm{Cone}^{\prime}(\bar{u})\subset C^{\prime}B
. Thus

\mathrm{Cone}^{\prime}(\bar{u})
is endowed with a metric given by the
restriction of the metric
d_{C^{\prime}B}
on
C^{\prime}B
. There are obvious
projections
\pi:\mathrm{Cone}^{\prime}(u)\to\mathrm{Cone}^{\prime}(\bar{u})
and

p:\mathrm{Cone}^{\prime}(u)\to S^{\prime}A
. Here
p
collapses
B
to the point
S

in the suspension and sends
(x,t)\to(x,t-\frac{1}{2})
for the
points
(x,t)\in C^{\prime}A
. We now define

d_{\mathrm{Cone}^{\prime}(u)}=\max\{\pi^{\ast}d_{C^{\prime}B}+p^{\ast}d_{S^{\prime}A}\}~.~

Notice that, if
u
is not injective and
B
is
not a single point, then the two pseudo-metrics in the right term of
the equality are each degenerate. Nonetheless,
d_{\mathrm{Cone}^{\prime}(u)}

is non-degenerate. Finally, the class of triangles

\Delta_{\mathcal{M}etr_{0}}
consists of triangles:

A\xrightarrow{u}B\xrightarrow{i}\mathrm{Cone}^{\prime}(u)\xrightarrow{p}S^{\prime}A

where
i
is the inclusion and
p
is the projection above.

With this preparation, we can now define the functor
\Phi^{\prime}
from
(
72
). Consider an object
(A,d_{A})
in our
category and the associated singular complex
C_{\ast}(A)
. This
chain complex is filtered as follows:

C^{\leq r}_{k}(A)=\left\{\sum_{i}a_{i}\sigma_{i}\ \bigg|\ a_{i}\in\mathbf{k}\ ,\ \sigma_{i}\ \mathrm{a\ singular\ simplex\ of\ diameter\ at\ most}\ e^{r}\right\}~.~

In other words, in
the expression above,
\sigma_{i}:\Delta^{k}\to A
is a continuous
map with the standard
k
-simplex as domain and such that

d_{A}(\sigma_{i}(x),\sigma_{i}(y))\leq e^{r}
for any

x,y\in\Delta^{k}
. Consider the constant map
c:A\to\ast
this
induces an obvious surjection
C_{\ast}\to C_{\ast}(\ast)
and we
denote by
\bar{C}_{\ast}(A)
the kernel of this map (this is
quasi-isomorphic to the reduced singular chain complex of
A
-
because
A
is connected - but is independent of the choice of
base-point). There is an induced filtration

\bar{C}^{\leq r}_{\ast}(A)
. We now put

\Phi^{\prime}(A,d_{A})=\bar{C}_{\ast}(A)\ \mathrm{with\ the\ filtration}\ \{\bar{C}^{\leq r}_{\ast}(A)\}_{r}~.~

Further, for a morphism

u:(A,d_{A})\to(B,d_{B})
we take
\Phi^{\prime}(u)=[C_{\ast}(u)]
, the
chain homotopy class of the singular chain map
C_{\ast}(u)

(restricted to the
\bar{C}(-)
complexes).

It is easy to see that this
\Phi^{\prime}
is indeed a functor as desired and
that
\Phi^{\prime}(\Delta)
is exact for each triangle
\Delta
as defined
above and, again,
\Phi^{\prime}
interchanges the shift functors in the
domain and target. In summary, the weight

\overline{w}_{\mathcal{M}etr_{0}}
is well defined as well as the
quantities
\underline{\delta}^{\mathcal{F}}
and the pseudo-metrics
associated to them.

Remark 6.3.5
.

(a) Similarly to Remark
6.3.3
, the definition of the
triangles in
\Delta_{\mathcal{M}etr_{0}}
is highly restrictive
and, in this case, even the objects in our category are subject to a
constraint - finiteness of the diameter - that might be a hindrance
in applications. One way to apply the methods above to study spaces
of infinite diameter is to consider triangles of the form

\Delta:A\to B\to C\to S^{\prime}A
where
A
is of finite diameter such
that
S^{\prime}A
admits a metric as above and analyze when
\Phi^{\prime}(\Delta)

is of finite persistence weight in

[H_{0}\mathcal{FK}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{k}}]_{\infty}
.

(b) In studying metric spaces of infinite diameter by these methods,
it is likely that the most appropriate structure that fits with the
cone construction is that of
length structure
, in the sense of
Gromov, as in Chapter 1, Section A in
[
27
]
. We will not
further pursue this theme here.

6.3.3.
Further remarks on topological examples.

A. In the topological examples above - for instance in

\mathcal{AT}op_{\ast}
- it is natural to see what the quantities

\underline{\delta}^{\mathcal{F}}(-)
mean even for the flat weight

w_{0}
, which associates to each exact triangle the value
1
. Of
course, in this case
\underline{\delta}^{\mathcal{F}}(A,B)
simply
counts the minimal number of cone-attachments in the category

\mathcal{AT}op_{\ast}
that are needed to obtain
A
out of the space

B
by attaching cones over spaces in the family
\mathcal{F}
using
the family of triangles
\Delta_{\mathcal{AT}op_{\ast}}
. Given that
the weight is flat, the question is independent of filtrations and
shift functors and it reduces to the identical question in the
category of pointed spaces
\mathcal{T}op_{\ast}
. In the examples
below we will focus on this category and on

\underline{\delta}^{\mathcal{F}}(A,\ast)
which is one of the most
basic quantities involved.

It is useful to keep in mind that there are two more choices that are
essential in defining
\underline{\delta}^{\mathcal{F}}(A,\ast)
: the
choice of family
\mathcal{F}
and the choice of the class of exact
triangles
\Delta_{\mathcal{T}op_{\ast}}
- see also Remark

6.3.3
(a).

(i)

\mathcal{F}=\{S^{0},S^{1},\ldots,S^{k},\ldots\}
;

\Delta_{\mathcal{T}op_{\ast}}
are the triangles

A\xrightarrow{u}B\to\mathrm{Cone}(u)\to SA
as in
(
71
) (but omitting the action functionals). In this
case,
\underline{\delta}^{\mathcal{F}}(A,\ast)=k<\infty
means
that
A
has the structure of a finite
CW
- complex with
k
cells.

(ii)

\mathcal{F}=\{S^{0},S^{1},\ldots,S^{k},\ldots\}
; we
now take
\Delta_{\mathcal{T}op_{\ast}}
to be the triangles
homotopy equivalent to the triangles

A\xrightarrow{u}B\to\mathrm{Cone}(u)\to SA
from
(
71
). In this case,

\underline{\delta}^{\mathcal{F}}(A,\ast)=k
means that
A
is
homotopy equivalent to a
CW
-complex with
k
cells. This number
is obviously a homotopy invariant. It is clearly bounded from below
by the sum of the Betti numbers of
A
.

(iii)

\mathcal{F}
are pointed spaces with the homotopy type
of
CW
-complexes;
\Delta_{\mathcal{T}op_{\ast}}
are as at
(ii). In this case, the definition

\underline{\delta}^{\mathcal{F}}(A,\ast)
coincides with that of
the cone-length,
\mathrm{Cl}(A)
, of
A
(for a space
A
with the
homotopy type of a
CW
-complex). Cone-length is a homotopical
invariant which is of interest because it is bigger, but not by more
than one, than the Lusternik-Schnirelmann category
[
16
]
which, in turn,
provides a lower bound for the minimal number of critical points of
smooth functions on manifolds. Incidentally, as noted by Smale

[
39
]
, a version of the Lusternik-Schnirelmann category provides
also a measure for the complexity of algorithms, see

[
14
]
for more on this subject.

(iv)

At this point we will change the underlying category and
place ourselves in the pointed category of finite type,
simply-connected rational spaces
\mathcal{T}op_{1}^{\mathbb{Q}}

(see
[
23
]
). We take
\mathcal{F}
to consist of finite
wedges of rational spheres of dimension at least
2
. The triangles

\Delta_{\mathcal{T}op^{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ast}}
are as at (ii) (in the category
of rational spaces) but we will also allow in

\Delta_{\mathcal{T}op^{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ast}}
“formal”, triangles of the
form
S^{-1}F\to\ast\to F
where
F\in\mathcal{F}
(de-suspending
is not possible in our category but we still want to have for a
rational
2
-sphere,
S^{2}_{\mathbb{Q}}
,

\underline{\delta}^{\mathcal{F}}(S^{2}_{\mathbb{Q}},\ast)=1
). In this
setting, it turns out
[
17
]
that

\underline{\delta}^{\mathcal{F}}(A,\ast)=\mathrm{Cl}(A)=\mathrm{nil}(A)~.~

Both equalities here are non-trivial, the first because in the
definition of
\mathrm{Cl}
we are using cones over arbitrary
(rational) spaces while in this example
\mathcal{F}
consists of
only wedges of spheres. For the second equality,
\mathrm{nil}(A)

is the minimal order of nilpotence of the augmentation ideal

\overline{\mathcal{A}}
of a rational differential graded
commutative algebra
\mathcal{A}
representing
A
(recall that by a
celebrated result of Sullivan
[
40
]
, the homotopy category of
rational simply connected spaces is equivalent to the homotopy
category of rational differential graded commutative algebras, the
representative of a given space being given by the so-called
PL
-de
Rham complex of
A
).

B. One of the difficulties of extracting a triangulated persistence
category from a topological category such as those considered in this
section is very basic and has to do with the difference between stable
and unstable homotopy. In essence, recall that if
\mathcal{C}
is a
TPC, then the
0
-level category
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is required to be
triangulated. However, in unstable settings, homotopy categories of
spaces are not triangulated.

(i)
